```python
        raise                 # send the same error on

divide(1, 0)
# could not divide
# ZeroDivisionError: division by zero
```

And remember that a `return` inside `try` ends the whole function at once, even from inside a loop. A decorator that [[retries|retry-real]] uses all three: a `for` loop over the tries, `return func(...)` inside `try`, and a bare `raise` only when the last try has failed.

**Watch out:** the real mistake with decorators that take arguments is leaving the brackets off: `@repeat` instead of `@repeat(3)`. Python then calls `repeat(roll)`, so the function itself arrives as `times`, and `roll` becomes `decorator`. Nothing fails on that line. The error comes later, far from the cause, when you call `roll()`. If a decorator takes a setting, it always needs brackets.

::: context first-class Functions as ordinary values
Programmers say Python has first-class functions: a function can go anywhere a value can go. You can store it in a variable, put it in a list or dict, pass it in, and hand it back. You have already done this without the name: `sorted(words, key=len)` in py2-05 passed the function `len` to `sorted`, without brackets, so that `sorted` could call it on each word.
:::

::: context closure A backpack of variables
Think of the inner function packing a small backpack when it is made. Into the backpack go the outer variables it uses. When the outer function returns, its other variables are thrown away, but the backpack stays with the inner function.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 140" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="10" y="20" width="150" height="100" rx="6" fill="white" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-dasharray="5 4" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="85" y="40" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">make_adder(5)</text>
  <text x="85" y="62" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">has returned</text>
  <text x="85" y="100" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">n = 5</text>
  <line x1="162" y1="96" x2="196" y2="96" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <polygon points="196,91 204,96 196,101" fill="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="206" y="20" width="144" height="100" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="278" y="44" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">add5 (a closure)</text>
  <text x="278" y="66" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">code: return x + n</text>
  <rect x="226" y="80" width="104" height="30" rx="5" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="278" y="100" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">backpack: n = 5</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context wraps-why Names in tracebacks
When something goes wrong, the traceback (py2-09) lists each function by its name. If ten different functions are all decorated and all called `wrapper`, the traceback and any logs full of "wrapper" tell you nothing. Test tools, documentation tools and the `help()` function also read `__name__` and the docstring. `functools.wraps` costs one line and keeps all of them working.
:::

::: context function-attribute Functions are objects too
Everything in Python is an object, and most objects let you attach attributes with a dot. You met this with `__defaults__` in py2-07, a value Python stores on every function. Your own attributes work the same way. `wrapper.last` is an ordinary attribute on the function object, and every call to the wrapper sees the same one, because there is only one wrapper function.
:::

::: context three-layers Three layers, three moments
Each layer runs at a different moment. `repeat(3)` runs once, when Python reaches the `@repeat(3)` line. `decorator(roll)` runs straight after, once, while the `def` is being finished. `wrapper()` runs every time anyone calls `roll()`. So: the setting goes in first, then the function, then the call's arguments, one layer for each. `functools.wraps(func)` is itself a decorator with an argument, which is why it has brackets.
:::

::: context exception-family What Exception does not catch
`Exception` sits near the top of Python's family tree of errors, with almost every error you will meet below it. A few special exceptions sit outside it on purpose. `KeyboardInterrupt`, raised when you press Ctrl+C to stop a program, is one. That way `except Exception:` does not stop you from stopping a program that is stuck retrying forever. `StopIteration` from py3-01, on the other hand, is inside the `Exception` family.
:::

::: context retry-real Retries in real systems
Many failures do not last: a network message is lost, a server is busy for a moment. So real software often tries again a few times before giving up, exactly like your `retry` decorator. Many systems also wait a little longer after each failed try, called exponential backoff, so they do not flood a busy server. The limit matters as much as the retry: giving up after a set number of tries and reporting the last error is what stops a program from hanging forever.
:::
--- task
Write two decorators. Both must use `@functools.wraps(func)` on their wrapper, and both must pass every argument through with `*args, **kwargs`.

- `count_calls(func)`: the function it returns has an attribute `calls`. It starts at `0` and goes up by one on every call. The wrapper returns whatever `func` returns.
- `retry(times)`: a decorator with an argument, built in three layers like `repeat`. The decorated function is tried up to `times` times in total. If a try raises an exception, it tries again. As soon as a try succeeds, its result is returned, and there are no more tries. If every try fails, the last exception is raised (with a bare `raise`).

For example, a function decorated with `@count_calls` and then called three times has `.calls` equal to `3`.
--- starter
import functools


def count_calls(func):
    return func


def retry(times):
    def decorator(func):
        return func
    return decorator
--- solution
import functools


def count_calls(func):
    @functools.wraps(func)
    def wrapper(*args, **kwargs):
        wrapper.calls += 1
        return func(*args, **kwargs)

    wrapper.calls = 0
    return wrapper


def retry(times):
    def decorator(func):
        @functools.wraps(func)
        def wrapper(*args, **kwargs):
            for attempt in range(1, times + 1):
                try:
                    return func(*args, **kwargs)
                except Exception:
                    if attempt == times:
                        raise
        return wrapper
    return decorator
--- hint
`count_calls` has the same shape as `remember_last`. Set `wrapper.calls = 0` after defining `wrapper` and before `return wrapper`. Inside the wrapper, add one (`wrapper.calls += 1`) and then `return func(*args, **kwargs)`.
--- hint
`retry` has three levels, like `repeat`: `retry(times)` returns `decorator`, `decorator(func)` returns `wrapper`, and `wrapper(*args, **kwargs)` does the tries. Put `@functools.wraps(func)` above `def wrapper`.
--- hint
Inside the wrapper, loop over the tries with `for attempt in range(1, times + 1):`. In the loop, `try:` `return func(*args, **kwargs)`. In `except Exception:`, re-raise with a bare `raise` only when `attempt == times` (the last try); otherwise do nothing, and the loop goes round for another try.
--- check test | count_calls counts every call
(lambda f: (f(1), f(2), f(3), f.calls)[3] == 3)(count_calls(lambda x: x))
--- check test | A new decorated function starts at 0
count_calls(lambda: 1).calls == 0
--- check case | count_calls passes arguments through and returns the result
count_calls(lambda a, b=1: a + b)(1, b=5)
=> 6
--- check test | Both decorators keep the original's name (functools.wraps)
count_calls(len).__name__ == "len" and retry(2)(len).__name__ == "len"
?? Put @functools.wraps(func) on the wrapper.
--- check case | retry succeeds on the third try
retry(3)((lambda seq: lambda: next(seq)())(iter([lambda: 1 / 0, lambda: 1 / 0, lambda: "ok"])))()
=> "ok"
--- check test | retry gives up after `times` tries and raises the last error
raises(ZeroDivisionError, retry(2)((lambda seq: lambda: next(seq)())(iter([lambda: 1 / 0, lambda: 1 / 0, lambda: "ok"]))))
--- check test | retry stops as soon as a try succeeds
(lambda log: (retry(5)(lambda: log.append(1) or len(log))(), log)[1] == [1])([])
--- check case | retry passes arguments through
retry(1)(lambda a, b: a * b)(6, b=7)
=> 42

+++ practice | Round every result
--- task
Write a decorator `round2(func)`. The function it returns calls `func` with exactly the arguments it was given, and returns the result rounded to 2 decimal places with `round(result, 2)`.

Pass every argument through with `*args, **kwargs`, and put `@functools.wraps(func)` on the wrapper so the decorated function keeps its name.

For example, after `@round2` above `def ratio(a, b): return a / b`, the call `ratio(1, 3)` gives `0.33`.
--- starter
import functools


def round2(func):
    return func
--- solution
import functools


def round2(func):
    @functools.wraps(func)
    def wrapper(*args, **kwargs):
        return round(func(*args, **kwargs), 2)

    return wrapper
--- hint
Follow the full decorator shape from the lesson: `round2` defines a `wrapper(*args, **kwargs)`, decorated with `@functools.wraps(func)`, and returns it.
--- hint
The wrapper's one line calls the original with `func(*args, **kwargs)` and returns that, passed through `round(..., 2)`.
--- check case | Rounds the result
round2(lambda a, b: a / b)(1, 3)
=> 0.33

=> 1.75

(lambda g: (exec("@round2\ndef third(x):\n    return x / 3\n", g), g["third"](10))[1] == 3.33)(dict(globals()))

+++ practice | Refuse negative arguments
--- task
Write a decorator `require_positive(func)` that checks the arguments **before** the call. If any positional or keyword argument is a number (an `int` or a `float`) below 0, the wrapper raises `ValueError` and does not call `func` at all. Otherwise it returns what `func` returns.

Arguments that are not numbers, like strings, are never checked. Use `@functools.wraps(func)` and pass everything through with `*args, **kwargs`.

For example, `require_positive(lambda a, b: a * b)(3, 4)` is `12`, but `require_positive(lambda a, b: a * b)(3, -4)` raises `ValueError`.
--- starter
import functools


def require_positive(func):
    @functools.wraps(func)
    def wrapper(*args, **kwargs):
        return func(*args, **kwargs)

    return wrapper
--- solution
import functools


def require_positive(func):
    @functools.wraps(func)
    def wrapper(*args, **kwargs):
        for value in list(args) + list(kwargs.values()):
            if isinstance(value, (int, float)) and value < 0:
                raise ValueError(f"negative argument: {value}")
        return func(*args, **kwargs)

    return wrapper
--- hint
`args` is a tuple of the positional arguments and `kwargs` is a dict of the keyword ones. Loop over both before you call `func`: over `args`, and over `kwargs.values()`.
--- hint
For each value, `isinstance(value, (int, float)) and value < 0` says "a negative number". Raise `ValueError` as soon as you find one. Only after the loop, `return func(*args, **kwargs)`.
--- check case | Good arguments pass through
require_positive(lambda a, b: a * b)(3, 4)
=> 12
--- check test | A negative positional argument raises ValueError
raises(ValueError, lambda: require_positive(lambda a, b: a * b)(3, -4))
--- check test | A negative keyword argument raises ValueError too
raises(ValueError, lambda: require_positive(lambda a, b=1: a * b)(3, b=-0.5))
--- check test | The function is not called when an argument is refused
(lambda calls: raises(ValueError, lambda: require_positive(lambda x: calls.append(x))(-1)) and calls == [])([])
--- check case | Zero is allowed, and strings are not checked
require_positive(lambda n, label: f"{label}{n}")(0, "-")
=> "-0"
--- check test | Keeps the original's name
require_positive(abs).__name__ == "abs"

+++ practice | A decorator that keeps a log
--- task
Write `logged(log)`, a decorator **with an argument**, built in three layers like `repeat`. `log` is a list. Every call to a decorated function appends one line to `log`, then returns the function's result:

- the function's name (`func.__name__`), then in round brackets the arguments: the `repr` of each positional argument, then each keyword argument as `name=` and its `repr`, all joined with `", "`;
- then `" -> "` and the `repr` of the result.

For example, a function `add(a, b)` decorated with `@logged(calls)` and called as `add(2, b=3)` appends `"add(2, b=3) -> 5"` to `calls`. Use `@functools.wraps(func)` on the wrapper.
--- starter
import functools


def logged(log):
    def decorator(func):
        @functools.wraps(func)
        def wrapper(*args, **kwargs):
            log.append(func.__name__)
            return func(*args, **kwargs)

        return wrapper

    return decorator
--- solution
import functools


def logged(log):
    def decorator(func):
        @functools.wraps(func)
        def wrapper(*args, **kwargs):
            result = func(*args, **kwargs)
            parts = [repr(a) for a in args] + [f"{k}={repr(v)}" for k, v in kwargs.items()]
            log.append(f"{func.__name__}({', '.join(parts)}) -> {repr(result)}")
            return result

        return wrapper

    return decorator
--- hint
Call the function first and keep its result, since the line needs it. Then build a list of the argument texts: `repr(a)` for each item of `args`, then `f"{k}={repr(v)}"` for each `k, v` in `kwargs.items()`.
--- hint
Join the parts with `", ".join(parts)`, put the name and the brackets around it in an f-string, add `" -> "` and `repr(result)`, append the line to `log`, and return the result.
--- check case | Logs positional and keyword arguments, and returns the result
(lambda g: (exec("calls = []\n@logged(calls)\ndef add(a, b):\n    return a + b\nr = add(2, b=3)\n", g), (g["r"], g["calls"]))[1])(dict(globals()))
=> (5, ["add(2, b=3) -> 5"])
--- check case | Strings show with their quotes
(lambda g: (exec("calls = []\n@logged(calls)\ndef greet(name, punct='.'):\n    return 'hi ' + name + punct\ngreet('ada', punct='!')\n", g), g["calls"])[1])(dict(globals()))
=> ["greet('ada', punct='!') -> 'hi ada!'"]
--- check case | No arguments, and a result of None, twice
(lambda g: (exec("calls = []\n@logged(calls)\ndef ping():\n    pass\nping()\nping()\n", g), g["calls"])[1])(dict(globals()))
=> ["ping() -> None", "ping() -> None"]
--- check test | Keeps the original's name
logged([])(len).__name__ == "len"

+++ practice | Remember answers, even None
--- task
Write a decorator `memoize(func)` for functions that take only positional arguments. The wrapper keeps a dict of answers in an attribute `cache`, filed under the tuple of arguments, `args`:

- if `args` is already in `cache`, return the stored answer without calling `func`;
- otherwise call `func`, store the answer under `args`, and return it.

A new decorated function starts with an empty `cache`. Answers like `0`, `""` and `None` must be remembered too, so test whether the key is **in** the dict, not whether the stored value is true. Use `@functools.wraps(func)`.
--- starter
import functools


def memoize(func):
    @functools.wraps(func)
    def wrapper(*args):
        if wrapper.cache.get(args):
            return wrapper.cache[args]
        result = func(*args)
        wrapper.cache[args] = result
        return result

    wrapper.cache = {}
    return wrapper
--- solution
import functools


def memoize(func):
    @functools.wraps(func)
    def wrapper(*args):
        if args in wrapper.cache:
            return wrapper.cache[args]
        result = func(*args)
        wrapper.cache[args] = result
        return result

    wrapper.cache = {}
    return wrapper
--- hint
`wrapper.cache.get(args)` gives back the stored answer, and an answer of `0` or `None` counts as false, so the starter calls `func` again for those.
--- hint
Ask the dict whether the key is there at all: `if args in wrapper.cache:`.
--- check test | A repeated call is answered from the cache
(lambda calls: (lambda f: (f(2), f(2), f(3), calls)[3] == [2, 3])(memoize(lambda x: calls.append(x) or x * x)))([])
--- check test | A result of None is remembered
(lambda calls: (lambda f: (f(1), f(1), len(calls))[2] == 1)(memoize(lambda x: calls.append(x))))([])
--- check test | A result of 0 is remembered
(lambda calls: (lambda f: (f(5), f(5), len(calls))[2] == 1)(memoize(lambda x: calls.append(x) or 0)))([])
--- check case | The cache is filed under the tuple of arguments
(lambda f: (f(2, 3), f.cache)[1])(memoize(lambda a, b: a ** b))
=> {(2, 3): 8}
--- check test | A new function starts empty, and keeps its name
memoize(len).cache == {} and memoize(len).__name__ == "len"

+++ practice | Debug: the fallback that fell over
--- task
`fallback(value)` is a decorator with an argument: if the decorated function raises any `Exception`, the wrapper returns `value` instead. `parse_speed(text)` is decorated with it, so that `parse_speed("12.5")` gives `12.5` and `parse_speed("fast")` gives `0.0`.

**The bug report:** `parse_speed("12.5")` gives back a function instead of a number. There are two bugs, one in the decorator and one on the `@` line. Fix both.
--- starter
import functools


def fallback(value):
    def decorator(func):
        @functools.wraps(func)
        def wrapper(*args, **kwargs):
            try:
                func(*args, **kwargs)
            except Exception:
                return value

        return wrapper

    return decorator


@fallback
def parse_speed(text):
    return float(text)
--- solution
import functools


def fallback(value):
    def decorator(func):
        @functools.wraps(func)
        def wrapper(*args, **kwargs):
            try:
                return func(*args, **kwargs)
            except Exception:
                return value

        return wrapper

    return decorator


@fallback(0.0)
def parse_speed(text):
    return float(text)
--- hint
`@fallback` with no brackets means `parse_speed = fallback(parse_speed)`. Which layer is `parse_speed` now, and what does calling it hand back?
--- hint
The decorator takes a setting, so it needs brackets: `@fallback(0.0)`. Then look at the `try` in the wrapper: when the call works, what does the wrapper return?
--- check case | A good value parses
parse_speed("12.5")
=> 12.5
--- check case | A bad value gives the fallback
parse_speed("fast")
=> 0.0
--- check case | The wrapper returns the result when nothing goes wrong
fallback(None)(lambda: 5)()
=> 5
--- check case | Any fallback value works
fallback(-1)(int)("x")
=> -1
--- check test | parse_speed keeps its name
parse_speed.__name__ == "parse_speed"

+++ practice | Trace a recursive function
--- task
Write a decorator `trace(func)` for functions that take positional arguments only. It records each call as lines in a list, indented to show which call is inside which, so you can watch a function that calls itself.

- The wrapper has two attributes: `lines`, a list that starts empty, and `depth`, a number that starts at 0.
- On each call it appends `"  " * depth` (two spaces per level), then the name, then the `repr` of each argument joined with `", "` in round brackets, like `"  fact(2)"`.
- Then it adds 1 to `depth`, calls `func`, and takes 1 off `depth` again. The `depth` must come back down **even if `func` raises**, so use `try` / `finally`.
- When the call returns, it appends the same indentation, then `"-> "` and the `repr` of the result, like `"  -> 2"`, and returns the result. A call that raises gets no `->` line; its error carries on.

For example, a decorated factorial called as `fact(3)` leaves `fact.lines` as `["fact(3)", "  fact(2)", "    fact(1)", "    -> 1", "  -> 2", "-> 6"]`. Use `@functools.wraps(func)`.
--- starter
import functools


def trace(func):
    @functools.wraps(func)
    def wrapper(*args):
        wrapper.lines.append(f"{func.__name__}{args}")
        return func(*args)

    wrapper.lines = []
    return wrapper
--- solution
import functools


def trace(func):
    @functools.wraps(func)
    def wrapper(*args):
        indent = "  " * wrapper.depth
        shown = ", ".join(repr(a) for a in args)
        wrapper.lines.append(f"{indent}{func.__name__}({shown})")
        wrapper.depth += 1
        try:
            result = func(*args)
        finally:
            wrapper.depth -= 1
        wrapper.lines.append(f"{indent}-> {repr(result)}")
        return result

    wrapper.lines = []
    wrapper.depth = 0
    return wrapper
--- hint
Because `@trace` replaces the name `fact` with the wrapper, the call `fact(n - 1)` inside the body goes through the wrapper too. So every level of the recursion is recorded, and `wrapper.depth` tells each call how deep it is.
--- hint
Work out the indentation once, at the start of the call, and use it for both of that call's lines. Put only `result = func(*args)` inside `try`, with `wrapper.depth -= 1` in the `finally`; append the `->` line after the `try`.
--- check case | A factorial, traced
(lambda g: (exec("@trace\ndef fact(n):\n    if n <= 1:\n        return 1\n    return n * fact(n - 1)\nfact(3)\n", g), g["fact"].lines)[1])(dict(globals()))
=> ["fact(3)", "  fact(2)", "    fact(1)", "    -> 1", "  -> 2", "-> 6"]
--- check case | Several arguments and a string result
(lambda f: (f("ab", 2), f.lines)[1])(trace(lambda s, n: s * n))
=> ["<lambda>('ab', 2)", "-> 'abab'"]
--- check case | An error leaves no -> line, and the depth comes back to 0
(lambda g: (exec("@trace\ndef half(n):\n    if n % 2:\n        raise ValueError(n)\n    return n // 2\ntry:\n    half(3)\nexcept ValueError:\n    pass\nhalf(4)\n", g), g["half"].lines, g["half"].depth)[1:])(dict(globals()))
=> (["half(3)", "half(4)", "-> 2"], 0)
--- check test | Starts empty, and keeps its name
(lambda f: f.lines == [] and f.depth == 0 and f.__name__ == "abs")(trace(abs))

=== py3-05 | Context managers
--- teach
Last lesson you wrapped a function in a decorator, so extra code ran before and after every call. This lesson does the same thing for a *block* of code. You will learn the `with` statement, which runs set-up code before a block and clean-up code after it, and you will build your own things that work with it.

Think of borrowing a library book. You check it out, you read it, and you give it back. Giving it back must happen whatever else happens: if you finished it, if you got bored halfway, or if you spilled juice on it. Programs borrow things all the time and must give them back.

### The problem: clean-up that gets skipped

In the exceptions lesson you met `finally`, the block that runs every time. It is the place for [[clean-up|resource-cleanup]]. Here is a file opened and closed by hand:

```python
f = open("notes.txt", "w")
try:
    f.write("hi")
finally:
    f.close()        # runs even if write() raised
```

`open(name, "w")` opens a file for writing ("w" is for write) and gives back a file object `f`. `f.close()` hands the file back to the computer. The `try` / `finally` makes sure the close happens. It works, but it is four lines of ceremony, and it is easy to forget.

### `with` does the `finally` for you

The **`with` statement** runs a block of code and guarantees the clean-up afterwards, whatever happens inside the block:

```python
with open("notes.txt", "w") as f:
    f.write("hi")
# the file is closed here, even if write() had raised
```

Read it as "with this file open, call it `f`, do the indented block". When the block ends, the file is closed. That is true if the block finished normally, and also if it raised an exception. The object after `with` is called a **context manager**: an object that knows how to set something up and how to clean it up again.

### The two methods behind `with`

Like the `for` loop in the iterators lesson, `with` runs on a small agreement, a [[protocol|protocol-word]]. Any object can work with `with` if it has two special methods:

- **`__enter__`** (say "dunder enter") does the set-up. Whatever it returns is what `as` puts in the variable.
- **`__exit__`** (say "dunder exit") does the clean-up. It is always called when the block ends.

So the statement

```python
with manager as value:
    body
```

does roughly this:

```python
value = manager.__enter__()
try:
    body
except BaseException as e:
    if not manager.__exit__(type(e), e, e.__traceback__):
        raise                   # __exit__ returned false: let it propagate
else:
    manager.__exit__(None, None, None)
```

Read it slowly, one piece at a time.

1. First `__enter__()` runs, and its result goes into `value`.
2. Then the body runs inside a `try`.
3. If the body raises anything at all, the `except` catches it. [[BaseException|base-exception]] is the parent at the very top of the exception family, so every exception is caught here. Python then calls `__exit__` with three details about the exception: its class, `type(e)`; the exception object itself, `e`; and its [[traceback|traceback-object]], `e.__traceback__`.
4. If the body raised nothing, the `else` runs, and `__exit__` is called with three `None`s, meaning "no error".

Look at the `raise` on its own line. A bare `raise` inside an `except` re-raises the exception it caught, as in the `retry` decorator you wrote last lesson. Here it runs only if `__exit__` returned something false, and then the exception carries on upward ("propagates") to whoever called this code.

### What `__exit__` gives back

That return value is the one decision `__exit__` makes about an exception:

- return `True` and the exception is **swallowed**: it stops right there, and the program carries on after the `with` block as if nothing happened;
- return `False` (or `None`, or nothing at all) and the exception carries on upward, to whoever can catch it.

Here is a small context manager written as a class. It prints an opening tag on the way in and a closing tag on the way out, like the tags around a web page's text:

```python
class Tag:
    def __init__(self, name):
        self.name = name
    def __enter__(self):
        print(f"<{self.name}>")
        return self
    def __exit__(self, exc_type, exc, tb):
        print(f"</{self.name}>")
        return False          # never hide errors

with Tag("b"):
    print("bold")
# <b>
# bold
# </b>
```

The three parameters of `__exit__` are named `exc_type`, `exc` and `tb`: the exception's class, the exception, and the traceback. Now see what happens when the block fails:

```python
try:
    with Tag("b"):
        print("bold")
        1 / 0
except ZeroDivisionError:
    print("caught it")
# <b>
# bold
# </b>
# caught it
```

The closing tag still printed. Then, because `__exit__` returned `False`, the `ZeroDivisionError` carried on and the outer `except` caught it. If `__exit__` had returned `True`, the error would have [[vanished at the end of the block|swallow-risk]], and "caught it" would never print.

### Checking the kind of exception: `issubclass`

A context manager that swallows errors should swallow only the kinds it was asked to. `__exit__` receives the exception's *class*, so it needs a test on classes. **`issubclass(A, B)`** is `True` when class `A` is class `B` or a child of it, in the family tree from the exceptions lesson:

```python
issubclass(KeyError, KeyError)       # True: a class counts as its own subclass
issubclass(KeyError, LookupError)    # True: KeyError is a child of LookupError
issubclass(ValueError, LookupError)  # False
issubclass(KeyError, (ValueError, LookupError))   # True: matches one in the tuple
```

As with `isinstance`, the second argument can be a tuple of classes, and the answer is `True` if any one of them matches. Be careful: when there was no error, `exc_type` is `None`, and `issubclass(None, ...)` raises a `TypeError`. So test `exc_type is not None` first.

### The shortcut: `@contextmanager`

Writing a class with two methods is a lot of ceremony for something small. The standard library's `contextlib` module has a decorator that builds a context manager from a generator, like the ones you wrote in the generators lesson. The generator must `yield` [[exactly once|one-yield]]:

- code **before** the `yield` is the set-up, the `__enter__` part;
- the `yield` is where the body of the `with` block runs;
- code **after** the `yield` is the clean-up, the `__exit__` part.

```python
from contextlib import contextmanager

@contextmanager
def tag(name):
    print(f"<{name}>")
    try:
        yield
    finally:
        print(f"</{name}>")

with tag("i"):
    print("hi")
# <i>
# hi
# </i>
```

Whatever you `yield` becomes the `as` value. A bare `yield`, like this one, yields `None`.

Here is the new part. If the block raises an exception, Python raises that exception inside the generator, *at the `yield`*, as if the `yield` line itself had raised it. That is why the `yield` sits inside `try` / `finally`: the `finally` runs, prints the closing tag, and the exception carries on upward.

### Two tools for the task

The task needs two more small things.

**Removing a key from a dict.** `del settings[key]` ("delete") removes that key, and its value, from the dict:

```python
settings = {"mode": "live", "x": 1}
del settings["x"]
print(settings)       # {'mode': 'live'}
```

**A marker for "not there".** `settings.get(key, default)` returns the value, or `default` when the key is missing. But if the default is `None`, you cannot tell "missing" from "the value really was `None`". The fix is a [[unique marker object|sentinel-object]]. `object()` makes a brand-new plain object that is equal to nothing else, and you test for it with `is`:

```python
_MISSING = object()
old = settings.get("speed", _MISSING)
print(old is _MISSING)     # True: "speed" was not in the dict
```

**Watch out:** the most common mistake with `@contextmanager` is leaving out the `try` / `finally` around the `yield`. Without it, the clean-up works every time you test it with good input. Then the first time the block raises, the exception comes out at the `yield`, the code after it never runs, and the clean-up is silently skipped. That is exactly the moment clean-up matters, and it is how programs end up [[leaving things changed|restore-state]] that should have been put back.

::: context resource-cleanup Borrowed things must go back
Programmers call anything a program borrows from the computer a resource: an open file, a network connection, a lock that stops two parts of a program changing the same data at once. The operating system lets each program hold only a limited number of open files at a time. A program that forgets to close them slowly runs out, and then fails in a place that has nothing to do with the real mistake. In flight software this matters even more, because one program may run for months without a restart.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 120" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">

  <rect x="10" y="30" width="95" height="40" rx="6" fill="white" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="57" y="55" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">set up</text>
  <rect x="132" y="30" width="95" height="40" rx="6" fill="white" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="180" y="55" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">use it</text>
  <rect x="255" y="30" width="95" height="40" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="302" y="55" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">give back</text>
  <line x1="105" y1="50" x2="129" y2="50" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#rc-arrow)"/>
  <line x1="227" y1="50" x2="252" y2="50" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#rc-arrow)"/>
  <text x="180" y="100" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">the last step must run even if "use it" fails</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context protocol-word An agreement, not a family
A protocol is a set of methods Python agrees to call. The iterator protocol was `__iter__` and `__next__`. The context manager protocol is `__enter__` and `__exit__`. Your class does not have to inherit from anything special. If it has the two methods, `with` accepts it. Python programmers call this duck typing: "if it walks like a duck and quacks like a duck, treat it as a duck".
:::

::: context base-exception The top of the family tree
`Exception` is not the very top of Python's exception family. Above it sits `BaseException`. A few special exceptions are children of `BaseException` but not of `Exception`: `KeyboardInterrupt`, raised when you press Ctrl+C to stop a program, and `SystemExit`, raised to end the program. `with` catches `BaseException` so that clean-up happens even for those.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 160" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="115" y="10" width="130" height="32" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="180" y="31" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">BaseException</text>
  <rect x="10" y="70" width="100" height="32" rx="6" fill="white" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="60" y="91" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">Exception</text>
  <rect x="125" y="70" width="110" height="32" rx="6" fill="white" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="180" y="91" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">KeyboardInterrupt</text>
  <rect x="250" y="70" width="100" height="32" rx="6" fill="white" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="300" y="91" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">SystemExit</text>
  <line x1="150" y1="42" x2="70" y2="70" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="180" y1="42" x2="180" y2="70" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="210" y1="42" x2="290" y2="70" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="60" y="130" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">ValueError, KeyError,</text>
  <text x="60" y="146" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">your own errors ...</text>
  <line x1="60" y1="102" x2="60" y2="117" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context traceback-object The red text, as an object
Every exception carries a traceback object in its `__traceback__` attribute. It holds the chain of frames you learned to read in the traceback lesson: which functions were running and on which lines. When an exception is never caught, Python prints that object as the red text. Most context managers ignore the `tb` parameter, but a logging tool might use it to write down where an error happened before letting it carry on.
:::

::: context swallow-risk Hiding an error on purpose
Swallowing an exception is sometimes right: "delete this file if it exists" should not fail when the file is already gone. The standard library has a ready-made tool for that, `contextlib.suppress`, and the `Suppress` class in the task is your own version of it. But a context manager that returns `True` for every exception hides real bugs, the way a smoke alarm with the battery taken out hides a fire. Swallow only the exact kinds you expect.
:::

::: context one-yield Why exactly one yield
The decorator runs your generator up to the first `yield` when the block starts, and resumes it once when the block ends. So it needs exactly one `yield`. A generator that never yields gives `RuntimeError: generator didn't yield`, and one that yields a second time gives `RuntimeError: generator didn't stop`. Behind the scenes, `@contextmanager` builds an object with `__enter__` and `__exit__` that does this driving for you, so both ways of writing a context manager end up using the same protocol.
:::

::: context sentinel-object A value that can only mean one thing
In the basics you met a sentinel: a special value that means "stop here" and can never be real data. `object()` is the safest sentinel there is. Each call makes a new object, and `is` compares identity, so `_MISSING` is only ever `is` itself. No value stored in a dict by anyone else can be mistaken for it. The underscore at the front of `_MISSING` is the same "inside detail" convention as `_balance` in the properties lesson.
:::

::: context restore-state Put everything back the way you found it
Test code often changes a setting for one test: a clock set to a fixed time, a simulator switched into a fault mode, a configuration value changed. If the test fails and the setting is not put back, every test after it runs in the wrong conditions, and the failures pile up far from the cause. Python's own testing tools include `unittest.mock.patch.dict`, which does what `temp_setting` does: change a dict for the length of a block, then restore it.
:::
--- task
Write two context managers.

**First, a class `Suppress`**, made as `Suppress(*exc_types)` with any number of exception classes. The starter already stores them in `self.exc_types`.

- In `__init__`, also set `self.caught = None`.
- `__enter__` returns `self`, the manager itself.
- `__exit__(self, exc_type, exc, tb)`: if `exc_type` is not `None` and `issubclass(exc_type, self.exc_types)` is true, store the exception `exc` in `self.caught` and return `True` to swallow it. That also catches subclasses of the listed types.
- Otherwise `__exit__` returns `False`, so any other exception passes through.

**Second, with `@contextmanager`, a generator `temp_setting(settings, key, value)`.**

- Inside the `with` block, `settings[key]` is `value`.
- Afterwards the old value is put back. If the key was not in the dict before, remove it with `del settings[key]`. Use a unique marker made with `object()` to remember "was not there".
- This must happen **even if the block raises**, and the exception must not be swallowed. So put the `yield` inside `try` / `finally`, with no `except`.
--- starter
from contextlib import contextmanager


class Suppress:
    def __init__(self, *exc_types):
        self.exc_types = exc_types


@contextmanager
def temp_setting(settings, key, value):
    settings[key] = value
    yield
--- solution
from contextlib import contextmanager


class Suppress:
    def __init__(self, *exc_types):
        self.exc_types = exc_types
        self.caught = None

    def __enter__(self):
        return self

    def __exit__(self, exc_type, exc, tb):
        if exc_type is not None and issubclass(exc_type, self.exc_types):
            self.caught = exc
            return True
        return False


_MISSING = object()


@contextmanager
def temp_setting(settings, key, value):
    old = settings.get(key, _MISSING)
    settings[key] = value
    try:
        yield
    finally:
        if old is _MISSING:
            del settings[key]
        else:
            settings[key] = old
--- hint
Start with `Suppress`. `__enter__` is one line, `return self`. In `__exit__`, remember that `exc_type` is `None` when nothing went wrong, so test `exc_type is not None` before you call `issubclass(exc_type, self.exc_types)`. A tuple of classes works as the second argument.
--- hint
For `temp_setting`, remember the old value before you change it: `old = settings.get(key, _MISSING)`, with `_MISSING = object()` written once above the function. `None` could be a real value, which is why a unique marker is used for "was not there".
--- hint
The shape is: remember `old`, set `settings[key] = value`, then `try:` with only `yield` inside, then `finally:`. In the `finally`, if `old is _MISSING` do `del settings[key]`, otherwise put `settings[key] = old` back.
--- check test | Suppress works in a real with block
(lambda g: (exec("with Suppress(ZeroDivisionError) as s:\n    1 / 0\n", g), type(g["s"].caught) is ZeroDivisionError)[1])(dict(globals()))
--- check test | __enter__ returns the manager, and a listed error is swallowed
(lambda s: s.__enter__() is s and bool(s.__exit__(KeyError, KeyError("k"), None)))(Suppress(KeyError, IndexError))
--- check test | The caught exception is stored
(lambda s, e: (s.__enter__(), s.__exit__(IndexError, e, None), s.caught is e)[2])(Suppress(KeyError, IndexError), IndexError("i"))
--- check test | Subclasses are caught too
bool(Suppress(LookupError).__exit__(KeyError, KeyError("k"), None))
--- check test | Other errors pass through
not Suppress(KeyError).__exit__(ValueError, ValueError("v"), None)
--- check test | With no error, caught stays None
(lambda s: (s.__enter__(), s.__exit__(None, None, None), s.caught)[2] is None)(Suppress(KeyError))
--- check case | temp_setting sets the value inside, restores it after
(lambda d: (lambda cm: [cm.__enter__(), d["mode"], cm.__exit__(None, None, None), dict(d)][1::2])(temp_setting(d, "mode", "test")))({"mode": "live"})
=> ["test", {"mode": "live"}]
--- check case | A key that was not there is removed afterwards
(lambda d: (lambda cm: [cm.__enter__(), d["x"], cm.__exit__(None, None, None), dict(d)][1::2])(temp_setting(d, "x", 1)))({})
=> [1, {}]
--- check case | The old value comes back even when the block raises, and the error still propagates
(lambda g: (exec("try:\n    with temp_setting(cfg, 'debug', True):\n        raise RuntimeError('boom')\nexcept RuntimeError:\n    cfg['propagated'] = True\n", g), g["cfg"])[1])({**globals(), "cfg": {"debug": False}})
=> {"debug": False, "propagated": True}
?? Put the yield inside try/finally.

+++ practice | A section in a log
--- task
Write a class `Section(log, name)` that works with `with`. `log` is a list.

- `__enter__` appends `"start "` followed by the name to `log`, and returns the `Section` itself.
- `__exit__(self, exc_type, exc, tb)` appends `"end "` followed by the name, and returns `False`, so any error in the block carries on.

For example, after `with Section(log, "boot"): log.append("work")`, the list `log` is `["start boot", "work", "end boot"]`. The `end` line must be there even when the block raises.
--- starter
class Section:
    def __init__(self, log, name):
        self.log = log
        self.name = name
--- solution
class Section:
    def __init__(self, log, name):
        self.log = log
        self.name = name

    def __enter__(self):
        self.log.append(f"start {self.name}")
        return self

    def __exit__(self, exc_type, exc, tb):
        self.log.append(f"end {self.name}")
        return False
--- hint
A class works with `with` once it has the two methods `__enter__(self)` and `__exit__(self, exc_type, exc, tb)`. Python calls `__exit__` whether or not the block raised.
--- hint
`__enter__` appends `f"start {self.name}"` and returns `self`. `__exit__` appends `f"end {self.name}"` and returns `False`.
--- check case | Start, the block, end
(lambda g: (exec("log = []\nwith Section(log, 'boot'):\n    log.append('work')\n", g), g["log"])[1])(dict(globals()))
=> ["start boot", "work", "end boot"]
--- check case | The end line is written even when the block raises, and the error carries on
(lambda g: (exec("log = []\ntry:\n    with Section(log, 'burn'):\n        1 / 0\nexcept ZeroDivisionError:\n    log.append('caught')\n", g), g["log"])[1])(dict(globals()))
=> ["start burn", "end burn", "caught"]
--- check case | Nested sections close in reverse order
(lambda g: (exec("log = []\nwith Section(log, 'a'):\n    with Section(log, 'b'):\n        pass\n", g), g["log"])[1])(dict(globals()))
=> ["start a", "start b", "end b", "end a"]
--- check test | as gives the Section itself
(lambda s: s.__enter__() is s)(Section([], "x"))

+++ practice | A stage that reports failure
--- task
Write a generator `stage(log, name)` decorated with `@contextmanager`, so it can be used as `with stage(log, "burn") as label:`. `log` is a list.

- Before the block: append `"begin "` plus the name, and yield the name in capitals, `name.upper()`, which becomes the `as` value.
- If the block raises any `Exception`: append `"failed "` plus the name, then let the same exception carry on with a bare `raise`.
- Whatever happens, as the very last thing: append `"end "` plus the name.

So a block that raises leaves `["begin burn", "failed burn", "end burn"]` in the log, and the error still reaches the caller.
--- starter
from contextlib import contextmanager


@contextmanager
def stage(log, name):
    log.append(f"begin {name}")
    yield name.upper()
    log.append(f"end {name}")
--- solution
from contextlib import contextmanager


@contextmanager
def stage(log, name):
    log.append(f"begin {name}")
    try:
        yield name.upper()
    except Exception:
        log.append(f"failed {name}")
        raise
    finally:
        log.append(f"end {name}")
--- hint
An error in the block comes out of the generator at the `yield`, as if the `yield` line had raised it. So wrap the `yield` in a `try`.
--- hint
Use all three parts: `try:` with only the `yield`; `except Exception:` that appends the "failed" line and then does a bare `raise`; and `finally:` that appends the "end" line.
--- check case | A block that works
(lambda g: (exec("log = []\nwith stage(log, 'burn') as label:\n    log.append(label)\n", g), g["log"])[1])(dict(globals()))
=> ["begin burn", "BURN", "end burn"]
--- check case | A block that fails is reported, and the error still propagates
(lambda g: (exec("log = []\ntry:\n    with stage(log, 'burn'):\n        raise KeyError('x')\nexcept KeyError:\n    log.append('caught')\n", g), g["log"])[1])(dict(globals()))
=> ["begin burn", "failed burn", "end burn", "caught"]
--- check case | Two stages, the inner one failing
(lambda g: (exec("log = []\ntry:\n    with stage(log, 'a'):\n        with stage(log, 'b'):\n            1 / 0\nexcept ZeroDivisionError:\n    pass\n", g), g["log"])[1])(dict(globals()))
=> ["begin a", "begin b", "failed b", "end b", "failed a", "end a"]

+++ practice | Spending within a budget
--- task
Write an exception class and a context manager class.

- `class OverBudget(Exception)`, with nothing more in it than `pass`.
- `class Budget`, made as `Budget(limit)`. In `__init__`, store the limit, set `self.spent = 0` and `self.exceeded = False`.
- A method `spend(amount)`: if `amount` is negative, raise `ValueError`. If `self.spent + amount` would be **more** than the limit, raise `OverBudget` and leave `spent` as it was. Otherwise add `amount` to `spent`. Spending exactly up to the limit is allowed.
- `__enter__` returns the `Budget` itself.
- `__exit__`: if the block raised `OverBudget`, set `self.exceeded = True` and return `True` to swallow it. Every other exception, including the `ValueError`, carries on. With no exception, return `False`.

For example, in `with Budget(100) as b:` the calls `b.spend(60)` and `b.spend(50)` leave `b.spent` at 60 and `b.exceeded` true, and nothing reaches the caller.
--- starter
class OverBudget(Exception):
    pass


class Budget:
    def __init__(self, limit):
        self.limit = limit
        self.spent = 0
        self.exceeded = False

    def spend(self, amount):
        self.spent += amount

    def __enter__(self):
        return self

    def __exit__(self, exc_type, exc, tb):
        return True
--- solution
class OverBudget(Exception):
    pass


class Budget:
    def __init__(self, limit):
        self.limit = limit
        self.spent = 0
        self.exceeded = False

    def spend(self, amount):
        if amount < 0:
            raise ValueError("amount cannot be negative")
        if self.spent + amount > self.limit:
            raise OverBudget(f"{amount} would go over the limit of {self.limit}")
        self.spent += amount

    def __enter__(self):
        return self

    def __exit__(self, exc_type, exc, tb):
        if exc_type is not None and issubclass(exc_type, OverBudget):
            self.exceeded = True
            return True
        return False
--- hint
Do the checks in `spend` before you change `self.spent`: a refused amount must leave it as it was.
--- hint
In `__exit__`, `exc_type` is `None` when nothing went wrong, so test `exc_type is not None and issubclass(exc_type, OverBudget)` before swallowing. The starter swallows everything, which hides the `ValueError` too.
--- check case | Going over is refused, swallowed and remembered
(lambda g: (exec("with Budget(100) as b:\n    b.spend(60)\n    b.spend(50)\n    b.spend(1)\n", g), (g["b"].spent, g["b"].exceeded))[1])(dict(globals()))
=> (60, True)
--- check case | Spending exactly the limit is fine
(lambda g: (exec("with Budget(100) as b:\n    b.spend(40)\n    b.spend(60)\n", g), (g["b"].spent, g["b"].exceeded))[1])(dict(globals()))
=> (100, False)
--- check test | A negative amount raises ValueError, and it is not swallowed
raises(ValueError, lambda: exec("with Budget(10) as b:\n    b.spend(-1)\n", dict(globals())))
--- check test | Other errors pass through
raises(KeyError, lambda: exec("with Budget(10):\n    {}['x']\n", dict(globals())))
--- check test | With no error, __exit__ returns false
(lambda b: not b.__exit__(None, None, None) and b.exceeded is False)(Budget(5))

+++ practice | Expect an error
--- task
Test code often needs to say "this block must raise this error". Write a class `Expect(exc_type)` that does that:

- `__init__` stores the class, and sets `self.error = None`.
- `__enter__` returns the `Expect` itself.
- `__exit__(self, exc_type, exc, tb)` has three cases:
  - the block raised the expected class, or a subclass of it: store the exception in `self.error` and return `True` to swallow it;
  - the block raised nothing: raise `AssertionError` with the message `"expected "` plus the expected class's name, such as `"expected ValueError"` (every class has its name in `__name__`);
  - the block raised something else: return `False`, so that error carries on.

For example, `with Expect(ValueError): int("x")` finishes quietly, but `with Expect(ValueError): int("5")` raises `AssertionError("expected ValueError")`.
--- starter
class Expect:
    def __init__(self, exc_type):
        self.exc_type = exc_type
        self.error = None

    def __enter__(self):
        return self

    def __exit__(self, exc_type, exc, tb):
        self.error = exc
        return True
--- solution
class Expect:
    def __init__(self, exc_type):
        self.exc_type = exc_type
        self.error = None

    def __enter__(self):
        return self

    def __exit__(self, exc_type, exc, tb):
        if exc_type is None:
            raise AssertionError(f"expected {self.exc_type.__name__}")
        if issubclass(exc_type, self.exc_type):
            self.error = exc
            return True
        return False
--- hint
Handle "no error at all" first: `exc_type is None`. Then `issubclass` is safe to call, and it also accepts subclasses.
--- hint
Raising inside `__exit__` is allowed: the new error is what leaves the `with` block. `self.exc_type.__name__` gives the text `"ValueError"` for `ValueError`.
--- check test | The expected error is swallowed and stored
(lambda g: (exec("with Expect(ValueError) as e:\n    int('x')\n", g), type(g["e"].error) is ValueError)[1])(dict(globals()))
--- check test | A subclass counts
(lambda g: (exec("with Expect(LookupError) as e:\n    {}['k']\n", g), type(g["e"].error) is KeyError)[1])(dict(globals()))
--- check case | No error at all is a failure
(lambda g: (exec("try:\n    with Expect(ValueError):\n        int('5')\nexcept AssertionError as err:\n    msg = str(err)\n", g), g["msg"])[1])(dict(globals()))
=> "expected ValueError"
--- check test | A different error carries on
raises(ZeroDivisionError, lambda: exec("with Expect(KeyError):\n    1 / 0\n", dict(globals())))

+++ practice | Debug: muted for good
--- task
`muted(settings)` should set `settings["volume"]` to `0` for the length of a `with` block, and put the old volume back afterwards, **even if the block raises**, without swallowing the error.

**The bug report:** after the block, the volume is always `0` instead of what it was. And when the block raises, even a correct version of the restore line would never run. Fix both bugs.
--- starter
from contextlib import contextmanager


@contextmanager
def muted(settings):
    settings["volume"] = 0
    old = settings["volume"]
    yield
    settings["volume"] = old
--- solution
from contextlib import contextmanager


@contextmanager
def muted(settings):
    old = settings["volume"]
    settings["volume"] = 0
    try:
        yield
    finally:
        settings["volume"] = old
--- hint
Read the first two lines in order. By the time `old` is taken, what is already in `settings["volume"]`?
--- hint
Remember the old volume first, then set it to 0. And an error in the block comes out at the `yield`, so the restore line must sit in a `finally` around it.
--- check case | Muted inside, restored after
(lambda g: (exec("s = {'volume': 7}\nwith muted(s):\n    inside = s['volume']\n", g), (g["inside"], g["s"]))[1])(dict(globals()))
=> (0, {"volume": 7})
--- check case | Restored even when the block raises, and the error carries on
(lambda g: (exec("s = {'volume': 4}\ntry:\n    with muted(s):\n        raise RuntimeError('boom')\nexcept RuntimeError:\n    s['caught'] = True\n", g), g["s"])[1])(dict(globals()))
=> {"volume": 4, "caught": True}
--- check case | Nested blocks restore the right value
(lambda g: (exec("s = {'volume': 9}\nwith muted(s):\n    with muted(s):\n        pass\n    middle = s['volume']\n", g), (g["middle"], g["s"]["volume"]))[1])(dict(globals()))
=> (0, 9)

+++ practice | Nested transactions
--- task
Write a class `Transaction(data)` for a dict of numbers, such as account balances. It can be used in `with` blocks, and even in a `with` block inside another one on the **same** object:

- On entering, it takes a snapshot of `data` (a new dict with the same keys and values) and adds it to the end of a list of snapshots, `self.snapshots`, which starts empty. It returns `data` itself, so `as` gives the dict to change.
- On leaving without an error, it just removes the last snapshot: the changes stay.
- On leaving with an error, it puts `data` back **in place** to the last snapshot, removes that snapshot, and lets the error carry on. In place means the caller's own dict changes back: delete every key with `del`, then copy each key and value from the snapshot.

An error in an inner block undoes only the inner block's changes. For example, with `accounts = {"ada": 10}` and `t = Transaction(accounts)`, a `with t as d:` block that sets `d["ada"] = 20` and then raises leaves `accounts` as `{"ada": 10}`.
--- starter
class Transaction:
    def __init__(self, data):
        self.data = data

    def __enter__(self):
        self.snapshot = self.data
        return self.data

    def __exit__(self, exc_type, exc, tb):
        if exc_type is not None:
            self.data = self.snapshot
        return False
--- solution
class Transaction:
    def __init__(self, data):
        self.data = data
        self.snapshots = []

    def __enter__(self):
        self.snapshots.append({k: v for k, v in self.data.items()})
        return self.data

    def __exit__(self, exc_type, exc, tb):
        snapshot = self.snapshots[-1]
        self.snapshots = self.snapshots[:-1]
        if exc_type is not None:
            for key in list(self.data):
                del self.data[key]
            for key, value in snapshot.items():
                self.data[key] = value
        return False
--- hint
The starter's snapshot is not a copy at all: `self.snapshot = self.data` is a second name for the same dict. Make a real new dict, for example with a dict comprehension over `self.data.items()`, and keep one per open block in a list.
--- hint
In `__exit__`, take the last snapshot with `self.snapshots[-1]` and drop it with `self.snapshots = self.snapshots[:-1]`. To restore in place, loop over `list(self.data)` (a list, because you must not change a dict while looping over it) deleting each key, then set every key from the snapshot. Return `False` so errors carry on.
--- check case | A failed block is undone in place, and the error carries on
(lambda g: (exec("acc = {'ada': 10}\nt = Transaction(acc)\ntry:\n    with t as d:\n        d['ada'] = 20\n        d['lin'] = 5\n        raise ValueError('no')\nexcept ValueError:\n    pass\n", g), g["acc"])[1])(dict(globals()))
=> {"ada": 10}
--- check case | A block that works keeps its changes
(lambda g: (exec("acc = {'ada': 10}\nt = Transaction(acc)\nwith t as d:\n    d['ada'] = 3\n", g), (g["acc"], g["t"].snapshots))[1])(dict(globals()))
=> ({"ada": 3}, [])
--- check case | An inner failure undoes only the inner block
(lambda g: (exec("acc = {'x': 1}\nt = Transaction(acc)\nwith t as d:\n    d['x'] = 2\n    try:\n        with t:\n            d['x'] = 99\n            d['y'] = 1\n            raise KeyError('k')\n    except KeyError:\n        pass\n    inner = dict(d)\n", g), (g["inner"], g["acc"], g["t"].snapshots))[1])(dict(globals()))
=> ({"x": 2}, {"x": 2}, [])
--- check test | as gives the caller's own dict
(lambda d: Transaction(d).__enter__() is d)({"a": 1})

=== py3-06 | Dataclasses and enums
--- teach
Last lesson you used a decorator, `@contextmanager`, to build a whole context manager out of a small generator. This lesson uses two more ready-made tools that save you writing the same code again and again. `@dataclass` writes the usual methods of a class for you, and `Enum` gives you a fixed set of named choices.

Think of a paper form, like a library card application. It has fixed boxes: name, date of birth, address. Nobody redraws the boxes for each new person; the form is printed once and filled in many times. A dataclass is that printed form for your objects.

### The ceremony of a class that holds data

Many classes exist only to hold a few pieces of data. From the lesson on classes that behave you know what a good one needs: an `__init__` to store the data, a `__repr__` so it prints well, and an `__eq__` so `==` compares the data. Written by hand, even a tiny class is long:

```python
class Item:
    def __init__(self, name, price):
        self.name = name
        self.price = price

    def __repr__(self):
        return f"Item(name={self.name!r}, price={self.price!r})"

    def __eq__(self, other):
        if not isinstance(other, Item):
            return NotImplemented
        return (self.name, self.price) == (other.name, other.price)
```

(The `!r` inside the f-string means "use `repr` here", so a string shows with its quotes.) Every field's name is typed several times. Add a field and you must remember to change three methods. Code like this, repeated with small changes, is called [[boilerplate|boilerplate]].

### `@dataclass` writes those methods for you

**`@dataclass`**, from the `dataclasses` module, is a [[decorator you put on a class|class-decorator]]. It reads the list of fields and writes `__init__`, `__repr__` and `__eq__` for you:

```python
from dataclasses import dataclass

@dataclass
class Item:
    name: str
    price: float

print(Item("tea", 2.5))                  # Item(name='tea', price=2.5)
print(Item("tea", 2.5) == Item("tea", 2.5))   # True
```

Those two short lines inside the class are the whole list of **fields**, the pieces of data each object holds. `Item("tea", 2.5)` works because the dataclass wrote an `__init__` that takes `name` and `price` in that order and stores them as `self.name` and `self.price`.

### Type annotations

`name: str` is read "name, of type str". It is a **[[type annotation|annotations]]**: a label that records what type a field is meant to hold. `price: float` says the price is meant to be a float. For a list, `list[str]` means "a list of strings".

Python does **not** check annotations when the program runs:

```python
print(Item("tea", "cheap"))    # Item(name='tea', price='cheap')   no error
```

The expert course shows the tools that do check them. `@dataclass` uses the annotations only to find the fields: a line with a name, a colon and a type is a field.

### Default values

A field can have a default, written with `=`, like a default argument in a function:

```python
@dataclass
class Item:
    name: str
    price: float = 0.0

print(Item("bag"))     # Item(name='bag', price=0.0)
```

Fields with defaults must come **after** fields without them, for the same reason as in a `def` line. Putting `price: float = 0.0` above `name: str` stops with a `TypeError` saying that a non-default argument follows a default argument.

### A list as a default: `field(default_factory=list)`

Remember the mutable default argument bug? A default of `[]` is made once, and then every call [[shares that one list|mutable-default]]. A dataclass would have the same problem, so it refuses a plain `= []`:

```python
@dataclass
class Item:
    name: str
    tags: list[str] = []
# ValueError: mutable default <class 'list'> for field tags is not allowed: use default_factory
```

The fix is `field(default_factory=list)`. A **factory** is something that makes new things. `default_factory=list` means "each time a new object is made without tags, call `list()` to make it a fresh empty list":

```python
from dataclasses import dataclass, field

@dataclass
class Item:
    name: str
    price: float
    tags: list[str] = field(default_factory=list)

a = Item("tea", 2.5)
b = Item("jam", 7.5)
a.tags.append("hot")
print(a.tags, b.tags)       # ['hot'] []   each has its own list
print(a)                    # Item(name='tea', price=2.5, tags=['hot'])
```

Note that you pass `list` itself, with no brackets. The dataclass calls it later, once per object.

### Your own methods still work

A dataclass is still an ordinary class. Below the fields you can write methods as usual, with `self`:

```python
@dataclass
class Item:
    name: str
    price: float

    def discount(self):
        self.price = self.price / 2

tea = Item("tea", 2.5)
tea.discount()
print(tea.price)       # 1.25
```

### `frozen=True`: read-only objects

`@dataclass(frozen=True)` makes objects that cannot be changed once they are made. Trying to set a field raises `FrozenInstanceError`:

```python
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class Point:
    x: float
    y: float

p = Point(1, 2)
p.x = 5      # dataclasses.FrozenInstanceError: cannot assign to field 'x'
```

A frozen dataclass is also **[[hashable|hashable]]**: Python can work out a hash for it, so it can go in a set and be a dict key. An ordinary dataclass cannot; `hash(Item("tea", 2.5))` raises `TypeError: unhashable type: 'Item'`.

```python
print(len({Point(1, 2), Point(1, 2), Point(2, 1)}))   # 2   the two equal points count once
```

### `order=True`: comparing with `<`

`@dataclass(order=True)` adds `<`, `>`, `<=` and `>=`. They compare the fields in order: the first field first, and only if that is a tie, the second, like the tuple keys in the sorting lesson:

```python
@dataclass(order=True)
class Version:
    major: int
    minor: int

print(Version(1, 2) < Version(1, 3))           # True: major ties, 2 < 3
print(sorted([Version(2, 0), Version(1, 5)]))  # [Version(major=1, minor=5), Version(major=2, minor=0)]
```

### Enums: a fixed set of named values

Now the second tool. Suppose tasks have a priority, and you store it as a string: `"low"`, `"medium"` or `"high"`. One day someone types `"hihg"`. Nothing complains. The task silently matches none of your `if` checks.

An **enum** (short for *enumeration*, a numbered list of choices) is a [[fixed set of named values|enum-modes]]. You make one as a class that inherits from `Enum`. Each line inside is one **member**: a name, `=`, and its value:

```python
from enum import Enum

class Color(Enum):
    RED = 1
    GREEN = 2
```

Now you pass `Color.RED` around instead of a string. A typo like `Color.REDD` is an immediate [[AttributeError|fail-loudly]], not a silent wrong answer.

Each member knows its name and its value, and you can look members up either way:

```python
Color.RED            # <Color.RED: 1>
Color.RED.name       # 'RED'
Color.RED.value      # 1
Color(2)             # <Color.GREEN: 2>   look up by value, with round brackets
Color["GREEN"]       # <Color.GREEN: 2>   look up by name, with square brackets
list(Color)          # [<Color.RED: 1>, <Color.GREEN: 2>]
len(Color)           # 2
```

An enum class is iterable, so `list(Color)` gives every member in the order they were written, and `len` counts them. Asking for a value that is not there, like `Color(5)`, raises `ValueError: 5 is not a valid Color`.

### Comparing enum members

A member is not the same thing as its value. Compare members with members:

```python
print(Color.RED == Color.RED)     # True
print(Color.RED is Color(1))      # True: there is only one RED
print(Color.RED == 1)              # False
```

`is` works because [[each member exists only once|enum-singleton]]. When you really need the number, use `.value`.

Plain enum members also cannot be compared with `<`: `Color.RED < Color.GREEN` raises `TypeError`. So to sort by an enum, sort on its `.value`. From the sorting lesson you know the minus trick for "biggest first" inside a tuple key. For a list of tasks that each have a `.priority` (an enum member) and a `.title`:

```python
sorted(tasks, key=lambda t: (-t.priority.value, t.title))
```

Read the key as: "highest value first (minus flips the order), then titles A to Z to break ties".

**Watch out:** comparing an enum member to its raw value. `Color.RED == 1` is `False`, with no error, so an `if` like that is quietly never true. Compare members with members (`==` or `is`), and use `.value` only when you really need the number.

::: context boilerplate Words stamped out again and again
The word comes from printing. Newspapers were once sent ready-made text, like adverts and syndicated columns, already cast on metal printing plates, to print without changing a word. Programmers borrowed it for code that is the same in every class except for a few names. Boilerplate is not only dull to write. When you add a field, it is easy to update `__init__` and forget `__eq__`, and then two objects that differ in that field compare equal.
:::

::: context class-decorator A decorator on a class
In the decorators lesson a decorator took a function and gave back a new one. A decorator can sit above a `class` too. Python builds the class, hands it to `dataclass`, and keeps what comes back under the same name. `dataclass` reads the annotated fields, writes the methods, attaches them to the class and returns it. `@dataclass(frozen=True)` has brackets because it takes settings, the same extra layer as `@repeat(3)`.
:::

::: context annotations Labels for readers and tools
Annotations also work on function parameters and results: `def area(width: float, height: float) -> float:`. The `->`, read "returns", labels the result. Python stores them but does not check them. Separate tools called type checkers, such as mypy, read the annotations and warn you before the program runs, for example when you pass a string where a float is expected. Large teams writing safety-critical code rely on checks like these, because a mistake found before a program runs is far cheaper than one found in flight.
:::

::: context mutable-default One list shared by everyone
A default value is made once, when the class or function is defined, not each time it is used. If that default is a list, every object that uses it holds the very same list, so appending for one object changes them all. `default_factory` fixes this by calling `list()` again for every new object.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 150" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <defs><marker id="md-arrow" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="9" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#1f2a44"/></marker></defs>
  <text x="85" y="18" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">tags = [ ]  (shared)</text>
  <rect x="15" y="30" width="60" height="30" rx="5" fill="white" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="45" y="50" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">a</text>
  <rect x="95" y="30" width="60" height="30" rx="5" fill="white" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="125" y="50" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">b</text>
  <rect x="45" y="100" width="80" height="30" rx="5" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="85" y="120" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">one list</text>
  <line x1="45" y1="60" x2="75" y2="97" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#md-arrow)"/>

  <rect x="205" y="30" width="60" height="30" rx="5" fill="white" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="235" y="50" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">a</text>
  <rect x="285" y="30" width="60" height="30" rx="5" fill="white" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="315" y="50" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">b</text>
  <rect x="200" y="100" width="70" height="30" rx="5" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="235" y="120" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">list</text>
  <rect x="280" y="100" width="70" height="30" rx="5" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="315" y="120" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">list</text>
  <line x1="235" y1="60" x2="235" y2="97" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#md-arrow)"/>
  <line x1="315" y1="60" x2="315" y2="97" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#md-arrow)"/>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context hashable Why only frozen objects can be keys
You met hashes when you first used sets: a set or dict turns each item into a number, its hash, and uses it to choose the shelf the item lives on. The hash is worked out from the data. If an object's data could change after it went on a shelf, its hash would change too, and Python would look for it on the wrong shelf and never find it. So, by default, a dataclass only gets a hash when it is frozen and its data can never change. Tuples and strings can be keys for the same reason: they never change.
:::

::: context enum-modes Named modes on a spacecraft
Flight software is full of fixed sets of choices. A spacecraft is in exactly one mode at a time, such as a normal operations mode or a safe mode, and each command it accepts is one of a fixed list. Writing these as named constants, rather than loose strings or bare numbers, means a misspelt mode is caught when the code is checked or first run, not discovered in orbit. The `.value` numbers are still useful when a mode has to be sent to the ground as a small number inside a telemetry packet.
:::

::: context fail-loudly Fail early, fail loudly
Engineers prefer a mistake that stops the program with a clear error over one that quietly gives a wrong answer. `Color.REDD` stops at once, on the exact line with the typo, and the traceback names it. A string `"hihg"` travels on through the program and causes trouble somewhere else, much later, where the real cause is hard to find. It is the same idea as fixing bad data at its source in the traceback lesson.
:::

::: context enum-singleton Only one of each
When Python builds the `Color` class, it makes each member exactly once. `Color.RED`, `Color(1)` and `Color["RED"]` all hand back that same object, which you can check with `id`. That is why `is` is safe for enum members, even though for most values you should use `==`. It is also why a member can safely be a dict key or go in a set.
:::
--- task
Write these four pieces. The starter already imports `dataclass`, `field` and `Enum`.

- An `Enum` called `Priority` with members `LOW = 1`, `MEDIUM = 2` and `HIGH = 3`.
- A dataclass `Task` with four fields, in this order: `title` (a `str`, no default); `priority` (default `Priority.MEDIUM`); `tags` (a list of strings, default empty and **not shared** between tasks, so use `field(default_factory=list)`); and `done` (a `bool`, default `False`). Replace the starter's hand-written `Task` class with it. Give it one method, `complete(self)`, that sets `self.done` to `True`.
- A frozen dataclass `Point` with two fields, `x` and `y` (floats).
- A function `by_priority(tasks)` that returns the tasks sorted highest priority first, and tasks with the same priority by `title` from A to Z. Use `sorted` with a `key`.
--- starter
from dataclasses import dataclass, field
from enum import Enum


class Task:
    def __init__(self, title):
        self.title = title


def by_priority(tasks):
    return tasks
--- solution
from dataclasses import dataclass, field
from enum import Enum


class Priority(Enum):
    LOW = 1
    MEDIUM = 2
    HIGH = 3


@dataclass
class Task:
    title: str
    priority: Priority = Priority.MEDIUM
    tags: list[str] = field(default_factory=list)
    done: bool = False

    def complete(self):
        self.done = True


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class Point:
    x: float
    y: float


def by_priority(tasks):
    return sorted(tasks, key=lambda t: (-t.priority.value, t.title))
--- hint
Build `Priority` like `Color`: `class Priority(Enum):` with one `NAME = number` line per member. Then write `@dataclass` above `class Task:` and list the four fields with annotations. `tags: list[str] = field(default_factory=list)` gives each task its own list.
--- hint
`complete` is an ordinary method inside `Task`, below the fields, whose only line is `self.done = True`. For `Point`, put `@dataclass(frozen=True)` above `class Point:` and give it two annotated fields, `x: float` and `y: float`.
--- hint
Enum members do not compare with `<`, so sort on the value: `return sorted(tasks, key=lambda t: (-t.priority.value, t.title))`. The minus puts the highest priority first, and the title breaks ties from A to Z.
--- check test | Priority members and lookups
Priority.HIGH.value == 3 and Priority(1) is Priority.LOW and Priority["MEDIUM"] is Priority.MEDIUM and len(Priority) == 3
--- check case | Task has a generated repr with the defaults
repr(Task("write"))
=> "Task(title='write', priority=<Priority.MEDIUM: 2>, tags=[], done=False)"
--- check test | Two tasks with the same data are equal
Task("a", Priority.LOW) == Task("a", Priority.LOW) and Task("a") != Task("b")
--- check test | Tasks do not share their tag list
(lambda a, b: (a.tags.append("x"), b.tags)[1] == [])(Task("a"), Task("b"))
--- check test | complete() marks the task done
(lambda t: (t.complete(), t.done)[1] is True)(Task("a"))
--- check test | Point is frozen
raises(__import__("dataclasses").FrozenInstanceError, lambda: setattr(Point(1, 2), "x", 5))
--- check test | Frozen points can go in a set
len({Point(1, 2), Point(1, 2), Point(2, 1)}) == 2
--- check case | by_priority sorts by priority, then title
[t.title for t in by_priority([Task("b"), Task("z", Priority.HIGH), Task("a"), Task("c", Priority.LOW), Task("m", Priority.HIGH)])]
=> ["m", "z", "a", "b", "c"]

+++ practice | A satellite record
--- task
Replace the hand-written class in the starter with a dataclass `Satellite` that has three fields, in this order: `name` (a `str`), `altitude_km` (a `float`) and `active` (a `bool`, default `True`).

Give it one method, `describe(self)`, that returns the name, `" at "`, the altitude with one decimal place, and `" km"`, like `"Hubble at 540.0 km"`. If the satellite is not active, add `" (inactive)"` at the end.

For example, `repr(Satellite("Hubble", 540.0))` is `"Satellite(name='Hubble', altitude_km=540.0, active=True)"`.
--- starter
from dataclasses import dataclass


class Satellite:
    def __init__(self, name, altitude_km):
        self.name = name
        self.altitude_km = altitude_km
--- solution
from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass
class Satellite:
    name: str
    altitude_km: float
    active: bool = True

    def describe(self):
        text = f"{self.name} at {self.altitude_km:.1f} km"
        if not self.active:
            text += " (inactive)"
        return text
--- hint
Put `@dataclass` above `class Satellite:` and list the three fields with annotations, the one with a default last: `active: bool = True`.
--- hint
In `describe`, the format spec `:.1f` inside the braces shows one decimal place. Build the text first, and add `" (inactive)"` to it when `self.active` is false.
--- check case | The generated repr, with the default
repr(Satellite("Hubble", 540.0))
=> "Satellite(name='Hubble', altitude_km=540.0, active=True)"
--- check test | Equal data means equal satellites
Satellite("A", 1.0) == Satellite("A", 1.0) and Satellite("A", 1.0) != Satellite("A", 1.0, False)
--- check case | describe an active satellite
Satellite("ISS", 408.33).describe()
=> "ISS at 408.3 km"
--- check case | describe an inactive one
Satellite("Vanguard 1", 3000, False).describe()
=> "Vanguard 1 at 3000.0 km (inactive)"

+++ practice | Turning on a compass
--- task
Write an `Enum` called `Direction` with four members, in this order: `NORTH = 0`, `EAST = 90`, `SOUTH = 180` and `WEST = 270`. Each value is the angle in degrees. Then write three functions:

- `turn_right(d)` returns the member 90 degrees clockwise: add 90 to the value, take the remainder after dividing by 360 (`% 360`), and look the member up by value.
- `turn_left(d)` returns the member 90 degrees the other way. (In Python, `-90 % 360` is `270`, so the same trick works.)
- `parse_direction(text)` looks a member up by name, ignoring spaces at the ends and capitals: `parse_direction(" east ")` is `Direction.EAST`. An unknown name raises the `KeyError` that the lookup raises.
--- starter
from enum import Enum


class Direction(Enum):
    NORTH = 0
    EAST = 90
    SOUTH = 180
    WEST = 270


def turn_right(d):
    return d


def turn_left(d):
    return d


def parse_direction(text):
    return Direction[text]
--- solution
from enum import Enum


class Direction(Enum):
    NORTH = 0
    EAST = 90
    SOUTH = 180
    WEST = 270


def turn_right(d):
    return Direction((d.value + 90) % 360)


def turn_left(d):
    return Direction((d.value - 90) % 360)


def parse_direction(text):
    return Direction[text.strip().upper()]
--- hint
`Direction(180)`, with round brackets, looks a member up by its value. `d.value` is the angle of the member you were given.
--- hint
`parse_direction` looks up by name, with square brackets: `Direction[...]`. Clean the text first with `strip()` and `upper()`.
--- check test | Turning right goes round the compass
turn_right(Direction.NORTH) is Direction.EAST and turn_right(Direction.WEST) is Direction.NORTH
--- check test | Turning left goes the other way
turn_left(Direction.NORTH) is Direction.WEST and turn_left(Direction.SOUTH) is Direction.EAST
--- check test | Four right turns come back to the start
turn_right(turn_right(turn_right(turn_right(Direction.SOUTH)))) is Direction.SOUTH
--- check test | parse_direction ignores spaces and capitals
parse_direction(" east ") is Direction.EAST and parse_direction("West") is Direction.WEST
--- check test | An unknown name raises KeyError
raises(KeyError, lambda: parse_direction("up"))

+++ practice | The newest version
--- task
Version numbers like `"1.10.2"` do not sort correctly as text: `"1.9.0"` comes after `"1.10.0"` in A to Z order. Fix that with a dataclass.

- `Version`: a dataclass with `order=True` and `frozen=True`, and three whole-number fields `major`, `minor` and `patch`, where `patch` defaults to `0`. Give it a method `text(self)` that gives back `"1.10.2"` for `Version(1, 10, 2)`.
- `parse_version(s)`: strips `s`, splits it at the dots, turns each part into an `int`, and returns a `Version`. `"2.1"` has no patch number, so it becomes `Version(2, 1, 0)`. Spreading the list of numbers into the call with `*` handles both lengths.
- `newest(texts)`: returns the text of the highest version in the list, or `None` for an empty list.

For example, `newest(["1.9.0", "1.10.0", "1.2.11"])` is `"1.10.0"`.
--- starter
from dataclasses import dataclass


def parse_version(s):
    return s.strip()


def newest(texts):
    return max(texts)
--- solution
from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(order=True, frozen=True)
class Version:
    major: int
    minor: int
    patch: int = 0

    def text(self):
        return f"{self.major}.{self.minor}.{self.patch}"


def parse_version(s):
