    return Version(*[int(part) for part in s.strip().split(".")])


def newest(texts):
    if not texts:
        return None
    return max(parse_version(t) for t in texts).text()
--- hint
With `order=True`, versions compare field by field: `major` first, then `minor`, then `patch`, as whole numbers. So `max` and `sorted` work on them directly.
--- hint
`[int(part) for part in s.strip().split(".")]` gives a list of two or three numbers, and `Version(*numbers)` spreads them into the call. In `newest`, parse every text, take the `max`, and return its `.text()`.
--- check case | 1.10 is newer than 1.9
newest(["1.9.0", "1.10.0", "1.2.11"])
=> "1.10.0"
--- check case | A missing patch number is 0
repr(parse_version(" 2.1 "))
=> "Version(major=2, minor=1, patch=0)"
--- check case | Sorting versions
[v.text() for v in sorted(parse_version(t) for t in ["0.10.1", "0.9.9", "0.10.0"])]
=> ["0.9.9", "0.10.0", "0.10.1"]
--- check case | No versions at all
newest([])
=> None
--- check test | Versions are frozen and can go in a set
len({parse_version("1.0"), parse_version("1.0.0"), parse_version("1.0.1")}) == 2 and raises(__import__("dataclasses").FrozenInstanceError, lambda: setattr(Version(1, 0), "major", 2))

+++ practice | Log levels from messy text
--- task
Write an `Enum` called `Level` with members `DEBUG = 10`, `INFO = 20`, `WARNING = 30` and `ERROR = 40`, and two functions.

`parse_level(text)` turns text from a settings file into a member, or returns `None` if it cannot:

- spaces at the ends do not matter;
- a name in any capitals works: `"  warning "` gives `Level.WARNING`;
- a whole number written as digits works if it is one of the values: `"30"` gives `Level.WARNING`, but `"25"` gives `None`;
- anything else gives `None`: an unknown name, `"-10"`, or an empty string.

`at_least(records, level)` takes a list of `(Level, message)` tuples and returns a list of the ones whose level's value is at least `level`'s value, in their original order.
--- starter
from enum import Enum


class Level(Enum):
    DEBUG = 10
    INFO = 20
    WARNING = 30
    ERROR = 40


def parse_level(text):
    return Level[text]


def at_least(records, level):
    return [r for r in records if r[0] == level]
--- solution
from enum import Enum


class Level(Enum):
    DEBUG = 10
    INFO = 20
    WARNING = 30
    ERROR = 40


def parse_level(text):
    text = text.strip()
    if text.isdigit():
        try:
            return Level(int(text))
        except ValueError:
            return None
    try:
        return Level[text.upper()]
    except KeyError:
        return None


def at_least(records, level):
    return [(lv, message) for lv, message in records if lv.value >= level.value]
--- hint
There are two lookups and each fails in its own way: `Level(25)` raises `ValueError`, and `Level["LOUD"]` raises `KeyError`. Catch each one and return `None`.
--- hint
Strip the text first. If `text.isdigit()`, try the lookup by value with `Level(int(text))`; otherwise try the lookup by name with `Level[text.upper()]`. `"-10"` is not all digits, so it goes to the name lookup and fails there. Members do not compare with `>=`, so compare their `.value`.
--- check test | Names in any capitals, with spaces
parse_level("  warning ") is Level.WARNING and parse_level("Info") is Level.INFO
--- check test | Numbers that are values
parse_level("30") is Level.WARNING and parse_level(" 10") is Level.DEBUG
--- check test | Anything else gives None
all(parse_level(t) is None for t in ["25", "-10", "", "   ", "loud", "4O"])
--- check case | at_least keeps the order
at_least([(Level.ERROR, "a"), (Level.DEBUG, "b"), (Level.WARNING, "c")], Level.WARNING)
=> [(Level.ERROR, "a"), (Level.WARNING, "c")]
--- check case | at_least of nothing
at_least([], Level.DEBUG)
=> []

+++ practice | Debug: the check that never failed
--- task
Two small bugs in a test report.

- `failures(checks)` should return the names of the checks whose status is `Status.FAIL`. It always returns `[]`, with no error.
- `distinct_cells(cells)` should count the different `Cell`s in a list: `distinct_cells([Cell(0, 0), Cell(0, 0), Cell(1, 2)])` should be `2`. It stops with `TypeError: unhashable type: 'Cell'`.

Fix both. Each fix is one line.
--- starter
from dataclasses import dataclass
from enum import Enum


class Status(Enum):
    OK = 1
    FAIL = 2


@dataclass
class Check:
    name: str
    status: Status = Status.OK


@dataclass
class Cell:
    row: int
    col: int


def failures(checks):
    return [c.name for c in checks if c.status == 2]


def distinct_cells(cells):
    return len(set(cells))
--- solution
from dataclasses import dataclass
from enum import Enum


class Status(Enum):
    OK = 1
    FAIL = 2


@dataclass
class Check:
    name: str
    status: Status = Status.OK


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class Cell:
    row: int
    col: int


def failures(checks):
    return [c.name for c in checks if c.status == Status.FAIL]


def distinct_cells(cells):
    return len(set(cells))
--- hint
Try `Status.FAIL == 2` on its own. An enum member is not equal to its value.
--- hint
Compare members with members: `c.status == Status.FAIL` (or `is`). Only frozen dataclasses get a hash, and a set needs one: `@dataclass(frozen=True)` above `class Cell:`.
--- check case | failures finds the failed checks
failures([Check("boot"), Check("fuel", Status.FAIL), Check("comms", Status.FAIL)])
=> ["fuel", "comms"]
--- check case | No failures
failures([Check("boot")])
=> []
--- check case | distinct_cells counts equal cells once
distinct_cells([Cell(0, 0), Cell(0, 0), Cell(1, 2)])
=> 2
--- check case | No cells
distinct_cells([])
=> 0

+++ practice | A cargo manifest
--- task
Build a cargo manifest from an enum and two dataclasses.

- `Kind`, an `Enum` with `FUEL = 1`, `FOOD = 2` and `TOOL = 3`.
- `Item`, a dataclass with fields `name` (`str`), `kind` (a `Kind`) and `qty` (`int`, default `0`).
- `Manifest`, a dataclass with one field, `items`: a list of `Item`s, empty by default and **not shared** between manifests. Give it three methods:
  - `add(name, kind, qty)`: if `qty` is less than 1, raise `ValueError`. If an item with the same name **and** kind is already listed, add `qty` to its `qty`. Otherwise append a new `Item`.
  - `total(kind)`: the sum of `qty` over the items of that kind (`0` if there are none).
  - `report()`: a list of lines, one per kind that has items, in the enum's order. Each line is the kind's name, `": "`, then its items sorted by name, each as the name, a space and the qty, joined with `", "`.

For example, after adding `("oxygen", Kind.FUEL, 12)`, `("hydrazine", Kind.FUEL, 40)` and `("rice", Kind.FOOD, 5)`, `report()` is `["FUEL: hydrazine 40, oxygen 12", "FOOD: rice 5"]`.
--- starter
from dataclasses import dataclass, field
from enum import Enum


class Kind(Enum):
    FUEL = 1
    FOOD = 2
    TOOL = 3


@dataclass
class Item:
    name: str
    kind: Kind
    qty: int = 0


class Manifest:
    def __init__(self, items=[]):
        self.items = items

    def add(self, name, kind, qty):
        self.items.append(Item(name, kind, qty))
--- solution
from dataclasses import dataclass, field
from enum import Enum


class Kind(Enum):
    FUEL = 1
    FOOD = 2
    TOOL = 3


@dataclass
class Item:
    name: str
    kind: Kind
    qty: int = 0


@dataclass
class Manifest:
    items: list[Item] = field(default_factory=list)

    def add(self, name, kind, qty):
        if qty < 1:
            raise ValueError("qty must be at least 1")
        for item in self.items:
            if item.name == name and item.kind == kind:
                item.qty += qty
                return
        self.items.append(Item(name, kind, qty))

    def total(self, kind):
        return sum(item.qty for item in self.items if item.kind == kind)

    def report(self):
        lines = []
        for kind in Kind:
            mine = sorted((item for item in self.items if item.kind == kind), key=lambda item: item.name)
            if mine:
                lines.append(f"{kind.name}: " + ", ".join(f"{item.name} {item.qty}" for item in mine))
        return lines
--- hint
The starter's `items=[]` is the shared default list bug: every manifest made without a list gets the same one. As a dataclass field, `field(default_factory=list)` gives each manifest its own.
--- hint
`add` loops over the items looking for one with the same `name` and `kind`; if it finds one, it adds to its `qty` and returns straight away. `total` is `sum` over a generator expression with an `if`.
--- hint
Looping over `Kind` gives its members in the order they were written. For each one, pick out its items, sort them by name, and skip the kind if there are none. `kind.name` gives `"FUEL"`.
--- check case | The report, kinds in enum order, items by name
(lambda m: (m.add("oxygen", Kind.FUEL, 12), m.add("hydrazine", Kind.FUEL, 40), m.add("rice", Kind.FOOD, 5), m.report())[3])(Manifest())
=> ["FUEL: hydrazine 40, oxygen 12", "FOOD: rice 5"]
--- check case | Adding the same item again adds to its qty
(lambda m: (m.add("rice", Kind.FOOD, 5), m.add("rice", Kind.FOOD, 3), m.add("rice", Kind.TOOL, 1), m.items)[3])(Manifest())
=> [Item("rice", Kind.FOOD, 8), Item("rice", Kind.TOOL, 1)]
--- check test | total, including a kind with nothing
(lambda m: (m.add("a", Kind.TOOL, 2), m.add("b", Kind.TOOL, 3), m.total(Kind.TOOL) == 5 and m.total(Kind.FUEL) == 0)[2])(Manifest())
--- check test | Two manifests do not share their items
(lambda a, b: (a.add("x", Kind.FOOD, 1), b.items)[1] == [])(Manifest(), Manifest())
--- check test | A qty below 1 raises ValueError
raises(ValueError, lambda: Manifest().add("x", Kind.FOOD, 0))
--- check case | An empty manifest
(repr(Manifest()), Manifest().report())
=> ("Manifest(items=[])", [])

=== py3-07 | Special methods: making your own types feel built in
--- teach
Last lesson `@dataclass` wrote `__init__`, `__repr__` and `__eq__` for you. Those are three of Python's special methods. There are many more, and this lesson shows the most useful ones. With them, your own objects can work with `len`, square brackets, `for` loops, `+`, `*`, `abs` and sets, the same way lists and numbers do.

Think of a wall socket. Any lamp, phone charger or kettle works in it, as long as it has the right plug. The socket does not care what the device is. Python's syntax is the socket, and special methods are the plug: give your class the right method, and the matching piece of syntax works on it.

### Syntax is method calls in disguise

Why does `len(x)` work on a list, `x[0]` on a string and `a + b` on numbers? Because those types define **[[special methods|dunder-word]]**, the methods with two underscores on each side of the name, also called *dunder* methods. When you write a piece of syntax, Python turns it into a call to one of them:

| You write | Python calls |
|---|---|
| `len(v)` | `v.__len__()` |
| `v[i]` | `v.__getitem__(i)` |
| `for x in v` | `v.__iter__()` |
| `x in v` | `v.__contains__(x)` |
| `a + b` | `a.__add__(b)` |
| `a * 3` | `a.__mul__(3)` |
| `3 * a` | `a.__rmul__(3)` (after `int.__mul__` gives up) |
| `a == b` | `a.__eq__(b)` |
| `hash(a)` | `a.__hash__()` |
| `abs(a)` | `a.__abs__()` |
| `bool(a)` | `a.__bool__()` (or `__len__` if there is no `__bool__`) |

Define them and your objects work with the whole language: `sum`, `sorted`, `in`, sets, dict keys. Giving `+` or `*` a meaning for your own class is called [[operator overloading|operator-overloading]]. The rest of this lesson takes the table a few rows at a time.

### Length, square brackets and loops

Here is a small class that holds some items in a tuple. Three special methods each hand the job over to the tuple:

```python
class Shelf:
    def __init__(self, *items):
        self.items = tuple(items)

    def __len__(self):
        return len(self.items)

    def __getitem__(self, index):
        return self.items[index]

    def __iter__(self):
        return iter(self.items)

s = Shelf("a", "b", "c")
print(len(s))        # 3      Python called s.__len__()
print(s[0], s[-1])   # a c    Python called s.__getitem__(0), then (-1)
print(list(s))       # ['a', 'b', 'c']
print("b" in s)      # True
```

`__init__` uses `*items`, the star from the `*args` lesson, so `Shelf` takes any number of items. `__iter__` returns an iterator, exactly as in the iterators lesson: `iter(self.items)` asks the tuple for one. There is no `__contains__` here, yet `in` still works: Python [[falls back|fallbacks]] to looping over the object and comparing each item.

### Adding two objects: `__add__`

When you write `a + b`, Python calls `a.__add__(b)`. The left side is `self`, and the right side arrives as the parameter, usually called `other`:

```python
class Money:
    def __init__(self, cents):
        self.cents = cents
    def __add__(self, other):
        if not isinstance(other, Money):
            return NotImplemented
        return Money(self.cents + other.cents)
    def __repr__(self):
        return f"Money({self.cents})"

print(Money(150) + Money(75))     # Money(225)
```

You met `NotImplemented` with `__eq__` in the lesson on classes that behave. It is a special value, not an error. Returning it tells Python "I don't know how to do this", so Python can [[try the other operand's method|reflected-ops]], and raise a clear `TypeError` if neither knows:

```python
Money(150) + 5
# TypeError: unsupported operand type(s) for +: 'Money' and 'int'
```

Notice that `__add__` builds a **new** `Money` and returns it. It does not touch `self.cents`. After `total = a + b`, the value `a` must be the same as before, exactly as with numbers. Subtraction works the same way, with **`__sub__`** for `a - b`.

### Numbers on the right: `__rmul__`

Multiplying by a number works the same way: `a * 3` calls `a.__mul__(3)`. But `3 * a` is harder, because now the number is on the left. Python first asks the number: `int.__mul__(3, a)`. An `int` has never heard of your class, so it returns `NotImplemented`. Python then tries the **reflected** method on the right-hand side, `a.__rmul__(3)` (the "r" is for "right").

For multiplying by a number, the answer is the same whichever side the number is on. So you can give the one method [[a second name|method-alias]] inside the class:

```python
class Money:
    # ... __init__, __add__ and __repr__ as before ...
    def __mul__(self, k):
        if not isinstance(k, int):
            return NotImplemented
        return Money(self.cents * k)

    __rmul__ = __mul__

print(Money(2) * 3)     # Money(6)
print(3 * Money(2))     # Money(6)
```

The line `__rmul__ = __mul__` says "the method called `__rmul__` is the same function as `__mul__`".

### Equal things need equal hashes

When you met sets, you learned that a set or dict turns each item into a number, its **hash**, to decide which shelf it lives on. `hash(x)` gives that number. The rule that keeps sets and dicts working is:

**Objects that compare equal must have equal hashes.**

If two equal objects had different hashes, a set would put them on different shelves and keep both, and a dict could not find a key you know is there. To stop that happening by accident, when your class defines `__eq__`, Python sets `__hash__` to `None`. That makes the objects **unhashable**:

```python
class Pair:
    def __init__(self, a, b):
        self.components = (a, b)
    def __eq__(self, other):
        if not isinstance(other, Pair):
            return NotImplemented
        return self.components == other.components

print(Pair(1, 2) == Pair(1, 2))   # True
{Pair(1, 2)}                      # TypeError: unhashable type: 'Pair'
```

To fix it, define **`__hash__`** yourself. [[Hash the same data you compare|hash-rule]], and only if that data cannot change. A tuple never changes, and a tuple of numbers can be hashed, so a class that keeps its data in a tuple can hand the job over:

```python fragment
    def __hash__(self):          # inside class Pair
        return hash(self.components)

print(len({Pair(1, 2), Pair(1, 2)}))   # 1   equal pairs count once
```

### Length as a number, and true or false

**`__abs__`** runs for `abs(a)`. For numbers, `abs` is the size without the sign: `abs(-7)` is `7`. For your own class, you decide what "size" means.

**`__bool__`** runs for `bool(a)`, and also whenever your object sits after `if` or `while`. It must return `True` or `False`. If a class has no `__bool__`, Python uses `__len__` instead: length 0 means false, as for an empty list. That is why `bool(Shelf())` is `False`.

Two built-ins help here. **`any(xs)`** is `True` if at least one item of `xs` is truthy. **`all(xs)`** is `True` if every item is:

```python
print(any([0, 0, 3]))    # True: 3 is truthy
print(any([0, 0, 0]))    # False: every one is 0
print(all([1, 2, 0]))    # False: 0 is falsy
```

### Slices arrive as a `slice` object

`__getitem__` receives whatever is inside the square brackets. For `v[2]` that is the number `2`. For `v[1:3]` it is a [[slice object|slice-object]], a small value that holds the start and the stop:

```python
class Show:
    def __getitem__(self, index):
        return index

print(Show()[2])      # 2
print(Show()[1:3])    # slice(1, 3, None)
```

The `None` is the step, which was not given. `isinstance(index, slice)` lets `__getitem__` handle both kinds: return one item for a number, and a new object of your own class for a slice. A tuple understands a slice object directly, so `self.items[index]` works for both.

### Building a new object from pieces

The last trick joins two things you know. A generator expression, from the generators lesson, makes values one at a time. A star in a call, from the lesson on spreading arguments, spreads values out into separate arguments. Together they build a new object:

```python
a = (1, 2)
b = (10, 20)
print(list(zip(a, b)))                    # [(1, 10), (2, 20)]
print(Shelf(*(x + y for x, y in zip(a, b))).items)   # (11, 22)
```

Read the last line from the inside out. `zip(a, b)` pairs the items up. The generator expression adds each pair. The `*` spreads the results out, so the call is `Shelf(11, 22)`.

For a `repr` of any number of values, `", ".join(...)` glues strings together with a comma and a space between them:

```python
print(", ".join(repr(c) for c in (1, 2.5)))    # 1, 2.5
```

For the length of a [[vector|vectors-in-flight]], you need a square root. `math.sqrt`, from the standard library lesson, gives it: `math.sqrt(3 * 3 + 4 * 4)` is `5.0`.

**Watch out:** making `__add__` change `self` and return it. It looks fine at first, but after `c = a + b`, `a` has changed too, and every other variable that holds `a` sees the change. `a + b` must never modify `a`. Always build and return a new object.

::: context dunder-word Double underscore, spoken fast
"Dunder" is short for "double underscore", so `__len__` is read "dunder len". Python keeps names shaped like this for itself. You write them, but you almost never call them yourself: you write `len(v)`, and Python finds `__len__`. Do not invent new names of this shape for your own ordinary methods, because a future version of Python might give that name a meaning of its own.
:::

::: context operator-overloading One symbol, many meanings
To overload something is to give it more than one job. `+` already means "add" for numbers, "join" for strings and lists, and now whatever you choose for your class. Used well, it lets maths code read like maths. The NumPy library, used throughout science and engineering, gives its arrays `__add__`, `__mul__` and friends, so `a + b` adds two whole arrays of numbers item by item.
:::

::: context fallbacks Python tries the next best thing
For `x in v`, Python looks for `__contains__` first. If there is none, it loops over `v` with `__iter__` and compares each item with `==`. For `bool(v)`, it looks for `__bool__`, then `__len__`. If a class has neither, every object of it counts as true. These fallbacks mean a few well-chosen methods give you a lot of behavior for free. Write the specific method only when you can do the job faster or differently.
:::

::: context reflected-ops Asking the other side
For `a + b`, Python first tries `a.__add__(b)`. If that returns `NotImplemented`, it tries the right-hand side's reflected method, `b.__radd__(a)`. Only if that also returns `NotImplemented`, or does not exist, does Python raise `TypeError`. This is how `3 * v` ends up calling `v.__rmul__(3)`.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 150" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <defs><marker id="ro-arrow" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="9" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#1f2a44"/></marker></defs>
  <rect x="10" y="20" width="100" height="44" rx="6" fill="white" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="60" y="40" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">a.__add__(b)</text>
  <text x="60" y="56" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">first try</text>
  <rect x="130" y="20" width="100" height="44" rx="6" fill="white" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="180" y="40" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">b.__radd__(a)</text>
  <text x="180" y="56" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">other side</text>
  <rect x="250" y="20" width="100" height="44" rx="6" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="300" y="40" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">TypeError</text>
  <text x="300" y="56" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">nobody knows</text>
  <line x1="110" y1="42" x2="127" y2="42" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#ro-arrow)"/>
  <line x1="230" y1="42" x2="247" y2="42" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#ro-arrow)"/>
  <text x="180" y="100" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">each arrow: the step before returned NotImplemented</text>
  <text x="180" y="125" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">any real answer stops the chain</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context method-alias Functions are values, even in a class
You saw in the decorators lesson that functions are values you can pass around and store under names. The inside of a class is a list of names too: each `def` makes a function and stores it under that name. `__rmul__ = __mul__` stores the same function under a second name. Python then finds it when it looks up `__rmul__`, and `self` and `k` arrive exactly as they would for `__mul__`. It only works because `k * v` and `v * k` mean the same thing; for subtraction, `k - v` and `v - k` differ, so a reflected method would need its own code.
:::

::: context hash-rule Why equal objects need equal hashes
A set looks an item up by working out its hash and going to that shelf. Suppose two equal vectors had different hashes. `Vector(1, 2)` goes on shelf 7. Later you ask whether `Vector(1, 2)` is in the set, a different object with equal data, and its hash sends Python to shelf 3. Nothing is there, so the answer is "no", even though an equal vector is in the set.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 140" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="20" y="70" width="60" height="40" rx="4" fill="white" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="50" y="126" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">shelf 3</text>
  <rect x="100" y="70" width="60" height="40" rx="4" fill="white" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="130" y="126" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">shelf 5</text>
  <rect x="180" y="70" width="60" height="40" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="210" y="95" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">(1, 2)</text>
  <text x="210" y="126" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">shelf 7</text>
  <text x="210" y="30" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">stored here</text>
  <line x1="210" y1="36" x2="210" y2="66" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="50" y="30" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">looked here</text>
  <line x1="50" y1="36" x2="50" y2="66" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="300" y="85" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">bad hash:</text>
  <text x="300" y="101" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">"not found"</text>
</svg>
```

Hashing the tuple you compare with `==` makes equal objects land on the same shelf every time.
:::

::: context slice-object The colon, packed into a value
`v[1:3]` is shorthand. Python builds `slice(1, 3, None)` from the numbers around the colons, with the start, the stop and the step, and passes that one value to `__getitem__`. You can build one yourself: `(10, 20, 30, 40)[slice(1, 3)]` gives `(20, 30)`. `v[::2]` becomes `slice(None, None, 2)`, "every second item". A slice object has `.start`, `.stop` and `.step` attributes if you ever need to look inside it.
:::

::: context vectors-in-flight Arrows with a size and a direction
A vector is a list of numbers that belong together, such as a position (east, north, up) or a velocity. Its length is found with Pythagoras, generalized to any number of parts: square each part, add them, take the square root. So `Vector(3, 4)` has length 5. Guidance and navigation software adds, subtracts and scales vectors constantly, for example to find how far a spacecraft is from where it should be. Libraries such as NumPy make that maths read naturally by defining exactly the special methods in this lesson.
:::
--- task
Write a class `Vector`. It is built from any number of numbers, as in `Vector(1, 2, 3)`, and it stores them in `self.components` as a **tuple**, so they cannot change. The starter stores a list; change that to `tuple(components)`. Then add these special methods.

- `__repr__`, so `repr(Vector(1, 2, 3))` is `"Vector(1, 2, 3)"`. Use `", ".join(...)` on the `repr` of each component.
- `__len__` and `__iter__`, handing over to the tuple.
- `__getitem__`, so `v[i]` gives one component. When the index is a slice, return a new `Vector` made from those components.
- `__add__` and `__sub__`, element by element, returning a new `Vector`. If the lengths differ, raise `ValueError`. If the other side is not a `Vector`, return `NotImplemented`.
- `__mul__`, so `v * k` multiplies every component by a number `k` (an `int` or a `float`), and `__rmul__ = __mul__` so `k * v` works too.
- `__eq__`, true when the other side is a `Vector` with equal components, and `__hash__` that returns `hash(self.components)`, so it agrees with `==`. A `Vector` is not equal to a plain tuple.
- `__abs__`, the length: square each component, add them up, and take `math.sqrt` of the total (import `math` at the top).
- `__bool__`, false only when every component is 0. `any` does this in one call.
--- starter
class Vector:
    def __init__(self, *components):
        self.components = list(components)
--- solution
import math


class Vector:
    def __init__(self, *components):
        self.components = tuple(components)

    def __repr__(self):
        return f"Vector({', '.join(repr(c) for c in self.components)})"

    def __len__(self):
        return len(self.components)

    def __iter__(self):
        return iter(self.components)

    def __getitem__(self, index):
        if isinstance(index, slice):
            return Vector(*self.components[index])
        return self.components[index]

    def _check(self, other):
        if not isinstance(other, Vector):
            return False
        if len(other) != len(self):
            raise ValueError("vectors must have the same length")
        return True

    def __add__(self, other):
        if not self._check(other):
            return NotImplemented
        return Vector(*(a + b for a, b in zip(self, other)))

    def __sub__(self, other):
        if not self._check(other):
            return NotImplemented
        return Vector(*(a - b for a, b in zip(self, other)))

    def __mul__(self, k):
        if not isinstance(k, (int, float)):
            return NotImplemented
        return Vector(*(a * k for a in self))

    __rmul__ = __mul__

    def __eq__(self, other):
        if not isinstance(other, Vector):
            return NotImplemented
        return self.components == other.components

    def __hash__(self):
        return hash(self.components)

    def __abs__(self):
        return math.sqrt(sum(a * a for a in self))

    def __bool__(self):
        return any(self.components)
--- hint
Store `self.components = tuple(components)`. Many methods then hand the job to the tuple, like `Shelf` did: `len(self.components)`, `iter(self.components)`, `hash(self.components)`. `__repr__` returns `f"Vector({', '.join(repr(c) for c in self.components)})"`.
--- hint
For `+` and `-`, first return `NotImplemented` if `other` is not a `Vector`, then raise `ValueError` if `len(other) != len(self)`. Then build the answer from pairs: `Vector(*(a + b for a, b in zip(self, other)))`. `__abs__` is `math.sqrt(sum(a * a for a in self))`, and `__bool__` is `any(self.components)`.
--- hint
`__mul__` checks `isinstance(k, (int, float))` and returns `Vector(*(a * k for a in self))`; the line `__rmul__ = __mul__` below it makes `3 * v` work. In `__getitem__`, if `isinstance(index, slice)`, return `Vector(*self.components[index])`; otherwise return `self.components[index]`.
--- check case | repr
repr(Vector(1, 2, 3))
=> "Vector(1, 2, 3)"
--- check test | len, iteration and indexing
(lambda v: len(v) == 3 and list(v) == [4, 5, 6] and v[0] == 4 and v[-1] == 6)(Vector(4, 5, 6))
--- check case | A slice is a Vector
Vector(1, 2, 3, 4)[1:3]
=> Vector(2, 3)
--- check case | Addition and subtraction
(Vector(1, 2) + Vector(10, 20), Vector(5, 5) - Vector(1, 2))
=> (Vector(11, 22), Vector(4, 3))
--- check test | Adding different lengths raises ValueError
raises(ValueError, lambda: Vector(1, 2) + Vector(1, 2, 3))
--- check test | Scaling works on both sides, and + does not change the original
(lambda v: v * 2 == Vector(2, 4) and 3 * v == Vector(3, 6) and (v + v, v)[1] == Vector(1, 2))(Vector(1, 2))
--- check test | Equal vectors hash equal and work in sets and dicts
len({Vector(1, 2), Vector(1, 2), Vector(2, 1)}) == 2 and {Vector(0, 1): "up"}[Vector(0, 1)] == "up"
--- check test | Vector is not equal to a tuple with the same numbers
Vector(1, 2) != (1, 2)
--- check test | abs is the length, bool is false only for zero
abs(Vector(3, 4)) == 5.0 and not Vector(0, 0) and bool(Vector(0, 1))
--- check test | Components cannot be changed
isinstance(Vector(1, 2).components, tuple)
?? Store the components as a tuple.

+++ practice | An inventory that answers to len, [] and in
--- task
Write a class `Inventory` that stores counts of items in a dict, `self.counts`, which starts empty. Give it one ordinary method and four special ones:

- `add(name, qty)` adds `qty` to that item's count. `qty` can be negative, to take items out.
- `inv[name]` (`__getitem__`) gives the count, or `0` for an item that was never added, instead of a `KeyError`.
- `name in inv` (`__contains__`) is true only if the count is more than 0.
- `len(inv)` (`__len__`) is how many different items have a count of more than 0.
- Looping over it (`__iter__`) gives the names with a count of more than 0, from A to Z. Return an iterator, for example `iter()` of a sorted list.

For example, after `inv.add("bolt", 3)`, `inv.add("nut", 2)` and `inv.add("nut", -2)`: `inv["bolt"]` is `3`, `inv["washer"]` is `0`, `"nut" in inv` is false and `len(inv)` is `1`.
--- starter
class Inventory:
    def __init__(self):
        self.counts = {}

    def add(self, name, qty):
        self.counts[name] = qty
--- solution
class Inventory:
    def __init__(self):
        self.counts = {}

    def add(self, name, qty):
        self.counts[name] = self.counts.get(name, 0) + qty

    def __getitem__(self, name):
        return self.counts.get(name, 0)

    def __contains__(self, name):
        return self[name] > 0

    def __len__(self):
        return sum(1 for qty in self.counts.values() if qty > 0)

    def __iter__(self):
        return iter(sorted(name for name, qty in self.counts.items() if qty > 0))
--- hint
`self.counts.get(name, 0)` gives the count or 0. Use it both in `add` (to add on to the old count) and in `__getitem__`.
--- hint
`__iter__` is a sorted list of the names whose count is more than 0, wrapped in `iter()`. `__len__` counts the counts above 0, for example with `sum(1 for ...)`. Do not write it as `len(list(self))`: `list()` asks for the length first, so that would call `__len__` again, for ever. `__contains__` can use `self[name] > 0`, which calls your own `__getitem__`.
--- check case | Counts add up, and missing items are 0
(lambda inv: (inv.add("bolt", 3), inv.add("bolt", 2), inv["bolt"], inv["washer"])[2:])(Inventory())
=> (5, 0)
--- check test | in is true only for a count above 0
(lambda inv: (inv.add("nut", 2), inv.add("nut", -2), inv.add("bolt", 1), "nut" not in inv and "bolt" in inv and "gear" not in inv)[3])(Inventory())
--- check case | len counts the items you actually have
(lambda inv: (inv.add("a", 1), inv.add("b", 0), inv.add("c", -1), inv.add("d", 4), len(inv))[4])(Inventory())
=> 2
--- check case | Looping gives the names A to Z
(lambda inv: (inv.add("washer", 1), inv.add("bolt", 1), inv.add("gear", 0), list(inv))[3])(Inventory())
=> ["bolt", "washer"]
--- check test | An empty inventory is false
not Inventory() and len(Inventory()) == 0

+++ practice | Durations you can add and scale
--- task
Write a class `Duration(minutes)` for lengths of time, stored as a whole number of minutes in `self.minutes`.

- `__repr__` shows hours and minutes, with the minutes always two digits: `repr(Duration(125))` is `"Duration(2h05m)"`. Use `//` and `%`, and the format spec `:02d` for the minutes.
- `+` and `-` between two durations give a new `Duration`. A result below zero raises `ValueError`. With anything that is not a `Duration`, return `NotImplemented`.
- `*` with a whole number (an `int`) gives a new `Duration`, with the number on either side. Anything else: `NotImplemented`.
- `==` is true for a `Duration` with the same minutes, and `__hash__` agrees with it.
- `bool(d)` is false only for zero minutes.

For example, `Duration(90) + Duration(45)` is `Duration(135)`, which shows as `Duration(2h15m)`.
--- starter
class Duration:
    def __init__(self, minutes):
        self.minutes = minutes

    def __add__(self, other):
        self.minutes += other.minutes
        return self
--- solution
class Duration:
    def __init__(self, minutes):
        self.minutes = minutes

    def __repr__(self):
        return f"Duration({self.minutes // 60}h{self.minutes % 60:02d}m)"

    def __add__(self, other):
        if not isinstance(other, Duration):
            return NotImplemented
        return Duration(self.minutes + other.minutes)

    def __sub__(self, other):
        if not isinstance(other, Duration):
            return NotImplemented
        if other.minutes > self.minutes:
            raise ValueError("a duration cannot be negative")
        return Duration(self.minutes - other.minutes)

    def __mul__(self, k):
        if not isinstance(k, int):
            return NotImplemented
        return Duration(self.minutes * k)

    __rmul__ = __mul__

    def __eq__(self, other):
        if not isinstance(other, Duration):
            return NotImplemented
        return self.minutes == other.minutes

    def __hash__(self):
        return hash(self.minutes)

    def __bool__(self):
        return self.minutes != 0
--- hint
Every operator builds and returns a **new** `Duration`; none of them changes `self`. The starter's `__add__` changes the left-hand duration, so `a + b` would quietly change `a`.
--- hint
`125 // 60` is 2 whole hours and `125 % 60` is the 5 minutes left over; `f"{5:02d}"` gives `"05"`. For `3 * d`, the line `__rmul__ = __mul__` under `__mul__` does it.
--- check case | repr shows hours and two-digit minutes
(repr(Duration(125)), repr(Duration(45)), repr(Duration(600)))
=> ("Duration(2h05m)", "Duration(0h45m)", "Duration(10h00m)")
--- check test | Adding makes a new Duration and leaves both alone
(lambda a, b: a + b == Duration(135) and a == Duration(90) and b == Duration(45))(Duration(90), Duration(45))
--- check test | Subtracting below zero raises ValueError
Duration(50) - Duration(20) == Duration(30) and raises(ValueError, lambda: Duration(10) - Duration(11))
--- check test | Scaling works on both sides
Duration(20) * 3 == Duration(60) and 2 * Duration(15) == Duration(30)
--- check test | Mixing with other types raises TypeError
raises(TypeError, lambda: Duration(5) + 5) and raises(TypeError, lambda: Duration(5) * 1.5)
--- check test | Equal durations count once in a set, and zero is false
len({Duration(60), Duration(60), Duration(61)}) == 2 and not Duration(0) and bool(Duration(1))

+++ practice | Money that sum() can add up
--- task
Write a frozen dataclass `Money` with two fields: `cents` (an `int`) and `currency` (a `str`, default `"EUR"`). The dataclass gives you `__eq__` and `__hash__`. Add two special methods:

- `__add__(self, other)`: if `other` is not a `Money`, return `NotImplemented`. If the currencies differ, raise `ValueError`. Otherwise return a new `Money` with the cents added up.
- `__radd__(self, other)`: `sum()` starts from `0`, so it first works out `0 + money`. The `int` gives up, and Python calls `money.__radd__(0)`. If `other == 0`, return `self`; otherwise return `NotImplemented`.

Then `sum(wallet)` adds up a list of `Money` directly. For example, `sum([Money(150), Money(75)])` is `Money(225)`.
--- starter
from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class Money:
    cents: int
    currency: str = "EUR"
--- solution
from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class Money:
    cents: int
    currency: str = "EUR"

    def __add__(self, other):
        if not isinstance(other, Money):
            return NotImplemented
        if other.currency != self.currency:
            raise ValueError("cannot add different currencies")
        return Money(self.cents + other.cents, self.currency)

    def __radd__(self, other):
        if other == 0:
            return self
        return NotImplemented
--- hint
`Money` is frozen, so `__add__` could not change `self` even if it tried: it must build `Money(self.cents + other.cents, self.currency)`.
--- hint
`__radd__` is only called when the thing on the left is not a `Money`. The one such thing you accept is the `0` that `sum()` starts from.
--- check case | Adding two amounts
Money(150) + Money(75)
=> Money(225)
--- check case | sum() adds up a wallet
sum([Money(150), Money(75), Money(5)])
=> Money(230)
--- check test | Different currencies raise ValueError
raises(ValueError, lambda: Money(1, "EUR") + Money(1, "USD"))
--- check test | Other types raise TypeError, on either side
raises(TypeError, lambda: Money(1) + 5) and raises(TypeError, lambda: 5 + Money(1))
--- check test | Still frozen and hashable
len({Money(1), Money(1), Money(1, "USD")}) == 2 and (Money(1) + Money(2)).currency == "EUR"

+++ practice | A ring that wraps around
--- task
Write a class `Ring(*items)` where the positions wrap around: after the last item comes the first again. Store the items as a tuple.

- `len(r)` is the number of items.
- `r[i]` gives the item at `i` wrapped around the length, so for a ring of 3 items, `r[3]` is `r[0]`, `r[5]` is `r[2]`, and `r[-1]` is the last item. `%` does the wrapping.
- On an **empty** ring, `r[i]` raises `IndexError` for every `i` (the obvious `%` by a length of 0 would raise `ZeroDivisionError` instead).
- A slice, like `r[0:2]`, raises `TypeError`.
- Write `__iter__` too, going once round the ring. Without it, a `for` loop or `in` would fall back on `__getitem__` with 0, 1, 2 and so on, waiting for an `IndexError` that a ring never raises, and loop for ever.

For example, with `r = Ring("a", "b", "c")`: `r[4]` is `"b"`, `list(r)` is `["a", "b", "c"]` and `"c" in r` is true.
--- starter
class Ring:
    def __init__(self, *items):
        self.items = tuple(items)
--- solution
class Ring:
    def __init__(self, *items):
        self.items = tuple(items)

    def __len__(self):
        return len(self.items)

    def __getitem__(self, index):
        if isinstance(index, slice):
            raise TypeError("a ring cannot be sliced")
        if not self.items:
            raise IndexError("the ring is empty")
        return self.items[index % len(self.items)]

    def __iter__(self):
        return iter(self.items)
--- hint
Wrapping is `self.items[index % len(self.items)]`, and `%` also turns negative positions into the right ones. That one line is the heart of `__getitem__`.
--- hint
Before that line, handle the two special cases: `isinstance(index, slice)` raises `TypeError`, and an empty tuple raises `IndexError`. `__iter__` can hand the job to the tuple: `iter(self.items)`.
--- check case | Positions wrap around
(lambda r: [r[0], r[3], r[4], r[-1], r[-4]])(Ring("a", "b", "c"))
=> ["a", "a", "b", "c", "c"]
--- check test | An empty ring raises IndexError
raises(IndexError, lambda: Ring()[0]) and raises(IndexError, lambda: Ring()[-1])
--- check test | A slice raises TypeError
raises(TypeError, lambda: Ring(1, 2, 3)[0:2])
--- check test | Looping goes round once, and in works
(lambda r: list(r) == [1, 2, 3] and 3 in r and 4 not in r and len(r) == 3)(Ring(1, 2, 3))
--- check test | An empty ring is false, a full one is true
not Ring() and bool(Ring(0))

+++ practice | Debug: the point that moved
--- task
`Point(x, y)` is a small class for positions on a map.

**The bug report:** after `c = a + b`, the point `a` has changed too: it now holds the sum. And points cannot go in a set: `{Point(1, 2)}` stops with `TypeError: unhashable type: 'Point'`.

Fix both. `a + b` must return a new point and leave `a` alone, and equal points must have equal hashes.
--- starter
class Point:
    def __init__(self, x, y):
        self.x = x
        self.y = y

    def __repr__(self):
        return f"Point({self.x}, {self.y})"

    def __add__(self, other):
        if not isinstance(other, Point):
            return NotImplemented
        self.x += other.x
        self.y += other.y
        return self

    def __eq__(self, other):
        if not isinstance(other, Point):
            return NotImplemented
        return (self.x, self.y) == (other.x, other.y)
--- solution
class Point:
    def __init__(self, x, y):
        self.x = x
        self.y = y

    def __repr__(self):
        return f"Point({self.x}, {self.y})"

    def __add__(self, other):
        if not isinstance(other, Point):
            return NotImplemented
        return Point(self.x + other.x, self.y + other.y)

    def __eq__(self, other):
        if not isinstance(other, Point):
            return NotImplemented
        return (self.x, self.y) == (other.x, other.y)

    def __hash__(self):
        return hash((self.x, self.y))
--- hint
`self.x += other.x` changes the point on the left of the `+`. Build a new `Point` from the two sums and return that instead.
--- hint
A class that defines `__eq__` loses its hash. Add `__hash__`, hashing the same data that `__eq__` compares: the tuple `(self.x, self.y)`.
--- check test | a + b leaves a alone
(lambda a, b: (a + b, a)[1] == Point(1, 2))(Point(1, 2), Point(10, 20))
--- check case | The sum is right
Point(1, 2) + Point(10, 20)
=> Point(11, 22)
--- check test | Points work in sets and as dict keys
len({Point(1, 2), Point(1, 2), Point(2, 1)}) == 2 and {Point(0, 0): "home"}[Point(0, 0)] == "home"
--- check test | Adding something that is not a point still raises TypeError
raises(TypeError, lambda: Point(1, 2) + 3)

+++ practice | Polynomials
--- task
A polynomial like 3x² + 2x + 1 can be stored as its coefficients, lowest power first: `(1, 2, 3)`. Write a class `Poly(*coeffs)` that does that.

- `__init__` removes zeros from the **end** (the highest powers), so `Poly(1, 2, 0, 0)` equals `Poly(1, 2)`, and stores the rest as a tuple in `self.coeffs`. `Poly()` and `Poly(0)` are the zero polynomial, with `coeffs == ()`.
- `__repr__`: `repr(Poly(1, 2, 3))` is `"Poly(1, 2, 3)"`, and the zero polynomial is `"Poly()"`.
- `evaluate(x)`: the value at `x`. `Poly(1, 2, 3).evaluate(2)` is `1 + 2*2 + 3*2*2`, which is `17`.
- `+` and `*` with another `Poly` or with an `int` (an `int` counts as a polynomial with one coefficient), on either side. Anything else: `NotImplemented`. For `*`, the coefficient at position `i` of one times the coefficient at position `j` of the other adds to position `i + j` of the answer.
- `==` compares coefficients (only with another `Poly`), `__hash__` agrees with it, and a zero polynomial is false.

For example, `Poly(1, 1) * Poly(-1, 1)` is `Poly(-1, 0, 1)`: (x + 1)(x - 1) is x² - 1.
--- starter
class Poly:
    def __init__(self, *coeffs):
        self.coeffs = coeffs

    def __repr__(self):
        return f"Poly{self.coeffs}"
--- solution
class Poly:
    def __init__(self, *coeffs):
        coeffs = list(coeffs)
        while coeffs and coeffs[-1] == 0:
            coeffs = coeffs[:-1]
        self.coeffs = tuple(coeffs)

    def __repr__(self):
        return f"Poly({', '.join(repr(c) for c in self.coeffs)})"

    def evaluate(self, x):
        total = 0
        for c in reversed(self.coeffs):
            total = total * x + c
        return total

    def _as_poly(self, other):
        if isinstance(other, int):
            return Poly(other)
        if isinstance(other, Poly):
            return other
        return None

    def __add__(self, other):
        other = self._as_poly(other)
        if other is None:
            return NotImplemented
        size = max(len(self.coeffs), len(other.coeffs))
        a = self.coeffs + (0,) * (size - len(self.coeffs))
        b = other.coeffs + (0,) * (size - len(other.coeffs))
        return Poly(*(x + y for x, y in zip(a, b)))

    __radd__ = __add__

    def __mul__(self, other):
        other = self._as_poly(other)
        if other is None:
            return NotImplemented
        if not self.coeffs or not other.coeffs:
            return Poly()
        out = [0] * (len(self.coeffs) + len(other.coeffs) - 1)
        for i, x in enumerate(self.coeffs):
            for j, y in enumerate(other.coeffs):
                out[i + j] += x * y
        return Poly(*out)

    __rmul__ = __mul__

    def __eq__(self, other):
        if not isinstance(other, Poly):
            return NotImplemented
        return self.coeffs == other.coeffs

    def __hash__(self):
        return hash(self.coeffs)

    def __bool__(self):
        return bool(self.coeffs)
--- hint
Trim the zeros in a loop: while the list is not empty and its last item is 0, drop the last item (`coeffs[:-1]` is everything but the last). Every operation builds its answer with `Poly(...)`, so the trimming then happens everywhere for free.
--- hint
For `+`, pad the shorter tuple with zeros so both are the same length (`(0,) * n` is a tuple of `n` zeros), then add them pair by pair with `zip`. A small helper method that turns an `int` into a `Poly`, and gives `None` for anything else, saves writing that check twice.
--- hint
For `*`, start with a list of zeros one shorter than the two lengths added together, then two nested loops with `enumerate`: `out[i + j] += x * y`. `evaluate` can go from the highest coefficient down: `total = total * x + c`.
--- check case | Trailing zeros are removed
(Poly(1, 2, 0, 0), Poly(0), Poly())
=> (Poly(1, 2), Poly(), Poly())
--- check case | repr
(repr(Poly(1, 2, 3)), repr(Poly(0, 0)), repr(Poly(-4)))
=> ("Poly(1, 2, 3)", "Poly()", "Poly(-4)")
--- check test | evaluate
Poly(1, 2, 3).evaluate(2) == 17 and Poly().evaluate(5) == 0 and Poly(0, 0, 1).evaluate(-3) == 9
--- check test | Adding, with cancelling at the top
Poly(1, -1) + Poly(0, 1) == Poly(1) and Poly(1, 2) + Poly(0, 0, 5) == Poly(1, 2, 5) and 3 + Poly(1, 1) == Poly(4, 1)
--- check test | Multiplying
Poly(1, 1) * Poly(-1, 1) == Poly(-1, 0, 1) and Poly(1, 1) * Poly(1, 1) == Poly(1, 2, 1) and 2 * Poly(1, 2) == Poly(2, 4) and Poly(5) * Poly() == Poly()
--- check test | Other types raise TypeError
raises(TypeError, lambda: Poly(1) + "x") and raises(TypeError, lambda: Poly(1) * 1.5)
--- check test | Hashing agrees with ==, and zero is false
len({Poly(1, 0), Poly(1), Poly(0, 1)}) == 2 and not Poly(0) and bool(Poly(0, 1))

=== py3-08 | functools: partial, reduce and lru_cache
--- teach
Last lesson you gave your own `Vector` class special methods, so that `+`, `len` and `abs` worked on it like on a built-in type. A few lessons before that, in the decorators lesson, you saw that a function is a value: you can hand it to another function and get a new function back. This lesson opens a drawer of the standard library built for exactly that kind of work. It is called `functools`, short for "function tools", and it is full of [[functions that work on other functions|higher-order]].

You will meet three of its tools, one at a time:

- `partial`, which fills in some of a function's arguments ahead of time,
- `reduce`, which boils a whole list down to one value,
- `lru_cache`, which lets a function remember answers it has already worked out.

You bring them in the usual way, with an import at the top of the file:

```python
from functools import partial, reduce, lru_cache
```

### partial: fill in some arguments now

Think of the speed-dial button on a phone. The number is already stored in it, so you press one button instead of typing ten digits. **`partial`** does that for a function. It takes a function and some of its arguments, and it gives you back a **new function** with those arguments already filled in. You give the rest later, when you call it.

Here is a small function to practice on. `**` means "to the power of", so `base ** exp` is `base` multiplied by itself `exp` times:

```python
def power(base, exp):
    return base ** exp

power(9, 2)        # 81   (9 times 9)
```

Now fill in `exp` ahead of time:

```python
square = partial(power, exp=2)
square(9)          # 81
square(5)          # 25
```

`square` is a new function. When you call `square(9)`, it calls `power(9, exp=2)` for you. The original `power` does not change.

### partial with positional arguments

You can also fill in arguments by position, with no name. Those fill the parameters from the left, so they become the *first* arguments. Python has a built-in function `pow` that works like our `power`: `pow(10, 3)` is 10 to the power of 3, which is 1000. Filling in its first argument fixes the base:

```python
ten_to_the = partial(pow, 10)
ten_to_the(3)      # 1000   same as pow(10, 3)
ten_to_the(2)      # 100
```

One more example, with a function you know well. `int("101")` turns text into the number one hundred and one. With the setting `base=2`, `int` reads the text as a [[binary number|binary]] instead:

```python
int_from_binary = partial(int, base=2)
int_from_binary("101")   # 5
```

`partial` is handy wherever a tool wants a function of **one** argument and yours takes more. In the sorting lesson, `key=` wanted exactly that, and a callback, a function handed over to be called later, often does too.

**Watch out:** positional values fill parameters from the left. `partial(power, 2)` fixes `base`, not `exp`, so it makes "2 to the power of something", not "something squared". When in doubt, fill in by name, as in `partial(power, exp=2)`.

### reduce: boil a list down to one value

Picture adding up a shopping receipt on a calculator. You start at 0. You add the first price and look at the running total. You add the next price to that total, and the next, until the receipt ends. The last running total is the answer.

**`reduce`** does exactly that. It takes three things: a function that combines two values, a sequence, and a **start value**. It keeps a running result, and for each item it calls the function with the running result and the item. Whatever the function hands back becomes the new running result. This is also called [[folding|fold]] a list.

The combining function takes **two** arguments. You met lambdas with one parameter in the sorting lesson; a lambda can have two, separated by a comma. Read `lambda acc, x: acc + x` as "given `acc` and `x`, hand back `acc + x`". The name `acc` is short for [[accumulator|accumulator]], the running result.

```python
reduce(lambda acc, x: acc + x, [1, 2, 3, 4], 0)    # 10
```

Step by step:

| Step | acc (running result) | x (next item) | acc + x |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 0 (the start value) | 1 | 1 |
| 2 | 1 | 2 | 3 |
| 3 | 3 | 3 | 6 |
| 4 | 6 | 4 | 10 |

That is `((((0 + 1) + 2) + 3) + 4)`, which is 10. The list has ended, so `reduce` hands back the last running result.

With `*` (times) instead of `+` you get a product. Start at 1, because multiplying by 1 changes nothing, the way adding 0 changes nothing:

```python
reduce(lambda acc, x: acc * x, [2, 3, 4], 1)    # 24   (2 times 3 times 4)
reduce(lambda acc, x: acc * x, [], 1)           # 1    empty list: you get the start value
```

**Always give a start value.** Without one, `reduce` uses the first item as the start, and on an empty list it has nothing to start from:

```python
reduce(lambda acc, x: acc + x, [])
# TypeError: reduce() of empty iterable with no initial value
```

For plain sums and products, `sum()` and `math.prod()` from the standard library lesson are easier to read. `reduce` earns its place when the combining step is your own, like the next one.

**Watch out:** the combining function always gets the running result *first* and the next item *second*. Mix them up and you get wrong answers, often with no error at all.

### reduce with functions: chaining them together

Here is where `reduce` shines. Functions are values, so a function can hand back a new function. This lambda takes two functions, `f` and `g`, and hands back a new function that runs `g` first and then `f` on the result:

```python
combine = lambda f, g: lambda x: f(g(x))

add_one = lambda x: x + 1
square = lambda x: x * x

both = combine(add_one, square)
both(3)          # 10   add_one(square(3)) = add_one(9) = 10
```

Read `f(g(x))` from the inside out: `g` runs first, `f` second. So the function written on the **right** runs first. Putting functions inside each other like this is called [[composing|compose-word]] them.

To chain a whole list of functions, `reduce` with `combine`. The start value is a function that hands back whatever it is given, `lambda x: x`, called the **[[identity function|identity]]**:

```python
chain = reduce(combine, [add_one, square], lambda x: x)
chain(3)         # 10   add_one(square(3))
```

Step by step: first `combine(identity, add_one)` makes "add one". Then `combine` of that with `square` makes "square, then add one". So the rightmost function in the list runs first, and the leftmost runs last. With an empty list you get back the start value, the identity function, which returns its argument unchanged.

### lru_cache: remember answers

Think of a times table you have learned by heart. Nobody works out 7 times 8 from scratch every time; you remember 56. **`lru_cache`** gives a function that kind of memory. It is a decorator, like the ones you wrote in the decorators lesson. It takes a setting, so it is written with brackets, like `@repeat(3)` was. It stores every answer, filed under the arguments that produced it. Call the function again with the same arguments, and you get the stored answer at once, without running the body.

```python
from functools import lru_cache

@lru_cache(maxsize=None)
def slow_square(n):
    print("computing", n)
    return n * n

slow_square(4)   # prints "computing 4", returns 16
slow_square(4)   # returns 16 at once, nothing printed
slow_square.cache_info()   # CacheInfo(hits=1, misses=1, maxsize=None, currsize=1)
```

A **cache** is a store of answers kept for later. `cache_info()` reports how it is doing:

- **hits**: calls answered from the store (the second call),
- **misses**: calls that had to run the body (the first call),
- **maxsize**: how many answers it may keep; `None` means no limit (the [[LRU in the name|lru]] is about what happens when there is a limit),
- **currsize**: how many answers it holds now.

This only makes sense for a [[pure function|pure]], one whose answer depends only on its arguments. Cache a function that reads the clock or a file, and it will keep handing back an old answer after the real one has changed.

**Watch out:** the arguments must be hashable, the same rule as for dict keys and set items. Numbers, strings and tuples are fine. A list is not: `slow_square([1])` stops with `TypeError: unhashable type: 'list'`.

### A cached function that calls itself

A function is allowed to call **itself** on a smaller version of the same problem. This is called **recursion**. It needs a stopping case, where it answers without calling itself again. Here is a function that counts how many times you can halve a number before you reach 1. `//` is whole-number division, so `8 // 2` is 4:

```python
@lru_cache(maxsize=None)
def halvings(n):
    if n == 1:
        return 0                    # the stopping case: already at 1
    return 1 + halvings(n // 2)     # one halving, plus the halvings of what is left

halvings(8)      # 3   (8 -> 4 -> 2 -> 1)
halvings.cache_info()    # CacheInfo(hits=0, misses=4, maxsize=None, currsize=4)
halvings(16)     # 4
halvings.cache_info()    # CacheInfo(hits=1, misses=5, maxsize=None, currsize=5)
```

`halvings(8)` asked for 8, 4, 2 and 1: four misses. Then `halvings(16)` ran its body once, asked for `halvings(8)`, and got it straight from the store: one hit. For a function that calls itself many times on the same numbers, those hits save a lot of work. Your task uses this on [[a famous number puzzle|collatz]]. (A later lesson goes much deeper into recursion.)

::: context higher-order Functions that handle functions
A function that takes a function as an argument, or hands one back, is called a higher-order function. You have used several already: `sorted` takes a `key=` function, and every decorator takes a function and returns a new one. They are useful because they let you write the *pattern* once ("do this to each item", "remember the answers") and plug in the details as a function. Many languages have them, and much of `functools` is higher-order functions.
:::

::: context binary Counting in twos
We count in tens: each place in a number is worth ten times the one to its right. Binary counts in twos: the places are worth 1, 2, 4, 8 and so on, and each digit is 0 or 1. So "101" in binary is one 4, no 2s and one 1, which is 5. Computers store everything in binary. Spacecraft telemetry often arrives as raw bits, and code that unpacks it uses exactly this kind of conversion; `int(text, base=16)` does the same for hexadecimal, which counts in sixteens.
:::

::: context fold Folding, step by step

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 120" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="10" y="44" width="40" height="30" rx="4" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="30" y="64" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">0</text>
  <text x="30" y="100" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">start</text>
  <line x1="50" y1="59" x2="88" y2="59" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="69" y="36" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">+1</text>
  <rect x="88" y="44" width="40" height="30" rx="4" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="108" y="64" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">1</text>
  <line x1="128" y1="59" x2="166" y2="59" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="147" y="36" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">+2</text>
  <rect x="166" y="44" width="40" height="30" rx="4" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="186" y="64" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">3</text>
  <line x1="206" y1="59" x2="244" y2="59" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="225" y="36" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">+3</text>
  <rect x="244" y="44" width="40" height="30" rx="4" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="264" y="64" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">6</text>
  <line x1="284" y1="59" x2="310" y2="59" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="297" y="36" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">+4</text>
  <rect x="310" y="44" width="40" height="30" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="330" y="64" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">10</text>
  <text x="330" y="100" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">result</text>
</svg>
```

The running result moves left to right, taking in one item at each step. Other languages call this operation "fold". In older Python 2, `reduce` was a built-in; Python 3 moved it into `functools`, partly because a plain loop or `sum` is often easier to read.
:::

::: context accumulator The running result
To accumulate means to pile up, like snow on a rolling snowball. An accumulator is the variable that holds the pile so far. You have written them before without the name: `total = 0` before a loop, then `total += x` inside it. `reduce` is that same loop, packed into one call: the start value is `total = 0`, and the combining function is the `total + x` step.
:::

::: context compose-word Composing functions
In maths, putting one function inside another is called composition, and it is often written f ∘ g, read "f after g". It means "do g, then do f to the result". The order surprises people at first, because you read f first but it runs last. Programmers compose small functions to build a pipeline, the same idea as the generator pipelines from earlier in this course.
:::

::: context identity A function that does nothing, on purpose
`lambda x: x` hands back exactly what it was given. That sounds useless, but it plays the same part for functions that 0 plays for adding and 1 plays for multiplying: combining with it changes nothing. That makes it the right start value for chaining functions, and it is also the right answer when there are no functions to chain at all.
:::

::: context lru Least recently used
LRU stands for "least recently used". If you give the cache a limit, say `@lru_cache(maxsize=100)`, and it is full when a new answer arrives, it throws away the answer that has gone unused the longest. That keeps memory in check while holding on to the answers you keep asking for. With no brackets at all, `@lru_cache` uses a limit of 128. Since Python 3.9, `functools.cache` is a short name for `lru_cache(maxsize=None)`.
:::

::: context pure Pure functions
A pure function works like a formula: the same inputs always give the same output, and it changes nothing outside itself. `n * n` is pure. A function that reads a sensor, the time or a file is not, because its answer can change while its arguments stay the same. Engineers like pure functions for more than caching: they are the easiest code to test, because a test only has to check inputs against outputs.
:::

::: context collatz The Collatz puzzle
Start from any whole number. If it is even, halve it. If it is odd, multiply by 3 and add 1. Repeat. The mathematician Lothar Collatz asked in 1937 whether every starting number reaches 1 in the end. Computers have checked an enormous range of starting numbers and all of them get there, yet nobody has proved it is always true. The chains wander: 27 takes 111 steps and climbs past 9,000 before it comes down. Many chains pass through the same numbers, which is exactly why a cache helps.
:::
--- task
Write these four, using the tools from this lesson. The starter already imports `lru_cache`, `partial` and `reduce`.

1. `product(nums)`: multiplies all the numbers in the list together, using `reduce`. `product([2, 3, 4])` is `24`, and `product([])` is `1`.
2. `compose(*funcs)`: takes any number of functions and returns **one** function that applies them **right to left**, so `compose(f, g, h)(x)` is `f(g(h(x)))`: `h` runs first and `f` runs last. `compose()` with no functions returns a function that hands back its argument unchanged. Use `reduce`.
3. `two_to_the`: a function made with `partial` from the built-in `pow`, so that `two_to_the(10)` is `1024`. This one is a variable, not a `def`: write `two_to_the = ...`.
4. `collatz_steps(n)`, decorated with `@lru_cache(maxsize=None)`. It returns `0` if `n` is 1. Otherwise it returns `1 +` the steps of the next number, where the next number is `n // 2` if `n` is even, or `3 * n + 1` if `n` is odd.
--- starter
from functools import lru_cache, partial, reduce


def product(nums):
    return 0


def compose(*funcs):
    return funcs[0]


def collatz_steps(n):
    return 0
--- solution
from functools import lru_cache, partial, reduce


def product(nums):
    return reduce(lambda acc, x: acc * x, nums, 1)


def compose(*funcs):
    return reduce(lambda f, g: lambda x: f(g(x)), funcs, lambda x: x)


two_to_the = partial(pow, 2)


@lru_cache(maxsize=None)
def collatz_steps(n):
    if n == 1:
        return 0
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return 1 + collatz_steps(n // 2)
    return 1 + collatz_steps(3 * n + 1)
--- hint
`product` is the times version of the receipt example: `reduce(lambda acc, x: acc * x, nums, 1)`. The last argument, `1`, is the start value, and it is what an empty list gives back.
--- hint
For `compose`, do what the "chaining" example did, inside the function: `reduce` over `funcs` with the combining lambda `lambda f, g: lambda x: f(g(x))` and the start value `lambda x: x` (the identity function). Return what `reduce` hands back.
--- hint
`two_to_the = partial(pow, 2)` fixes the first argument of `pow`, the base. For `collatz_steps`, put `@lru_cache(maxsize=None)` on the line directly above `def collatz_steps(n):`. Inside: if `n == 1`, return `0`. If `n % 2 == 0` (the remainder after dividing by 2 is 0, so `n` is even), return `1 + collatz_steps(n // 2)`. Otherwise return `1 + collatz_steps(3 * n + 1)`.
--- check test | product, including the empty case
product([2, 3, 4]) == 24 and product([]) == 1 and product([-1, 5]) == -5
--- check case | compose applies right to left
compose(lambda x: x + 1, lambda x: x * 2)(5)
=> 11
--- check case | compose works with any number of functions
compose(str, abs, lambda x: x - 10)(3)
=> "7"
--- check case | compose() is the identity
compose()("same")
=> "same"
--- check test | two_to_the is a partial of pow
two_to_the(10) == 1024 and isinstance(two_to_the, __import__("functools").partial)
--- check test | collatz_steps small values
[collatz_steps(n) for n in (1, 2, 6, 27)] == [0, 1, 8, 111]
--- check case | The longest chain below 30,000 (the cache makes this fast)
max(range(1, 30000), key=collatz_steps)
=> 26623
--- check test | collatz_steps is cached with lru_cache
collatz_steps.cache_info().hits > 0
?? Put @lru_cache(maxsize=None) on collatz_steps.
--- check source | Uses reduce
\breduce\(

+++ practice | Converters made with partial
--- task
The starter has a function `scale(value, factor, offset=0)`, which returns `value * factor + offset`. Make three new functions with `partial`. Each one is a variable, not a `def`:

- `km_to_miles`: `scale` with `factor=0.621371`, so `km_to_miles(100)` is about `62.1371`.
- `c_to_f`: `scale` with `factor=1.8` and `offset=32`, so `c_to_f(100)` is `212.0`.
- `parse_hex`: the built-in `int` with `base=16`, so `parse_hex("ff")` is `255`.

Fill the settings in by name, as in `partial(power, exp=2)`.
--- starter
from functools import partial


def scale(value, factor, offset=0):
    return value * factor + offset


km_to_miles = scale
--- solution
from functools import partial


def scale(value, factor, offset=0):
    return value * factor + offset


km_to_miles = partial(scale, factor=0.621371)
c_to_f = partial(scale, factor=1.8, offset=32)
parse_hex = partial(int, base=16)
--- hint
`partial(scale, factor=0.621371)` makes a new function that calls `scale(value, factor=0.621371)` with whatever value you give it.
--- hint
`partial` can fill in more than one setting at once: `partial(scale, factor=1.8, offset=32)`. The third one is made from `int`, the same way the lesson made `int_from_binary`.
--- check test | km_to_miles
round(km_to_miles(100), 4) == 62.1371 and km_to_miles(0) == 0
--- check test | c_to_f
c_to_f(100) == 212.0 and c_to_f(-40) == -40.0 and round(c_to_f(37), 1) == 98.6
--- check test | parse_hex
parse_hex("ff") == 255 and parse_hex("10") == 16 and parse_hex("0") == 0
--- check test | All three are made with partial
all(isinstance(f, __import__("functools").partial) for f in (km_to_miles, c_to_f, parse_hex))
?? Make each one with partial(...), not with def or lambda.

+++ practice | Digits into a number
--- task
Write `to_number(digits, base=10)`. It turns a list of digits into the number they spell, using `reduce`: `to_number([4, 0, 2])` is `402`.

Each step shifts the running result one place to the left and adds the next digit: `acc * base + digit`. Start from `0`, so an empty list gives `0`. The same step works in any base: `to_number([1, 0, 1], 2)` is `5`.
--- starter
from functools import reduce


def to_number(digits, base=10):
    return sum(digits)
--- solution
from functools import reduce


def to_number(digits, base=10):
    return reduce(lambda acc, digit: acc * base + digit, digits, 0)
--- hint
Walk it by hand for `[4, 0, 2]`: start at 0, then 0 × 10 + 4 = 4, then 4 × 10 + 0 = 40, then 40 × 10 + 2 = 402. The combining step is `acc * base + digit`.
--- hint
`reduce(lambda acc, digit: acc * base + digit, digits, 0)`. The running result comes first in the lambda, and the start value `0` comes last in the call.
--- check case | Base 10
to_number([4, 0, 2])
=> 402
--- check case | Base 2
to_number([1, 0, 1], 2)
=> 5
--- check case | An empty list is 0
to_number([])
=> 0
--- check case | Leading zeros do nothing
to_number([0, 0, 7, 1])
=> 71
--- check source | Uses reduce
\breduce\(

+++ practice | Sort by distance from a point
--- task
Write two functions.

- `distance(a, b)`: the straight-line distance between two points, each a tuple `(x, y)`. Use `math.hypot` on the differences in `x` and in `y`.
- `by_distance(points, origin)`: returns a new list of the points, sorted from nearest to `origin` to furthest. Use `sorted` with `key=partial(distance, origin)`. Points at the same distance keep their original order.

For example, `by_distance([(3, 4), (1, 0), (0, 2)], (0, 0))` is `[(1, 0), (0, 2), (3, 4)]`.
--- starter
import math
from functools import partial


def distance(a, b):
    return 0


def by_distance(points, origin):
    return sorted(points)
--- solution
import math
from functools import partial


def distance(a, b):
    return math.hypot(b[0] - a[0], b[1] - a[1])


def by_distance(points, origin):
    return sorted(points, key=partial(distance, origin))
--- hint
`key=` wants a function of **one** argument, the point being sorted, but `distance` takes two. `partial(distance, origin)` fills in the first one, so what is left takes just the point.
--- hint
`distance` is `math.hypot(b[0] - a[0], b[1] - a[1])`. `sorted` is stable, so ties keep their order without any extra work.
--- check case | Nearest first
by_distance([(3, 4), (1, 0), (0, 2)], (0, 0))
=> [(1, 0), (0, 2), (3, 4)]
--- check case | From a different origin
by_distance([(0, 0), (10, 10), (6, 5)], (5, 5))
=> [(6, 5), (0, 0), (10, 10)]
--- check case | Ties keep their original order
by_distance([(0, 1), (1, 0), (-1, 0)], (0, 0))
=> [(0, 1), (1, 0), (-1, 0)]
--- check test | distance, and nothing to sort
distance((1, 1), (4, 5)) == 5.0 and by_distance([], (0, 0)) == []
--- check source | Uses partial
\bpartial\(

+++ practice | Climbing stairs, with a list of steps
--- task
Write `ways_to_climb(n, steps)`: the number of different sequences of steps that climb exactly `n` stairs, where each step is one of the sizes in the list `steps`. Order matters: with steps `[1, 2]`, going 1 then 2 and going 2 then 1 are two ways to climb 3.

- Climbing 0 stairs has exactly one way: do nothing. A negative number of stairs has none.
- Otherwise, the ways to climb `n` are the ways to climb `n - s`, added up over every step size `s`.

It must be fast for `n = 100`, so cache it with `lru_cache`. But `steps` is a list, and a cached function cannot take a list. So write a helper `_ways(n, steps)` with `@lru_cache(maxsize=None)` that takes a **tuple**, and make `ways_to_climb` call it with `tuple(steps)`.

For example, `ways_to_climb(4, [1, 2])` is `5`, and `ways_to_climb(3, [])` is `0`.
--- starter
from functools import lru_cache


@lru_cache(maxsize=None)
def ways_to_climb(n, steps):
    return 0
--- solution
from functools import lru_cache


@lru_cache(maxsize=None)
def _ways(n, steps):
    if n == 0:
        return 1
    if n < 0:
        return 0
    return sum(_ways(n - s, steps) for s in steps)


def ways_to_climb(n, steps):
    return _ways(n, tuple(steps))
--- hint
The helper has two stopping cases, `n == 0` (one way) and `n < 0` (no ways), and one line that calls itself: `sum(_ways(n - s, steps) for s in steps)`.
--- hint
Only the helper is cached. `ways_to_climb` does nothing but turn the list into a tuple: `return _ways(n, tuple(steps))`. A tuple is hashable, so the cache can file answers under it.
--- check case | Four stairs, steps of 1 or 2
ways_to_climb(4, [1, 2])
=> 5
--- check case | Zero stairs, one way; no steps, no ways
(ways_to_climb(0, [1, 2]), ways_to_climb(3, []), ways_to_climb(0, []))
=> (1, 0, 1)
--- check case | Some heights cannot be reached
(ways_to_climb(5, [2]), ways_to_climb(10, [2, 5]))
=> (0, 2)
--- check case | 100 stairs with steps of 1, 2 or 3 (fast only with a cache)
ways_to_climb(100, [1, 2, 3])
=> 180396380815100901214157639
--- check test | Taking a list works
ways_to_climb(3, [1, 2]) == 3

+++ practice | Debug: the cube that squared
--- task
Two small functions built with `functools` give wrong answers, with no error.

- `cube` should raise a number to the power of 3: `cube(2)` should be `8`. It gives `9`.
- `sentence(words)` should join the words with spaces, in order: `sentence(["go", "for", "launch"])` should be `"go for launch"`. It gives `"launch for go"`.

Fix both. Each fix is in one line.
--- starter
from functools import partial, reduce


def power(base, exp):
    return base ** exp


cube = partial(power, 3)


def sentence(words):
    return reduce(lambda acc, word: word + " " + acc, words, "").strip()
--- solution
from functools import partial, reduce


def power(base, exp):
    return base ** exp


cube = partial(power, exp=3)


def sentence(words):
    return reduce(lambda acc, word: acc + " " + word, words, "").strip()
--- hint
`partial(power, 3)` fills parameters from the left. Which parameter of `power` got the 3?
--- hint
Fill `exp` by name: `partial(power, exp=3)`. In `sentence`, the running result is `acc`, the text so far, so the next word belongs after it: `acc + " " + word`.
--- check case | cube(2)
cube(2)
=> 8
--- check case | cube of a negative number
cube(-3)
=> -27
--- check case | Words stay in order
sentence(["go", "for", "launch"])
=> "go for launch"
--- check case | One word, and no words
(sentence(["hold"]), sentence([]))
=> ("hold", "")

+++ practice | Splitting text into words
--- task
Some writing systems, and some squashed log formats, have no spaces between words. Write `count_splits(text, words)`: the number of ways to cut `text` into pieces that are all in the list `words`. A word can be used any number of times.

- An empty `text` has exactly one way: no pieces at all.
- Otherwise, try every word that `text` **starts with** at the current position (compare a slice, `text[i:i + len(word)] == word`), and add up the ways to split what is left after it.

Inside `count_splits`, define a helper `ways(i)`, the number of ways to split `text` from position `i` to the end, and decorate it with `@lru_cache(maxsize=None)`. It can use `text` and `words` from the outer function, as a closure. Return `ways(0)`. It must be fast on 60 letters.

For example, `count_splits("catsanddog", ["cat", "cats", "and", "sand", "dog"])` is `2`: "cats and dog" and "cat sand dog".
--- starter
from functools import lru_cache


def count_splits(text, words):
    return 0
--- solution
from functools import lru_cache


def count_splits(text, words):
    @lru_cache(maxsize=None)
    def ways(i):
        if i == len(text):
            return 1
        total = 0
        for word in words:
            if word and text[i:i + len(word)] == word:
                total += ways(i + len(word))
        return total

    return ways(0)
--- hint
Think from the position `i`: if `i` is already at the end of the text, that is one complete split. Otherwise, each word that matches at `i` leads to a smaller problem starting at `i + len(word)`.
--- hint
The helper only needs `i` as its argument; `text` and `words` come from `count_splits` itself. Many different cuts reach the same position, which is why the cache turns a hopeless search into a quick one. Skip an empty word, which would never move `i` forward.
--- check case | Two ways
count_splits("catsanddog", ["cat", "cats", "and", "sand", "dog"])
=> 2
--- check case | Three ways
count_splits("pineapplepenapple", ["apple", "pen", "applepen", "pine", "pineapple"])
=> 3
--- check case | No way at all
count_splits("catsandog", ["cats", "dog", "sand", "and", "cat"])
=> 0
--- check case | Empty text has one way
count_splits("", ["a"])
=> 1
--- check case | Sixty letters (fast only with the cache)
count_splits("a" * 60, ["a", "aa"])
=> 2504730781961

=== py3-09 | Debugging: aliasing and shared state
--- teach
Last lesson `lru_cache` refused lists as arguments, because a list can change after it has been stored. This lesson is a bug hunt about that same fact: what goes wrong when something that can change is shared by two names. You met the start of this story in the intermediate course, in the mutable default argument bug. Here you will see the same cause hiding in three new places, and learn the tools that fix it for good.

### Names are labels, not boxes

It is tempting to picture a variable as a box with a value inside. In Python that picture is wrong, and the wrong picture causes the bugs in this lesson. A variable is a **name tied to an object**, like a luggage label tied to a suitcase. The name `a` is not the list; it is a label pointing at the list. The pointing is called a [[reference|reference]].

Two labels can be tied to the same suitcase. Then anything you put in the suitcase through one label shows up when you open it through the other. Two names for one object is called **aliasing**. An alias is a second name for the same thing, like a nickname.

```python
a = [1, 2]
b = a          # no copy: b is another name for the same list
b.append(3)
a              # [1, 2, 3]   changed, though you only touched b
a is b         # True        the very same object
```

`b = a` never makes a copy. It ties a second label to the list that `a` already points at.

### The debugging tool: is

When two things change together that should not, ask: **are they the same object?** You met the tools in the mutable default lesson. `a is b` asks "are these the very same object?", and `id(a) == id(b)` asks the same thing with the object's identity number. `==` does not help here, because it only asks whether the values are equal, and a copy has equal values too.

### Where aliasing hides: multiplying a list of lists

`[0] * 3` makes a list holding three zeros: `*` on a list means "repeat the contents this many times". That works well:

```python
row = [0] * 3             # [0, 0, 0]
```

Now try to build a grid of 2 rows by repeating a row:

