::: context memo-word A memo to yourself
The word comes from "memo", a note you write so you do not have to remember something. The computer scientist Donald Michie named "memo functions" in 1968: functions that keep a note of every answer they have given. British books usually spell it memoisation and American ones memoization; both mean the same thing. It is not "memorisation", although that is a fair way to picture it.
:::

::: context dp-name A name chosen to sound impressive
Richard Bellman worked out dynamic programming at the RAND Corporation in the 1950s. He later wrote that he picked the name partly because it sounded impressive and hard to object to, not because it describes the idea well. "Programming" here means planning, as in a TV programme schedule, not writing code. Bellman's ideas grew into optimal control, the branch of engineering that works out the best way to steer a system, and they still sit underneath methods used to plan rocket and spacecraft trajectories.
:::

::: context greedy Grab the best thing now
A greedy method makes the choice that looks best right now and never goes back to reconsider. It is fast and sometimes perfect: with US coins (1, 5, 10 and 25 cents), taking the biggest coin that fits always gives the fewest coins. But for coins like 1, 3 and 4 it goes wrong, because the best first step depends on what comes after. DP looks at every choice for every smaller amount, so it cannot be fooled that way.
:::

::: context loop-order Why the loop order changes the count
With amounts on the outside, every amount tries every coin as its *last* coin. So 3 can end with a 2 (after making 1, as 1 + 2) or end with a 1 (after making 2, which includes 2 + 1): the two orders become two separate ways. With coins on the outside, the 2-coins are only added after all the 1-coins have been placed, so a combination is only ever built as "all the 1s, then all the 2s". Each combination is counted once. Counting orders has its own use, for example counting the different sequences of steps up a staircase, so neither loop is wrong in general. Only one of them answers this question.
:::
--- task
Solve two coin problems with dynamic programming, filling a table from the bottom up. Both must handle an amount of 10,000 quickly. You have an unlimited supply of each coin.

- `min_coins(coins, amount)` returns the fewest coins that add up to `amount`, or `-1` if it cannot be done. `min_coins(coins, 0)` is `0`. Replace the starter's greedy version: make a list `best` that starts as `0` followed by `amount` copies of `float("inf")`, fill it with the two loops from the lesson, and return `best[amount]`, or `-1` if that is still infinity.
- `count_ways(coins, amount)` returns how many different **combinations** of coins add up to `amount` (order does not matter, so `1+2` and `2+1` are one way). There is exactly one way to make `0`. Make a list `ways` that starts as `1` followed by `amount` zeros, loop over the **coins on the outside**, and return `ways[amount]`.
--- starter
def min_coins(coins, amount):
    count = 0
    for coin in sorted(coins, reverse=True):
        while amount >= coin:
            amount -= coin
            count += 1
    return count if amount == 0 else -1


def count_ways(coins, amount):
    return 0
--- solution
def min_coins(coins, amount):
    INF = float("inf")
    best = [0] + [INF] * amount
    for a in range(1, amount + 1):
        for c in coins:
            if c <= a and best[a - c] + 1 < best[a]:
                best[a] = best[a - c] + 1
    return best[amount] if best[amount] != INF else -1


def count_ways(coins, amount):
    ways = [1] + [0] * amount
    for c in coins:
        for a in range(c, amount + 1):
            ways[a] += ways[a - c]
    return ways[amount]
--- hint
The starter's greedy `min_coins` gets coins `[1, 3, 4]` and amount 6 wrong: it answers 3, but 3 + 3 is 2 coins. Build the table `best`, where `best[a]` is the fewest coins for amount `a`: `best = [0] + [float("inf")] * amount`.
--- hint
For each amount `a` from 1 to `amount` (`range(1, amount + 1)`), try every coin `c`: if `c <= a` and `best[a - c] + 1 < best[a]`, set `best[a] = best[a - c] + 1`. At the end, return `-1` if `best[amount]` is still `float("inf")`, otherwise return `best[amount]`.
--- hint
For `count_ways`: `ways = [1] + [0] * amount`. Then `for c in coins:` on the outside, and inside it `for a in range(c, amount + 1):` doing `ways[a] += ways[a - c]`. Return `ways[amount]`.
--- check case | min_coins, the usual case
min_coins([1, 2, 5], 11)
=> 3
--- check case | Greedy is wrong here, DP is not
min_coins([1, 3, 4], 6)
=> 2
?? Taking the biggest coin first gives 4+1+1. Try every coin at every amount.
--- check case | Impossible gives -1
min_coins([2], 3)
=> -1
--- check case | Zero needs zero coins
min_coins([1, 5], 0)
=> 0
--- check case | A big amount
min_coins([1, 7, 23, 97, 331], 10000)
=> 34
--- check case | A big amount with no 1-coin
min_coins([7, 23, 97, 331], 9999)
=> 33
--- check case | count_ways counts combinations, not orders
count_ways([1, 2, 5], 5)
=> 4
--- check case | count_ways with several coins
count_ways([2, 5, 3, 6], 10)
=> 5
--- check test | One way to make zero, none when it cannot be done
count_ways([1, 2], 0) == 1 and count_ways([7], 3) == 0
--- check case | count_ways for 10,000 with eight coins
count_ways([1, 2, 5, 10, 20, 50, 100, 200], 10000)
=> 1133873304647601

+++ practice | Flatten nested lists
--- task
Write a recursive function `flatten(items)`. `items` is a list whose items can be lists themselves, nested to any depth. Return one flat list of all the non-list items, in order, from left to right.

- Base case: an item that is not a list goes straight into the result.
- Recursive case: an item that is a list (`isinstance(item, list)`) is flattened by calling `flatten` on it, and everything that comes back joins the result.

Strings are not lists, so they stay whole. For example, `flatten([1, [2, [3, "ab"]], [], 4])` is `[1, 2, 3, "ab", 4]`.
--- starter
def flatten(items):
    result = []
    for item in items:
        if isinstance(item, list):
            result = result + item
        else:
            result.append(item)
    return result
--- solution
def flatten(items):
    result = []
    for item in items:
        if isinstance(item, list):
            result = result + flatten(item)
        else:
            result.append(item)
    return result
--- hint
The starter handles one level of nesting: a list inside a list inside a list comes out still nested. Which call would flatten the inner list, whatever is inside it?
--- hint
Instead of joining `item` onto the result, join `flatten(item)`. Each call makes its list smaller in depth, and an empty list simply adds nothing.
--- check case | Nested to several levels
flatten([1, [2, [3, "ab"]], [], 4])
=> [1, 2, 3, "ab", 4]
--- check case | Already flat, and empty
(flatten([5, 6]), flatten([]))
=> ([5, 6], [])
--- check case | Only empty lists inside
flatten([[], [[]], [[], [[]]]])
=> []
--- check case | Very deep on one side
flatten([[[[[[[[["core"]]]]]]]], "shell"])
=> ["core", "shell"]

+++ practice | The cheapest climb, bottom-up
--- task
Each stair of a tower has a toll: you pay `costs[i]` whenever you step on stair `i`. You start on the ground, below stair 0, and every move goes up 1 or 2 stairs. The top is just above the last stair. Write `cheapest_climb(costs)`: the least you can pay to reach the top.

Fill a table bottom-up, where `best[i]` is the least you pay to be standing on stair `i`:

- stairs 0 and 1 can be reached straight from the ground, so `best[0]` is `costs[0]` and `best[1]` is `costs[1]`;
- any higher stair is reached from one of the two stairs below it: `best[i]` is `costs[i]` plus the smaller of `best[i - 1]` and `best[i - 2]`;
- the top is reached from one of the last two stairs, so the answer is the smaller of the last two entries. With fewer than two stairs, you can step from the ground straight to the top, and pay `0`.

It must handle 100,000 stairs, far past the recursion limit, so use a loop; no function may call itself. For example, `cheapest_climb([10, 15, 20])` is `15`: step on stair 1, then jump to the top.
--- starter
from functools import lru_cache


def cheapest_climb(costs):
    @lru_cache(maxsize=None)
    def best(i):
        if i < 2:
            return costs[i]
        return costs[i] + min(best(i - 1), best(i - 2))

    n = len(costs)
    return min(best(n - 1), best(n - 2))
--- solution
def cheapest_climb(costs):
    best = []
    for i, cost in enumerate(costs):
        if i < 2:
            best.append(cost)
        else:
            best.append(cost + min(best[i - 1], best[i - 2]))
    if len(best) < 2:
        return 0
    return min(best[-1], best[-2])
--- hint
The starter is the same recipe written top-down. It is correct for a short tower, but for 100,000 stairs it needs 100,000 calls waiting inside each other, and Python stops at about 1000. It also breaks on towers of 0 or 1 stairs.
--- hint
Build `best` with a loop over `enumerate(costs)`: append the stair's own cost for the first two stairs, and `cost + min(best[i - 1], best[i - 2])` after that. Each entry only needs the two before it, and they are already there. Handle the short towers before reading `best[-2]`.
--- check case | Three stairs
cheapest_climb([10, 15, 20])
=> 15
--- check case | Skipping the expensive stairs
cheapest_climb([1, 100, 1, 1, 1, 100, 1, 1, 100, 1])
=> 6
--- check case | Towers of 0, 1 and 2 stairs
(cheapest_climb([]), cheapest_climb([5]), cheapest_climb([4, 7]))
=> (0, 0, 4)
--- check case | 100,000 stairs, far past the recursion limit
cheapest_climb([(i * 37) % 101 for i in range(100000)])
=> 1996041
--- check source absent | Does not call itself
\bbest\s*\(\s*i\s*-

+++ practice | Paths around rocks
--- task
A rover crosses a field of `rows` by `cols` squares, from the top-left square `(0, 0)` to the bottom-right one, moving only **right** or **down**, one square at a time. Some squares have rocks; they are in the set `blocked`, as `(row, col)` tuples. Write `grid_paths(rows, cols, blocked)`: how many different paths there are.

Fill a table bottom-up: `paths[r][c]` is the number of paths from the start to square `(r, c)`. The start has 1 path. A blocked square has 0. Any other square can only be reached from the square above it or the one to its left, so its paths are theirs added up (a square outside the field counts as 0).

Build the table with a comprehension, so every row is its own list. For example, `grid_paths(3, 3, set())` is `6`, and `grid_paths(3, 3, {(1, 1)})` is `2`. If the start or the end is blocked, the answer is `0`.
--- starter
def grid_paths(rows, cols, blocked):
    paths = [[0] * cols] * rows
    for r in range(rows):
        for c in range(cols):
            if (r, c) in blocked:
                continue
            if r == 0 and c == 0:
                paths[r][c] = 1
            else:
                paths[r][c] = paths[r - 1][c] + paths[r][c - 1]
    return paths[rows - 1][cols - 1]
--- solution
def grid_paths(rows, cols, blocked):
    paths = [[0] * cols for _ in range(rows)]
    for r in range(rows):
        for c in range(cols):
            if (r, c) in blocked:
                continue
            if r == 0 and c == 0:
                paths[r][c] = 1
                continue
            if r > 0:
                paths[r][c] += paths[r - 1][c]
            if c > 0:
                paths[r][c] += paths[r][c - 1]
    return paths[rows - 1][cols - 1]
--- hint
The starter has two bugs from earlier lessons. `[[0] * cols] * rows` makes one row shared by every position. And at the top edge, `paths[r - 1]` with `r` equal to 0 is `paths[-1]`: a negative index quietly reads the last row instead of failing.
--- hint
Build the rows with `[[0] * cols for _ in range(rows)]`. Then add the square above only `if r > 0`, and the square to the left only `if c > 0`.
--- check case | An open 3 by 3 field
grid_paths(3, 3, set())
=> 6
--- check case | A rock in the middle
grid_paths(3, 3, {(1, 1)})
=> 2
--- check case | Only one way round
grid_paths(3, 4, {(0, 1), (1, 1)})
=> 1
--- check case | Blocked start or end
(grid_paths(2, 2, {(0, 0)}), grid_paths(2, 2, {(1, 1)}))
=> (0, 0)
--- check case | A single square, and a single row
(grid_paths(1, 1, set()), grid_paths(1, 5, set()))
=> (1, 1)
--- check case | An open 18 by 18 field
grid_paths(18, 18, set())
=> 2333606220

+++ practice | The best haul, never two in a row
--- task
A rover drives past a line of sample sites. `values[i]` is how much the sample at site `i` is worth. Drilling heats the drill, so it can never drill two sites that are next to each other. Write `best_haul(values)`: the most total value it can collect.

Think of it as DP, one site at a time. After each site, keep two numbers: the best total if you **took** this site, and the best total if you **skipped** it. Taking a site means you skipped the one before it; skipping it means you keep the better of the two totals so far.

Watch the edges: no sites at all gives `0`; one site gives its value; and a negative value is a loss, never worth taking, so all negative values give `0`. It must handle 100,000 sites.

For example, `best_haul([2, 7, 9, 3, 1])` is `12` (2 + 9 + 1).
--- starter
def best_haul(values):
    return max(sum(values[0::2]), sum(values[1::2]))
--- solution
def best_haul(values):
    took = 0
    skipped = 0
    for v in values:
        took, skipped = skipped + v, max(took, skipped)
    return max(took, skipped)
--- hint
Taking every second site is not always best: in `[2, 1, 1, 2]`, the best is the first and the last, 2 + 2 = 4, which are three apart.
--- hint
Start both totals at 0. For each value `v`, the new "took" is the old "skipped" plus `v`, and the new "skipped" is the bigger of the old two. Update both at once, `took, skipped = ..., ...`, so each uses the old values. The answer is the bigger of the two at the end.
--- check case | The classic example
best_haul([2, 7, 9, 3, 1])
=> 12
--- check case | The best two can be three apart
best_haul([2, 1, 1, 2])
=> 4
--- check case | No sites, and one site
(best_haul([]), best_haul([5]))
=> (0, 5)
--- check case | Losses are never taken
(best_haul([-5, -1]), best_haul([4, -10, -10, 4]))
=> (0, 8)
--- check case | 100,000 sites
best_haul([(i * 37) % 101 for i in range(100000)])
=> 3003912

+++ practice | Debug: the cargo that packed itself twice
--- task
`best_cargo(items, capacity)` should find the most value that fits in a cargo bay. Each item is a tuple `(weight, value)`, and each item can be packed **at most once**. The table `best[c]` is meant to hold the most value that fits in weight `c` using the items seen so far.

**The bug report:** `best_cargo([(2, 3)], 4)` gives `6`, as if the single item had been packed twice, instead of `3`. Fix the one line that causes it.
--- starter
def best_cargo(items, capacity):
    best = [0] * (capacity + 1)
    for weight, value in items:
        for c in range(weight, capacity + 1):
            best[c] = max(best[c], best[c - weight] + value)
    return best[capacity]
--- solution
def best_cargo(items, capacity):
    best = [0] * (capacity + 1)
    for weight, value in items:
        for c in reversed(range(weight, capacity + 1)):
            best[c] = max(best[c], best[c - weight] + value)
    return best[capacity]
--- hint
Walk the example by hand. With the item `(2, 3)`: `best[2]` becomes 3. Then `best[4]` looks at `best[2]`, which already includes this same item, and adds it again.
--- hint
`best[c - weight]` must still hold the value from **before** this item. Go through the capacities from the top down, `reversed(range(weight, capacity + 1))`, so the smaller entries have not been updated yet when the bigger ones read them.
--- check case | A single item is packed once
best_cargo([(2, 3)], 4)
=> 3
--- check case | The best pair
best_cargo([(5, 10), (4, 40), (6, 30), (3, 50)], 10)
=> 90
--- check case | Nothing fits, or nothing to pack
(best_cargo([(6, 9)], 5), best_cargo([], 5))
=> (0, 0)
--- check case | 200 items and a big bay
best_cargo([(i % 37 + 1, (i * 7) % 50 + 1) for i in range(200)], 1000)
=> 3080

+++ practice | Edit distance
--- task
The **edit distance** between two strings is the fewest single-letter changes that turn one into the other, where a change is inserting a letter, deleting a letter, or replacing one letter with another. It is how spelling checkers find the word you meant.

Write `edit_distance(a, b)`. Build it from subproblems: let `d(i, j)` be the distance between the first `i` letters of `a` and the first `j` letters of `b`.

- `d(0, j)` is `j` (insert every letter) and `d(i, 0)` is `i` (delete every letter).
- If letter `i - 1` of `a` equals letter `j - 1` of `b`, then `d(i, j)` is `d(i - 1, j - 1)`.
- Otherwise it is 1 plus the smallest of `d(i - 1, j)` (delete), `d(i, j - 1)` (insert) and `d(i - 1, j - 1)` (replace).

The answer is `d(len(a), len(b))`. It must handle two strings of 300 letters each, so remember every subproblem: fill a table bottom-up, or use `lru_cache`.

For example, `edit_distance("kitten", "sitting")` is `3`.
--- starter
def edit_distance(a, b):
    return abs(len(a) - len(b))
--- solution
def edit_distance(a, b):
    d = [[0] * (len(b) + 1) for _ in range(len(a) + 1)]
    for i in range(len(a) + 1):
        d[i][0] = i
    for j in range(len(b) + 1):
        d[0][j] = j
    for i in range(1, len(a) + 1):
        for j in range(1, len(b) + 1):
            if a[i - 1] == b[j - 1]:
                d[i][j] = d[i - 1][j - 1]
            else:
                d[i][j] = 1 + min(d[i - 1][j], d[i][j - 1], d[i - 1][j - 1])
    return d[len(a)][len(b)]
--- hint
A plain recursive function built straight from the three rules is correct, but it solves the same `(i, j)` pairs again and again, like the plain recursive `fib`: for two 300-letter strings it would never finish. Every pair must be worked out once.
--- hint
Make a table with `len(a) + 1` rows and `len(b) + 1` columns, each row its own list. Fill row 0 and column 0 with the base cases first.
--- hint
Then fill the rest row by row, left to right, with the two rules. When you work out `d[i][j]`, the entries above it, to its left and diagonally up-left are already filled. Return the bottom-right entry.
--- check case | kitten to sitting
edit_distance("kitten", "sitting")
=> 3
--- check case | From or to nothing
(edit_distance("", "abc"), edit_distance("abc", ""), edit_distance("", ""))
=> (3, 3, 0)
--- check case | Equal strings, and one swap
(edit_distance("orbit", "orbit"), edit_distance("flaw", "lawn"))
=> (0, 2)
--- check case | Two 300-letter strings
edit_distance("orbit" * 60, "rabbit" * 50)
=> 180

=== py3-12 | Big-O: code that finishes
--- teach
Last lesson, the plain recursive `fib(40)` made over 300 million calls, and the memoised one did the real work 41 times. Same answer, wildly different waiting time. This lesson gives you a way to talk about that difference, and to spot it by reading code, before you run it.

### How the work grows

Picture a party. If every guest shakes hands with every other guest, 10 guests make 45 handshakes, but 1,000 guests make 499,500. If instead every guest waves once, 10 guests make 10 waves and 1,000 guests make 1,000 waves. Both parties get bigger by the same amount, but the work grows in completely different ways.

**Big-O notation** is a short way to write how the work grows as the input grows. The letter `n` stands for the size of the input, such as how many items are in the list. Big-O ignores **constant factors**, meaning fixed multipliers: 3 steps per item and 1 step per item both count as "grows in step with n". What it cares about is the *shape* of the growth. Read O(n) aloud as "O of n", or "[[order n|big-o-name]]".

| Big-O | Name | 1,000 items | 1,000,000 items |
|---|---|---|---|
| O(1) | constant | 1 | 1 |
| O(log n) | logarithmic | 10 | 20 |
| O(n) | linear | 1,000 | 1,000,000 |
| O(n log n) | sorting | 10,000 | 20,000,000 |
| O(n²) | quadratic | 1,000,000 | 1,000,000,000,000 |

Row by row:

- **O(1), constant**: the same work however big the input is, like looking up one key in a dict.
- **O(log n), logarithmic**: the number of times you can [[halve n|logarithm]] before you get down to 1. That grows very slowly: about 10 for a thousand, about 20 for a million.
- **O(n), linear**: a fixed amount of work per item, like one pass of a `for` loop.
- **O(n log n)**: what a good sort, like `sorted()`, costs.
- **O(n²), quadratic**: said "O of n squared" (the small ² means "squared", n times n). Every item paired with every other item, like the handshakes.

Python does, [[very roughly|step-speed]], ten million simple steps a second. So O(n²) on a million items is a million million steps, which is about 100,000 seconds: more than a day. That is not "slow". For any real use, it never finishes.

### Know the cost of what you call

Quadratic code rarely looks like two loops. Usually it is one loop, with a second loop **hidden** inside a single operation. These are the costs to know:

| Operation | list | set / dict |
|---|---|---|
| `x in c` | O(n): checks every item | O(1) [[on average|hash-average]] |
| `c.append(x)` / `c.add(x)` | O(1) | O(1) |
| `c.insert(0, x)`, `c.pop(0)` | O(n) | n/a |
| `sum(c[a:b])` | O(b - a) | n/a |

- `x in c` on a list looks at the items one by one until it finds `x`. On a set or dict it jumps straight to the answer.
- `append` adds to the end of a list, and `add` adds to a set. Both are quick.
- `insert(0, x)` and `pop(0)` work at the *front* of a list, so every other item must shift along one place, as in the deque note of the standard library lesson.
- `sum(c[a:b])` copies the slice from position `a` up to (not including) `b`, then adds each number in it: `b - a` numbers, so O(b - a). "n/a" means "not available": sets have no positions, so there is nothing to insert at or slice.

### A hidden loop in real code

This innocent-looking loop removes duplicates, keeping the first of each:

```python
result = []
for x in items:
    if x not in result:      # hidden loop: checks every item in result
        result.append(x)
```

The `for` runs once per item, n times. Inside it, `x not in result` checks through the list, which can hold up to n items. That is n times n: O(n²).

The fix is to keep a `set` beside the list. The set answers "have I seen this?" instantly, and the list keeps the order, which a set [[does not remember|set-order]]:

```python
items = [3, 1, 3, 2]
seen = set()
for x in items:
    if x not in seen:
        seen.add(x)
        print("first time:", x)
# first time: 3
# first time: 1
# first time: 2
```

Now every step inside the loop is O(1), so the whole loop is O(n).

### Precompute to answer many questions

Suppose you will be asked, over and over, for the sum of `nums[lo:hi]` for different `lo` and `hi`. Summing each slice is O(n) per question. With 200,000 questions about a list of 100,000 numbers, that is billions of steps.

Think of the distance markers along a highway. To know how far it is from marker 40 to marker 75, nobody measures the road again: you subtract, 75 - 40 = 35. The markers are totals worked out once, from the start of the road.

Do the same with numbers. Build **prefix sums** once: `prefix[i]` is the sum of the first `i` numbers. Then any range sum is [[one subtraction|mile-markers]]:

```python
nums   = [3, 1, 4, 1, 5]
prefix = [0, 3, 4, 8, 9, 14]      # prefix[i] = sum(nums[:i])
# sum(nums[1:4]) == prefix[4] - prefix[1] == 9 - 3 == 6
```

Why does that work? `prefix[4]` is the first four numbers, 3 + 1 + 4 + 1. `prefix[1]` is the first one, 3. Take the first away and you are left with 1 + 4 + 1, which is `nums[1:4]`, and that is 6. Notice that `prefix` has one more entry than `nums`, because it starts with the sum of zero numbers, 0.

To build it, start with `[0]` and keep adding the next number to the last total:

```python
nums = [3, 1, 4, 1, 5]
prefix = [0]
for n in nums:
    prefix.append(prefix[-1] + n)
print(prefix)   # [0, 3, 4, 8, 9, 14]
```

`prefix[-1]` is the last item in the list: a negative index counts from the end. Building the table is O(n) once, and after that each question is O(1), instead of O(n) per question.

**Watch out:** the common mistake is testing only on tiny inputs. Everything is fast on ten items, so the O(n²) version passes every small test and then [[freezes on real data|deadlines]]. Always ask: *what happens when this is 100,000?*

::: context big-o-name What the O stands for
The O stands for "order", as in "on the order of a thousand", meaning roughly that size. Mathematicians used the notation for over a century before programmers borrowed it; Paul Bachmann introduced it in 1894. Big-O deliberately drops details: an algorithm that takes 3n + 7 steps is O(n), because for big n the 3 and the 7 hardly matter next to how the n grows. That is why you can compare two methods before measuring either.
:::

::: context logarithm Counting the halvings
A logarithm answers "how many times do I multiply 2 by itself to reach this number?" or, the other way round, "how many times can I halve it before I reach 1?". 1,024 halves ten times: 512, 256, 128, 64, 32, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1. A million halves about twenty times. So an O(log n) method barely notices the input growing: double the input and it takes one more step. You will build one of these in the binary search lesson.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 150" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="20" y="15" width="320" height="18" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="20" y="43" width="160" height="18" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="20" y="71" width="80" height="18" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="20" y="99" width="40" height="18" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="20" y="127" width="20" height="18" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="180" y="28" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">16</text>
  <text x="190" y="56" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44">8   (1 halving)</text>
  <text x="110" y="84" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44">4   (2 halvings)</text>
  <text x="70" y="112" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44">2   (3 halvings)</text>
  <text x="50" y="140" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44">1   (4 halvings: log of 16 is 4)</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context step-speed Why only roughly
How many steps a second depends on the computer and on what a "step" is: adding two numbers is quicker than looking up a key in a dict. Ten million a second is a fair rule of thumb for plain Python, and good enough for the question that matters: seconds, hours or years? One million squared is 1,000,000,000,000 steps; at ten million a second that is 100,000 seconds, a little over 27 hours.
:::

::: context hash-average What "on average" means here
A set or dict works out a number from each item, called a hash, and uses it to choose a slot to store the item in. Looking something up means working out the same hash and going straight to that slot. Now and then two different items want the same slot, and then Python has to check a few extra places. That is rare, so the time is the same "on average" however big the set is, even though an unlucky lookup is a little slower.
:::

::: context set-order Sets forget the order
A set only remembers *what* is in it, not the order the items arrived in, so printing a set can show them in any order. That is why the fix keeps both: the set for fast checks, the list for the order. There is another neat way: dicts *do* remember the order keys were added, so `list(dict.fromkeys(items))` also removes duplicates and keeps the first of each. Writing the loop yourself first is how you learn why it works.
:::

::: context mile-markers Totals from the start of the road
Each prefix total is like a highway marker: the distance from the start. The sum of a stretch is the marker at its end minus the marker at its start. You have met a tool that builds these totals: `accumulate` from the standard library lesson gives running totals, so `[0] + list(accumulate(nums))` makes the same prefix list.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 120" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <line x1="20" y1="60" x2="340" y2="60" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <g font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">
    <line x1="20" y1="52" x2="20" y2="68" stroke="#1f2a44"/><text x="20" y="84">0</text>
    <line x1="86" y1="52" x2="86" y2="68" stroke="#1f2a44"/><text x="86" y="84">3</text>
    <line x1="109" y1="52" x2="109" y2="68" stroke="#1f2a44"/><text x="109" y="84">4</text>
    <line x1="200" y1="52" x2="200" y2="68" stroke="#1f2a44"/><text x="200" y="84">8</text>
    <line x1="223" y1="52" x2="223" y2="68" stroke="#1f2a44"/><text x="223" y="84">9</text>
    <line x1="340" y1="52" x2="340" y2="68" stroke="#1f2a44"/><text x="340" y="84">14</text>
  </g>
  <rect x="86" y="54" width="137" height="12" fill="#f2b880" opacity="0.8"/>
  <text x="154" y="40" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">nums[1:4] = 9 - 3 = 6</text>
  <text x="180" y="108" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">prefix totals, like markers along a road</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context deadlines When slow means broken
In many programs, too slow is the same as wrong. Flight software runs in a loop that must finish each pass before the next one is due: a control loop running 50 times a second has 20 milliseconds per pass, and one that sometimes takes longer can miss a steering command. Engineers therefore test with the biggest inputs the system can ever see, not only small friendly ones.
:::
--- task
Both functions must handle 100,000-item inputs in well under a second. The starter gives the right answers but is too slow.

- `dedupe(items)` returns the items with duplicates removed, keeping the **first** occurrence of each, in order. Keep a `set` called `seen` beside the result list, and check `seen` instead of the list.
- `range_sums(nums, queries)` takes a list of `(lo, hi)` pairs and returns a list with `sum(nums[lo:hi])` for each (`lo <= hi`, both within the list). Use prefix sums: build `prefix` once, then answer each pair with `prefix[hi] - prefix[lo]`.
--- starter
def dedupe(items):
    result = []
    for x in items:
        if x not in result:
            result.append(x)
    return result


def range_sums(nums, queries):
    return [sum(nums[lo:hi]) for lo, hi in queries]
--- solution
def dedupe(items):
    seen = set()
    result = []
    for x in items:
        if x not in seen:
            seen.add(x)
            result.append(x)
    return result


def range_sums(nums, queries):
    prefix = [0]
    for n in nums:
        prefix.append(prefix[-1] + n)
    return [prefix[hi] - prefix[lo] for lo, hi in queries]
--- hint
The starter gives the right answers. It is too slow because `x not in result` and `sum(nums[lo:hi])` are hidden loops, and each one runs once for every item or every question.
--- hint
In `dedupe`, start with `seen = set()` as well as `result = []`. Test `if x not in seen:`, and when it is new, do both `seen.add(x)` and `result.append(x)`.
--- hint
In `range_sums`, build `prefix = [0]`, then `for n in nums: prefix.append(prefix[-1] + n)`. It has one more entry than `nums`. Then return `[prefix[hi] - prefix[lo] for lo, hi in queries]`.
--- check case | dedupe keeps the first of each, in order
dedupe([3, 1, 3, 2, 1, 3])
=> [3, 1, 2]
--- check case | dedupe works on strings and empty input
(dedupe(list("mississippi")), dedupe([]))
=> (["m", "i", "s", "p"], [])
--- check test | dedupe on 200,000 items
dedupe(list(range(100000)) * 2) == list(range(100000))
?? `x not in list` scans the whole list. A set answers that instantly.
--- check case | range_sums answers each query
range_sums([3, 1, 4, 1, 5], [(1, 4), (0, 5), (2, 2), (4, 5)])
=> [6, 14, 0, 5]
--- check case | Negative numbers
range_sums([5, -2, -3, 10], [(0, 3), (1, 4)])
=> [0, 5]
--- check test | 200,000 queries over 100,000 numbers
range_sums(list(range(100000)), [(0, 100000)] * 200000 + [(5, 10)])[-2:] == [4999950000, 35]
?? Summing a slice for every query is billions of steps here. Build prefix sums once.

+++ practice | Any duplicates?
--- task
Write `has_duplicates(items)`. It returns `True` if any value appears more than once in the list, and `False` otherwise. It must handle 200,000 items in well under a second, so it must be O(n): walk the list once, keeping a `set` of the values seen so far, and stop as soon as you meet one that is already in it.

For example, `has_duplicates([3, 1, 4, 1])` is `True`, and `has_duplicates([])` is `False`.
--- starter
def has_duplicates(items):
    return False
--- solution
def has_duplicates(items):
    seen = set()
    for x in items:
        if x in seen:
            return True
        seen.add(x)
    return False
--- hint
Comparing every pair with two loops would be right, but for 200,000 items that is about 20 billion comparisons, O(n²).
--- hint
One loop. For each value, ask `x in seen` first (O(1) for a set); if it is there, return `True`. Otherwise `seen.add(x)`. After the loop, return `False`.
--- check case | A repeat
has_duplicates([3, 1, 4, 1])
=> True
--- check case | No repeats, and no items
(has_duplicates([3, 1, 4]), has_duplicates([]))
=> (False, False)
--- check case | Strings
has_duplicates(["ada", "lin", "Ada"])
=> False
--- check case | 200,000 different items
has_duplicates(list(range(200000)))
=> False
?? Comparing every pair is billions of steps here. A set remembers what you have seen.
--- check case | 200,000 items with one repeat at the very end
has_duplicates(list(range(199999)) + [7])
=> True

+++ practice | Items in both lists
--- task
Write `common_items(a, b)`. It returns the items of `a` that also appear in `b`, in `a`'s order, keeping repeats from `a`. It must handle two lists of 100,000 items each in well under a second.

`x in b` on a list checks every item of `b`, a hidden loop. Make a `set` of `b` once, before the loop, and test against that.

For example, `common_items([5, 1, 5, 7], [7, 5])` is `[5, 5, 7]`.
--- starter
def common_items(a, b):
    return []
--- solution
def common_items(a, b):
    wanted = set(b)
    return [x for x in a if x in wanted]
--- hint
`[x for x in a if x in b]` gives the right answer. Count its work: the comprehension runs once per item of `a`, and each `x in b` can look at every item of `b`. That is 100,000 × 100,000 steps.
--- hint
Build `wanted = set(b)` once, which is O(len(b)), then use `x in wanted` in the comprehension, which is O(1).
--- check case | Keeps a's order and repeats
common_items([5, 1, 5, 7], [7, 5])
=> [5, 5, 7]
--- check case | Nothing in common, and empty lists
(common_items([1, 2], [3]), common_items([], [1]), common_items([1], []))
=> ([], [], [])
--- check case | Repeats in b do not matter
common_items(["x", "y"], ["y", "y", "y"])
=> ["y"]
--- check test | Two lists of 100,000
len(common_items(list(range(100000)), list(range(50000, 150000)))) == 50000
?? `x in list` scans the whole list. Make a set of b first.

+++ practice | How many slices add up to k?
--- task
Write `count_sums(nums, k)`: how many **contiguous** slices `nums[i:j]` (with `i < j`) add up to exactly `k`. The numbers can be negative. It must handle 100,000 numbers, so it must be O(n).

Combine two ideas: prefix sums, and the two sum trick of remembering what you have seen in a dict. Walk the list once, keeping the running total, which is the prefix sum so far. A slice ending here adds up to `k` exactly when some **earlier** prefix sum equals `running - k`. So keep a `Counter` of the prefix sums seen so far, starting with one prefix sum of `0` (the empty start), and add up how many times `running - k` has been seen.

For example, `count_sums([1, 1, 1], 2)` is `2`: the first two 1s, and the last two.
--- starter
def count_sums(nums, k):
    return 0
--- solution
from collections import Counter


def count_sums(nums, k):
    seen = Counter()
    seen[0] = 1
    running = 0
    count = 0
    for x in nums:
        running += x
        count += seen[running - k]
        seen[running] += 1
    return count
--- hint
The sum of `nums[i:j]` is `prefix[j] - prefix[i]`. At position `j` you want every earlier `i` with `prefix[j] - prefix[i] == k`, that is, `prefix[i] == prefix[j] - k`. You only need to know how many such earlier prefix sums there were.
--- hint
Start with an empty `Counter`, set `seen[0] = 1` for the empty start, and set `running = 0`. For each number: add it to `running`, add `seen[running - k]` to the count (a `Counter` gives 0 for a missing key), and only then count `running` itself with `seen[running] += 1`.
--- check case | Two slices of two 1s
count_sums([1, 1, 1], 2)
=> 2
--- check case | A slice of one, and a slice of two
count_sums([1, 2, 3], 3)
=> 2
--- check case | Negative numbers and a k of 0
(count_sums([3, -3, 3, -3], 0), count_sums([0, 0, 0], 0))
=> (4, 6)
--- check case | Empty list, and one number
(count_sums([], 0), count_sums([5], 5))
=> (0, 1)
--- check case | 100,000 numbers
count_sums([(i * 7) % 11 - 5 for i in range(100000)], 3)
=> 413249587
?? Trying every slice is billions of steps. Keep a Counter of the prefix sums you have seen.

+++ practice | The best window
--- task
Write `best_window(nums, k)`: the largest sum of `k` numbers that sit next to each other in `nums`. It must handle 100,000 numbers with `k` of 50,000, so it must be O(n):

- work out the sum of the first `k` numbers once;
- then slide the window one place at a time: add the number that comes in and take away the number that drops out, keeping the best sum seen.

Edge cases: if `k` is less than 1 or more than `len(nums)`, return `None`. The numbers can all be negative, and then the answer is negative too, so do not start the best at 0.

For example, `best_window([1, 4, 2, 10, 2, 3, 1, 0, 20], 4)` is `24` (3 + 1 + 0 + 20).
--- starter
def best_window(nums, k):
    return None
--- solution
def best_window(nums, k):
    if k < 1 or k > len(nums):
        return None
    window = sum(nums[:k])
    best = window
    for i in range(k, len(nums)):
        window += nums[i] - nums[i - k]
        best = max(best, window)
    return best
--- hint
Summing every window from scratch, with `sum(nums[i:i + k])`, is about 50,000 windows of 50,000 numbers each. Neighboring windows share all but two numbers.
--- hint
Check the edge cases first. Then `window = sum(nums[:k])` and `best = window`. For each `i` from `k` to the end, the number `nums[i]` comes in and `nums[i - k]` drops out.
--- check case | The best window of four
best_window([1, 4, 2, 10, 2, 3, 1, 0, 20], 4)
=> 24
--- check case | All negative
best_window([-3, -1, -2], 2)
=> -3
--- check case | k too big, or 0
(best_window([1, 2], 3), best_window([1, 2], 0), best_window([], 1))
=> (None, None, None)
--- check case | k equal to the length
best_window([2, -1, 4], 3)
=> 5
--- check case | 100,000 numbers, windows of 50,000
best_window([(i * 13) % 17 - 8 for i in range(100000)], 50000)
=> 12
?? Summing each window again is billions of steps. Slide it: add the new number, take away the old one.

+++ practice | Debug: sums that were one short
--- task
`inclusive_sums(nums, queries)` answers questions about sums. Each query is a pair `(lo, hi)`, and the answer is the sum of `nums[lo]` up to **and including** `nums[hi]`. It should be O(n) to set up and O(1) per query.

**The bug report:** every answer is missing the first number of its range: for `[3, 1, 4, 1, 5]` and the query `(1, 3)` it gives `5` (1 + 4) instead of `6` (1 + 4 + 1), and for `(0, 4)` it gives `11` instead of `14`. Fix it, and keep it O(1) per query.
--- starter
def inclusive_sums(nums, queries):
    prefix = []
    total = 0
    for n in nums:
        total += n
        prefix.append(total)
    return [prefix[hi] - prefix[lo] for lo, hi in queries]
--- solution
def inclusive_sums(nums, queries):
    prefix = [0]
    for n in nums:
        prefix.append(prefix[-1] + n)
    return [prefix[hi + 1] - prefix[lo] for lo, hi in queries]
--- hint
Write out the starter's `prefix` for `[3, 1, 4, 1, 5]`: `[3, 4, 8, 9, 14]`. Here `prefix[i]` includes `nums[i]` itself. So what does `prefix[hi] - prefix[lo]` take away, besides everything before `lo`?
--- hint
The lesson's way avoids the trap: start the list at `[0]`, so `prefix[i]` is the sum of the numbers **before** position `i`. Then the sum from `lo` to `hi`, both included, is `prefix[hi + 1] - prefix[lo]`.
--- check case | Queries that include both ends
inclusive_sums([3, 1, 4, 1, 5], [(1, 3), (0, 4), (2, 2)])
=> [6, 14, 4]
--- check case | Negative numbers
inclusive_sums([5, -2, -3, 10], [(0, 2), (1, 3)])
=> [0, 5]
--- check case | No queries
inclusive_sums([1, 2], [])
=> []
--- check case | 100,000 numbers
inclusive_sums(list(range(100000)), [(0, 99999), (10, 10)])
=> [4999950000, 10]

+++ practice | The longest run of consecutive numbers
--- task
Write `longest_streak(nums)`: the length of the longest run of whole numbers that follow on from each other, like 4, 5, 6, 7, among the values in `nums`. The list is in no order, and it can have repeats and negative numbers. An empty list gives `0`.

It must be O(n) on 200,000 numbers. Put the values in a set. A run can only **start** at a value `x` whose `x - 1` is not in the set; from each such start, count upwards while the next number is in the set. Each value is then counted at most once in all.

For example, `longest_streak([100, 4, 200, 1, 3, 2])` is `4` (1, 2, 3, 4).
--- starter
def longest_streak(nums):
    return 0
--- solution
def longest_streak(nums):
    values = set(nums)
    best = 0
    for x in values:
        if x - 1 in values:
            continue
        end = x
        while end + 1 in values:
            end += 1
        best = max(best, end - x + 1)
    return best
--- hint
The obvious way has two slow parts: `x + 1 in nums` searches a list, and counting upwards from **every** value counts a run of 10,000 again from each of its 10,000 values.
--- hint
Search a set instead of the list, and skip any `x` with `x - 1` in the set, since it is not the start of a run. From a real start, move `end` up one at a time while `end + 1` is in the set. The run's length is `end - x + 1`.
--- check case | The classic example
longest_streak([100, 4, 200, 1, 3, 2])
=> 4
--- check case | Repeats count once, and negative numbers work
(longest_streak([5, 5, 5]), longest_streak([-2, -1, 0, 1, 9]))
=> (1, 4)
--- check case | Empty
longest_streak([])
=> 0
--- check case | 110,001 numbers with one long run hidden inside
longest_streak(list(range(0, 200000, 2)) + list(range(50000, 60001)))
=> 10001
?? Counting up from every value, in a list, is far too slow. Use a set, and only count from the start of a run.

=== py3-13 | Problem solving: shortest path on a grid (BFS)
--- teach
Last lesson you learned to ask how the work grows, and to watch for hidden loops like `pop(0)`. This lesson puts that to use on a famous puzzle: "what is the fewest number of moves from here to there?" It comes up in games, maps, mazes, robots and networks. When every move costs the same, the answer is **breadth-first search**, or **BFS**.

### The idea: ripples in a pond

Drop a stone in a still pond. The ripples spread out in rings, and the ring that reaches a leaf first got there by the shortest way.

BFS explores a map the same way, [[in rings|ripple-grid]]. First it visits every square 1 step from the start, then every square 2 steps away, then 3, and so on. The first time it reaches the goal, it got there by a shortest route, because every shorter distance was fully explored before. "Breadth" means width: the search goes wide, ring by ring, before it goes far.

### The grid

A grid is a list of equal-length strings. `#` is a wall and `.` is open floor:

```python
grid = ["S#.",
        ".#.",
        "..E"]
print(grid[2][1])   # .   row 2, column 1
```

`grid[r]` is row `r`, a string, and `grid[r][c]` is the character in column `c` of that row. So a square's position is the pair `(r, c)`, [[row first|row-col]], stored as a tuple. `len(grid)` is the number of rows and `len(grid[0])`, the length of the first row, is the number of columns.

### Finding the start

Before searching, find where `S` is. Two nested loops with `enumerate` visit every square along with its row and column number:

```python
grid = ["S#.", ".#.", "..E"]
for r, row in enumerate(grid):
    for c, ch in enumerate(row):
        if ch == "S":
            print("start", (r, c))
        elif ch == "E":
            print("goal", (r, c))
# start (0, 0)
# goal (2, 2)
```

### The four neighbors

From a square you can move down, up, right or left. Write each move as a change in row and a change in column, `(dr, dc)`:

- `(1, 0)`: one row down;
- `(-1, 0)`: one row up;
- `(0, 1)`: one column right;
- `(0, -1)`: one column left.

```python
r, c = 1, 2
for dr, dc in ((1, 0), (-1, 0), (0, 1), (0, -1)):
    nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
    print((nr, nc))
# (2, 2)
# (0, 2)
# (1, 3)
# (1, 1)
```

`nr` and `nc` are short for "new row" and "new column". Two of those four are no good in the grid above: `(1, 3)` is off the right edge, and `(1, 1)` is a wall.

### Checking a neighbor before you step there

A neighbor is worth visiting only if it is inside the grid, is not a wall, and has not been seen already:

```python
0 <= nr < rows and 0 <= nc < cols and grid[nr][nc] != "#" and (nr, nc) not in seen
```

- `0 <= nr < rows` is a chained comparison, read "`nr` is at least 0 *and* less than `rows`". It says the row is inside the grid. The same for the column.
- `!=` means "is not equal to", so `grid[nr][nc] != "#"` means "not a wall".
- `(nr, nc) not in seen` means "not visited yet". `seen` is a set, so this check is O(1).

The order matters. Python checks the parts of an `and` from left to right and stops at the first false one, so `grid[nr][nc]` is only read once the bounds are known to be good. That is important, because a negative index does not fail in Python: it quietly [[wraps around|negative-wrap]] to the other side of the grid.

### The queue: first in, first out

To explore in rings, keep the squares waiting to be visited in a **queue**: [[first in, first out|fifo]], like a line at a shop. New squares join at the back; the next square to visit leaves from the front.

Use a `deque` from the standard library lesson. `queue.append(x)` joins the back and `queue.popleft()` takes from the front, both in O(1). A plain list's `pop(0)` would shift every other item along, which is O(n), last lesson's hidden loop.

Each item in the queue is a pair: a square, and how many steps it took to get there, like `((0, 0), 0)`. Taking one out, you can unpack both parts in one line, with brackets that match its shape:

```python
from collections import deque

queue = deque([((0, 0), 0)])
(r, c), dist = queue.popleft()
print(r, c, dist)   # 0 0 0
```

### The whole search

```python
from collections import deque

def steps_to(grid, start, goal):
    rows, cols = len(grid), len(grid[0])
    queue = deque([(start, 0)])
    seen = {start}
    while queue:
        (r, c), dist = queue.popleft()
        if (r, c) == goal:
            return dist
        for dr, dc in ((1, 0), (-1, 0), (0, 1), (0, -1)):
            nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
            if 0 <= nr < rows and 0 <= nc < cols and grid[nr][nc] != "#" and (nr, nc) not in seen:
                seen.add((nr, nc))
                queue.append(((nr, nc), dist + 1))
    return -1
```

Read it in order. The queue starts holding the start square at distance 0, and `seen` starts holding the start square. `while queue:` keeps going as long as the queue is not empty. Each time round, take the front square. If it is the goal, its distance is the answer. Otherwise, queue every good neighbor at one more step. If the queue runs empty without reaching the goal, the goal cannot be reached, and the answer is `-1`.

On `["S..E"]`, starting at `(0, 0)` with the goal at `(0, 3)`: the queue hands out `(0, 0)` at 0 steps, then `(0, 1)` at 1, then `(0, 2)` at 2, then `(0, 3)` at 3, which is the goal. The answer is 3.

Three details make it correct and fast:

- **`deque.popleft()`, not `list.pop(0)`.** Removing from the front of a list shifts every other item along; a deque does it in O(1).
- **Mark squares as seen when you *add* them to the queue**, not when you take them out. Otherwise the same square can be queued many times, once from each neighbor that finds it.
- **Check the bounds before indexing.** A negative index does not fail in Python; it quietly wraps around to the other side of the grid.

Every square is added to the queue at most once, so BFS is O(rows × cols), where × means "times": the work grows in step with the number of squares.

**Watch out:** the common mistake is using **depth-first search**, DFS, for shortest paths. DFS follows one path as far as it goes before backing up, using recursion or a **stack** (last in, first out). It finds *a* path, not the shortest, and exploring every path to compare them is [[exponential|dfs-paths]]. When every step costs the same, reach for BFS and a queue. When steps cost [[different amounts|weighted-maps]], you need a bigger tool.

::: context ripple-grid The rings on the example grid
Here is the example grid with each open square labeled by its distance from S, the ring BFS finds it in. The walls are gray. E is in ring 4, so the answer is 4 steps. The square in the top right is further still, 6 steps, because the path has to go all the way round the wall.
