+++ problem | Merge sorted streams, lazily
--- task
Write a generator function `merge_sorted(*streams)`. Each stream is an iterable whose values are already in order, smallest first, and a stream may be an iterator or even endless. It yields all the values of all the streams as one sorted stream.

- It must be lazy: at the start it takes the first value of each stream, and after that it takes one more value only from the stream whose value it has just yielded. It never reads further ahead.
- When values are equal, the one from the stream given first comes out first.
- Empty streams are fine, and `merge_sorted()` with no streams yields nothing.

For example, `list(merge_sorted([1, 4, 9], [2, 3, 10], [5]))` is `[1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 9, 10]`.
--- starter
def merge_sorted(*streams):
    for stream in streams:
        for value in stream:
            yield value
--- solution
def merge_sorted(*streams):
    iterators = [iter(s) for s in streams]
    heads = []
    for i, it in enumerate(iterators):
        try:
            heads.append((next(it), i))
        except StopIteration:
            pass
    while heads:
        best = min(range(len(heads)), key=lambda j: heads[j])
        value, i = heads[best]
        yield value
        try:
            heads[best] = (next(iterators[i]), i)
        except StopIteration:
            heads = heads[:best] + heads[best + 1:]
--- check case | Three lists
list(merge_sorted([1, 4, 9], [2, 3, 10], [5]))
=> [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 9, 10]
--- check case | Empty streams, and no streams
(list(merge_sorted([], [1], [])), list(merge_sorted()))
=> ([1], [])
--- check case | Endless streams
list(__import__("itertools").islice(merge_sorted((3 * n for n in __import__("itertools").count()), (3 * n + 1 for n in __import__("itertools").count()), (3 * n + 2 for n in __import__("itertools").count())), 7))
=> [0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6]
--- check test | Never reads ahead
(lambda a: (next(merge_sorted(a, [5])), next(a))[1] == 2)(iter([1, 2, 3]))
--- check test | Equal values come out in stream order
[type(x) for x in merge_sorted([1.0, 2], [1, 2.0])] == [float, int, int, float]
--- check test | It is a generator function
__import__("inspect").isgeneratorfunction(merge_sorted)

+++ problem | A decorator that rations calls
--- task
Write `limit_calls(n)`, a decorator with an argument. A function decorated with `@limit_calls(n)` may be called at most `n` times:

- The wrapper has an attribute `remaining`, which starts at `n` and goes down by one on every call that is allowed, including a call whose function then raises.
- When `remaining` is already 0, the wrapper does not call the function; it raises `RuntimeError` with the message `"too many calls to "` followed by the function's name, such as `"too many calls to launch"`.
- The wrapper also has an attribute `reset`, a function with no arguments that sets `remaining` back to `n`.
- It passes every argument through, returns the function's result, and keeps the function's name with `functools.wraps`.

For example, after `@limit_calls(2)` above `def launch(x): return x`, the calls `launch(1)` and `launch(2)` work, and a third call raises `RuntimeError`.
--- starter
import functools


def limit_calls(n):
    def decorator(func):
        return func

    return decorator
--- solution
import functools


def limit_calls(n):
    def decorator(func):
        @functools.wraps(func)
        def wrapper(*args, **kwargs):
            if wrapper.remaining <= 0:
                raise RuntimeError(f"too many calls to {func.__name__}")
            wrapper.remaining -= 1
            return func(*args, **kwargs)

        def reset():
            wrapper.remaining = n

        wrapper.remaining = n
        wrapper.reset = reset
        return wrapper

    return decorator
--- check test | Two calls work, the third raises
(lambda f: f(1) == 2 and f(2) == 4 and raises(RuntimeError, lambda: f(3)))(limit_calls(2)(lambda x: x * 2))
--- check case | The message names the function
(lambda g: (exec("@limit_calls(1)\ndef launch(x):\n    return x\nlaunch(1)\ntry:\n    launch(2)\nexcept RuntimeError as e:\n    msg = str(e)\n", g), g["msg"])[1])(dict(globals()))
=> "too many calls to launch"
--- check case | remaining counts down
(lambda f: [f.remaining, f(1), f.remaining, f(2), f.remaining])(limit_calls(3)(lambda x: x))
=> [3, 1, 2, 2, 1]
--- check test | reset gives the calls back
(lambda f: (f(), raises(RuntimeError, f), f.reset(), f.remaining, f())[3:] == (1, "ok"))(limit_calls(1)(lambda: "ok"))
--- check test | A call that raises still counts, and the function is not called once the limit is reached
(lambda calls: (lambda f: raises(ZeroDivisionError, lambda: f(0)) and raises(RuntimeError, lambda: f(1)) and calls == [0])(limit_calls(1)(lambda x: calls.append(x) or 1 / x)))([])
--- check test | A limit of 0, arguments passed through, and the name kept
(lambda f: raises(RuntimeError, f) and limit_calls(1)(lambda a, b=0: a - b)(5, b=2) == 3 and limit_calls(1)(len).__name__ == "len")(limit_calls(0)(lambda: 1))

+++ problem | A ledger with transactions
--- task
Write a ledger whose transfers happen all together or not at all.

- `class Transfers`, made as `Transfers(balances)` with a dict of balances, stored as `self.balances`, and `self.moves`, a list that starts empty. Its method `move(src, dst, amount)` raises `KeyError` if either account is not in the balances, and `ValueError` if `amount` is not more than 0 or `src` has less than `amount`. Otherwise it takes `amount` from `src`, adds it to `dst`, and appends a line like `"ada->lin 30"` to `self.moves`.
- `class Ledger`, made as `Ledger(balances)`. It keeps its **own copy** of the dict in `self.balances`, so the caller's dict never changes, and a list `self.log` that starts empty.
- `Ledger.transaction()` is a method decorated with `@contextmanager`. It yields a `Transfers` working on a **new copy** of the ledger's balances. If the block finishes without an error, the ledger's balances become the copy's, and the copy's moves are added to the end of `self.log`. If the block raises, nothing in the ledger changes and the error carries on to the caller.

For example, with `ledger = Ledger({"ada": 100, "lin": 20})`, the block `with ledger.transaction() as t: t.move("ada", "lin", 30)` leaves `ledger.balances` as `{"ada": 70, "lin": 50}` and `ledger.log` as `["ada->lin 30"]`.
--- starter
from contextlib import contextmanager


class Ledger:
    def __init__(self, balances):
        self.balances = balances
        self.log = []
--- solution
from contextlib import contextmanager


class Transfers:
    def __init__(self, balances):
        self.balances = balances
        self.moves = []

    def move(self, src, dst, amount):
        if src not in self.balances or dst not in self.balances:
            raise KeyError(f"unknown account in {src}->{dst}")
        if amount <= 0:
            raise ValueError("amount must be more than 0")
        if self.balances[src] < amount:
            raise ValueError(f"{src} cannot pay {amount}")
        self.balances[src] -= amount
        self.balances[dst] += amount
        self.moves.append(f"{src}->{dst} {amount}")


class Ledger:
    def __init__(self, balances):
        self.balances = dict(balances)
        self.log = []

    @contextmanager
    def transaction(self):
        work = Transfers(dict(self.balances))
        yield work
        self.balances = work.balances
        self.log = self.log + work.moves
--- check case | A transaction that works is committed and logged
(lambda g: (exec("ledger = Ledger({'ada': 100, 'lin': 20})\nwith ledger.transaction() as t:\n    t.move('ada', 'lin', 30)\n    t.move('lin', 'ada', 5)\n", g), (g["ledger"].balances, g["ledger"].log))[1])(dict(globals()))
=> ({"ada": 75, "lin": 45}, ["ada->lin 30", "lin->ada 5"])
--- check case | A failed transaction changes nothing, and the error carries on
(lambda g: (exec("ledger = Ledger({'ada': 100, 'lin': 20})\ntry:\n    with ledger.transaction() as t:\n        t.move('ada', 'lin', 30)\n        t.move('lin', 'ada', 500)\nexcept ValueError:\n    caught = True\n", g), (g["ledger"].balances, g["ledger"].log, g["caught"]))[1])(dict(globals()))
=> ({"ada": 100, "lin": 20}, [], True)
--- check test | Moves are not seen by the ledger until the block ends
(lambda g: (exec("ledger = Ledger({'a': 5, 'b': 0})\nwith ledger.transaction() as t:\n    t.move('a', 'b', 5)\n    inside = dict(ledger.balances)\n", g), g["inside"] == {"a": 5, "b": 0} and g["ledger"].balances == {"a": 0, "b": 5})[1])(dict(globals()))
--- check test | Unknown accounts and bad amounts
(lambda t: raises(KeyError, lambda: t.move("a", "zed", 1)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: t.move("a", "b", 0)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: t.move("a", "b", 6)) and t.balances == {"a": 5, "b": 0} and t.moves == [])(Transfers({"a": 5, "b": 0}))
--- check test | The caller's dict is never changed
(lambda d: (lambda ledger: (ledger.transaction().__enter__().move("x", "y", 1), d)[1] == {"x": 1, "y": 0} and ledger.balances is not d)(Ledger(d)))({"x": 1, "y": 0})
--- check case | Two transactions in a row, the second failing
(lambda g: (exec("ledger = Ledger({'a': 10, 'b': 0})\nwith ledger.transaction() as t:\n    t.move('a', 'b', 4)\ntry:\n    with ledger.transaction() as t:\n        t.move('b', 'a', 1)\n        t.move('b', 'c', 1)\nexcept KeyError:\n    pass\n", g), (g["ledger"].balances, g["ledger"].log))[1])(dict(globals()))
=> ({"a": 6, "b": 4}, ["a->b 4"])

+++ problem | Sort and de-duplicate alerts
--- task
Alerts arrive as lines like `"critical | power | bus B undervoltage"`: a severity, a component and a message, separated by `|`. Write these pieces:

- `Severity`, an `Enum` with `INFO = 1`, `WARNING = 2` and `CRITICAL = 3`.
- `Alert`, a **frozen** dataclass with the fields `severity` (a `Severity`), `component` and `message` (both `str`).
- `parse_alerts(lines)`, which returns a list of `Alert`s:
  - split each line at `|` and strip each part; skip a line that does not have exactly 3 parts, or whose severity is not a member's name (in any capitals);
  - keep only the **first** of any alerts that are exactly equal;
  - sort by severity, highest first, then by component from A to Z; alerts that tie on both keep the order they arrived in.

It must handle 100,000 lines with 50,000 different alerts in well under a second. For example, `parse_alerts(["info | comms | ok", "CRITICAL | power | low", "info | comms | ok"])` is `[Alert(Severity.CRITICAL, "power", "low"), Alert(Severity.INFO, "comms", "ok")]`.
--- starter
from dataclasses import dataclass
from enum import Enum


def parse_alerts(lines):
    return []
--- solution
from dataclasses import dataclass
from enum import Enum


class Severity(Enum):
    INFO = 1
    WARNING = 2
    CRITICAL = 3


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class Alert:
    severity: Severity
    component: str
    message: str


def parse_alerts(lines):
    seen = set()
    alerts = []
    for line in lines:
        parts = [part.strip() for part in line.split("|")]
        if len(parts) != 3:
            continue
        name, component, message = parts
        try:
            severity = Severity[name.upper()]
        except KeyError:
            continue
        alert = Alert(severity, component, message)
        if alert not in seen:
            seen.add(alert)
            alerts.append(alert)
    return sorted(alerts, key=lambda a: (-a.severity.value, a.component))
--- check case | Duplicates removed, highest severity first
parse_alerts(["info | comms | ok", "CRITICAL | power | low", "info | comms | ok"])
=> [Alert(Severity.CRITICAL, "power", "low"), Alert(Severity.INFO, "comms", "ok")]
--- check case | Ties on severity go by component, then arrival order
[a.message for a in parse_alerts(["warning | thermal | b", "warning | power | a", "warning | thermal | c", "warning | power | a"])]
=> ["a", "b", "c"]
--- check case | Bad lines are skipped
parse_alerts(["loud | power | x", "info | power", "info | a | b | c", "", "Warning|gps|drift"])
=> [Alert(Severity.WARNING, "gps", "drift")]
--- check test | Alerts are frozen and hashable
raises(__import__("dataclasses").FrozenInstanceError, lambda: setattr(Alert(Severity.INFO, "a", "b"), "message", "c")) and len({Alert(Severity.INFO, "a", "b"), Alert(Severity.INFO, "a", "b")}) == 1
--- check test | 100,000 lines, 50,000 different alerts
(lambda r: len(r) == 50000 and r[0].component == "c0" and r[-1].component == "c9999")(parse_alerts([f"warning | c{i % 50000} | m" for i in range(100000)]))

+++ problem | Exact fractions
--- task
Write a class `Ratio(num, den=1)` for exact fractions of whole numbers.

- `__init__` raises `ZeroDivisionError` if `den` is 0. It stores the fraction in lowest terms in `self.num` and `self.den`, with the sign on the top: `Ratio(2, -4)` stores `-1` and `2`. `math.gcd` gives the greatest common divisor, and `Ratio(0, 5)` stores `0` and `1`.
- `__repr__`: `repr(Ratio(2, 4))` is `"Ratio(1, 2)"`.
- `+`, `-` and `*` work between two `Ratio`s, and between a `Ratio` and an `int` on either side (an `int` counts as that number over 1). `1 - r` needs `__rsub__`, which works out `other - self`. Anything else, such as a float: `NotImplemented`.
- `==` is true for equal fractions, and also for an `int` of the same value: `Ratio(4, 2) == 2`. `__hash__` must agree with `==`, even with ints: equal things need equal hashes, so a whole-number ratio must hash like its `int`.
- `bool(r)` is false only for zero.

For example, `sum([Ratio(1, 2), Ratio(1, 3), Ratio(1, 6)])` is `Ratio(1, 1)`, which equals `1`.
--- starter
class Ratio:
    def __init__(self, num, den=1):
        self.num = num
        self.den = den

    def __add__(self, other):
        return Ratio(self.num + other.num, self.den + other.den)
--- solution
import math


class Ratio:
    def __init__(self, num, den=1):
        if den == 0:
            raise ZeroDivisionError("the bottom of a ratio cannot be 0")
        if den < 0:
            num, den = -num, -den
        g = math.gcd(num, den)
        self.num = num // g
        self.den = den // g

    def __repr__(self):
        return f"Ratio({self.num}, {self.den})"

    def _as_ratio(self, other):
        if isinstance(other, int):
            return Ratio(other)
        if isinstance(other, Ratio):
            return other
        return None

    def __add__(self, other):
        other = self._as_ratio(other)
        if other is None:
            return NotImplemented
        return Ratio(self.num * other.den + other.num * self.den, self.den * other.den)

    __radd__ = __add__

    def __sub__(self, other):
        other = self._as_ratio(other)
        if other is None:
            return NotImplemented
        return Ratio(self.num * other.den - other.num * self.den, self.den * other.den)

    def __rsub__(self, other):
        other = self._as_ratio(other)
        if other is None:
            return NotImplemented
        return other - self

    def __mul__(self, other):
        other = self._as_ratio(other)
        if other is None:
            return NotImplemented
        return Ratio(self.num * other.num, self.den * other.den)

    __rmul__ = __mul__

    def __eq__(self, other):
        other = self._as_ratio(other)
        if other is None:
            return NotImplemented
        return (self.num, self.den) == (other.num, other.den)

    def __hash__(self):
        if self.den == 1:
            return hash(self.num)
        return hash((self.num, self.den))

    def __bool__(self):
        return self.num != 0
--- check case | Lowest terms, sign on top
(Ratio(2, 4), Ratio(1, -2), Ratio(0, -5), Ratio(-6, -3))
=> (Ratio(1, 2), Ratio(-1, 2), Ratio(0, 1), Ratio(2, 1))
--- check test | repr, and a zero bottom
repr(Ratio(2, 4)) == "Ratio(1, 2)" and raises(ZeroDivisionError, lambda: Ratio(1, 0))
--- check test | Adding, taking away and multiplying
Ratio(1, 2) + Ratio(1, 3) == Ratio(5, 6) and Ratio(1, 2) - Ratio(3, 4) == Ratio(-1, 4) and Ratio(2, 3) * Ratio(3, 4) == Ratio(1, 2)
--- check test | ints on either side
Ratio(1, 2) + 1 == Ratio(3, 2) and 1 - Ratio(1, 3) == Ratio(2, 3) and 3 * Ratio(1, 6) == Ratio(1, 2) and Ratio(5, 2) - 2 == Ratio(1, 2)
--- check test | sum() works, and the answer equals an int
sum([Ratio(1, 2), Ratio(1, 3), Ratio(1, 6)]) == 1 and Ratio(4, 2) == 2 and Ratio(1, 2) != 0
--- check test | Equal things hash equal, even across int and Ratio
len({Ratio(1, 2), Ratio(2, 4), Ratio(4, 2), 2}) == 2
--- check test | Floats are refused, and zero is false
raises(TypeError, lambda: Ratio(1, 2) + 0.5) and not Ratio(0, 3) and bool(Ratio(-1, 3))

+++ problem | A text cleaner built from steps
--- task
Write `make_cleaner(steps)`. It takes a list of cleaning steps and returns **one** function that takes a string and applies every step to it, first step first. The steps are:

- `"strip"`: remove spaces at both ends;
- `"lower"`: make it lower case;
- `"collapse"`: turn every run of spaces inside it into one space (`" ".join(text.split())` does it);
- `("replace", old, new)`: replace every `old` with `new`;
- `("truncate", n)`: keep only the first `n` characters.

Build one small function for each step. For the two steps with settings, write a plain function that takes the text and the settings, and fill the settings in with `partial`. Then join the step functions into one with `reduce`, left to right, starting from a function that returns its argument unchanged. An empty list gives that unchanged function.

Any other step, including a `"replace"` or `"truncate"` tuple of the wrong length, raises `ValueError` **when `make_cleaner` is called**, not later. For example, `make_cleaner(["strip", "lower", ("replace", " ", "-")])("  Hello World ")` is `"hello-world"`.
--- starter
def make_cleaner(steps):
    return lambda text: text.strip()
--- solution
from functools import partial, reduce


def strip(text):
    return text.strip()


def lower(text):
    return text.lower()


def collapse(text):
    return " ".join(text.split())


def replace(text, old, new):
    return text.replace(old, new)


def truncate(text, n):
    return text[:n]


SIMPLE = {"strip": strip, "lower": lower, "collapse": collapse}


def make_step(step):
    if isinstance(step, str) and step in SIMPLE:
        return SIMPLE[step]
    if isinstance(step, tuple) and len(step) == 3 and step[0] == "replace":
        return partial(replace, old=step[1], new=step[2])
    if isinstance(step, tuple) and len(step) == 2 and step[0] == "truncate":
        return partial(truncate, n=step[1])
    raise ValueError(f"unknown step: {step}")


def make_cleaner(steps):
    funcs = [make_step(step) for step in steps]
    return reduce(lambda f, g: lambda text: g(f(text)), funcs, lambda text: text)
--- check case | Strip, lower, then replace
make_cleaner(["strip", "lower", ("replace", " ", "-")])("  Hello World ")
=> "hello-world"
--- check case | The order of the steps matters
(make_cleaner(["lower", ("replace", "a", "b")])("AAA"), make_cleaner([("replace", "a", "b"), "lower"])("AAA"))
=> ("bbb", "aaa")
--- check case | collapse and truncate
make_cleaner(["collapse", ("truncate", 9)])("  the   quick  brown ")
=> "the quick"
--- check case | No steps changes nothing, and truncate to 0
(make_cleaner([])("  As Is "), make_cleaner([("truncate", 0)])("abc"))
=> ("  As Is ", "")
--- check test | Unknown or malformed steps are refused when the cleaner is built
raises(ValueError, lambda: make_cleaner(["strip", "shout"])) and raises(ValueError, lambda: make_cleaner([("replace", "x")])) and raises(ValueError, lambda: make_cleaner([("truncate", 1, 2)]))
--- check test | One cleaner can be used on many texts
(lambda clean: [clean(t) for t in ["  A ", "b  ", " C"]] == ["a", "b", "c"])(make_cleaner(["strip", "lower"]))

+++ problem | Debug: the report with three bugs
--- task
`team_report(names, scores, template)` builds a report for a round of a game. `names` and `scores` are lists of the same length, with at least one name. `template` is a dict like `{"title": "Round 1", "sections": {}}`. The report is a copy of the template whose `"sections"` dict gets three entries: `"best"`, the name with the highest score (the first one on a tie); `"high"`, the names that scored 50 or more; and `"low"`, the others, both lists in the order given.

For `names = ["ada", "lin", "sam"]` and `scores = [40, 90, 55]`, the report should be `{"title": "Round 1", "sections": {"best": "lin", "high": ["lin", "sam"], "low": ["ada"]}}`.

**The bug report:** `"high"` and `"low"` always come out empty. Once that is fixed, every name turns up in both lists. And after each call, the caller's `template` has changed too, so the next report starts dirty. Fix all three bugs. Do not change what the report looks like.
--- starter
def team_report(names, scores, template):
    pairs = zip(names, scores)
    best = max(pairs, key=lambda p: p[1])[0]
    teams = dict.fromkeys(["high", "low"], [])
    for name, score in pairs:
        if score >= 50:
            teams["high"].append(name)
        else:
            teams["low"].append(name)
    report = template.copy()
    report["sections"]["best"] = best
    report["sections"]["high"] = teams["high"]
    report["sections"]["low"] = teams["low"]
    return report
--- solution
import copy


def team_report(names, scores, template):
    pairs = list(zip(names, scores))
    best = max(pairs, key=lambda p: p[1])[0]
    teams = {"high": [], "low": []}
    for name, score in pairs:
        if score >= 50:
            teams["high"].append(name)
        else:
            teams["low"].append(name)
    report = copy.deepcopy(template)
    report["sections"]["best"] = best
    report["sections"]["high"] = teams["high"]
    report["sections"]["low"] = teams["low"]
    return report
--- check case | The report for one round
team_report(["ada", "lin", "sam"], [40, 90, 55], {"title": "Round 1", "sections": {}})
=> {"title": "Round 1", "sections": {"best": "lin", "high": ["lin", "sam"], "low": ["ada"]}}
--- check test | The template is not changed
(lambda t: (team_report(["a"], [60], t), t)[1] == {"title": "T", "sections": {}})({"title": "T", "sections": {}})
--- check test | A second report does not change the first
(lambda t: (lambda first: (team_report(["b"], [10], t), first)[1] == {"title": "T", "sections": {"best": "a", "high": ["a"], "low": []}})(team_report(["a"], [60], t)))({"title": "T", "sections": {}})
--- check case | A tie for best goes to the first, and 50 counts as high
team_report(["x", "y"], [50, 50], {"sections": {"note": "tie"}})
=> {"sections": {"note": "tie", "best": "x", "high": ["x", "y"], "low": []}}

+++ problem | The most profitable schedule
--- task
A ground station can take jobs, each a tuple `(start, end, profit)`, but only one job at a time. Two jobs clash if they overlap; a job may start at the very minute another ends. Write `best_schedule(jobs)`: the most total profit from a set of jobs that do not clash. It must handle 50,000 jobs, so it must be O(n log n).

Use dynamic programming with binary search. Sort the jobs by end time. Let `best[i]` be the most profit using only the first `i` jobs in that order, with `best[0] = 0`. For job number `i` (counting from 1), either skip it, keeping `best[i - 1]`, or take it: its profit plus `best[p]`, where `p` is how many of the earlier jobs end at or before its start. Because the jobs are sorted by end time, `p` is found with a binary search on the end times. The answer is the last entry of `best`.

For example, `best_schedule([(1, 2, 50), (3, 5, 20), (6, 19, 100), (2, 100, 200)])` is `250`: the first job and the last one.
--- starter
def best_schedule(jobs):
    total = 0
    last_end = None
    for start, end, profit in sorted(jobs, key=lambda j: j[1]):
        if last_end is None or start >= last_end:
            total += profit
            last_end = end
    return total
--- solution
def best_schedule(jobs):
    jobs = sorted(jobs, key=lambda j: j[1])
    ends = [end for _, end, _ in jobs]
    best = [0] * (len(jobs) + 1)
    for i, (start, end, profit) in enumerate(jobs, start=1):
        lo, hi = 0, i - 1
        while lo < hi:
            mid = (lo + hi) // 2
            if ends[mid] <= start:
                lo = mid + 1
            else:
                hi = mid
        best[i] = max(best[i - 1], best[lo] + profit)
    return best[-1]
--- check case | Two jobs beat three
best_schedule([(1, 2, 50), (3, 5, 20), (6, 19, 100), (2, 100, 200)])
=> 250
--- check case | A mix of overlaps
best_schedule([(1, 3, 5), (2, 5, 6), (4, 6, 5), (6, 7, 4), (5, 8, 11), (7, 9, 2)])
=> 17
--- check case | Touching jobs do not clash
best_schedule([(1, 4, 3), (4, 6, 3)])
=> 6
--- check case | No jobs, and one job
(best_schedule([]), best_schedule([(0, 10, 7)]))
=> (0, 7)
--- check case | 50,000 jobs
best_schedule([((i * 37) % 100000, (i * 37) % 100000 + (i * 13) % 500 + 1, (i * 7) % 97 + 1) for i in range(50000)])
=> 143866

+++ problem | A word ladder
--- task
A word ladder turns one word into another by changing one letter at a time, and every word along the way must be a real word. Write `ladder_length(start, goal, words)`: the number of words in the shortest ladder from `start` to `goal`, counting both ends, or `0` if there is none.

- Every word after `start` must be in the list `words`; `start` itself need not be. All words are lower case, with the letters a to z.
- If `goal` is not in `words`, the answer is `0`. If `start` is `goal`, the answer is `1`.

It must handle a list of about 8,000 five-letter words quickly, so do not compare every pair of words. Put the words in a set, and find a word's neighbors by trying every letter from `"abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz"` in every position.

For example, `ladder_length("hit", "cog", ["hot", "dot", "dog", "lot", "log", "cog"])` is `5`: hit, hot, dot, dog, cog.
--- starter
def ladder_length(start, goal, words):
    if goal not in words:
        return 0
    return len(start)
--- solution
from collections import deque


def ladder_length(start, goal, words):
    words = set(words)
    if goal not in words:
        return 0
    if start == goal:
        return 1
    queue = deque([(start, 1)])
    seen = {start}
    while queue:
        word, length = queue.popleft()
        for i in range(len(word)):
            for ch in "abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz":
                nxt = word[:i] + ch + word[i + 1:]
                if nxt in words and nxt not in seen:
                    if nxt == goal:
                        return length + 1
                    seen.add(nxt)
                    queue.append((nxt, length + 1))
    return 0
--- check case | The classic ladder
ladder_length("hit", "cog", ["hot", "dot", "dog", "lot", "log", "cog"])
=> 5
--- check case | No ladder when the goal is missing
ladder_length("hit", "cog", ["hot", "dot", "dog", "lot", "log"])
=> 0
--- check case | No ladder when the words do not connect
ladder_length("abc", "xyz", ["abd", "xyz"])
=> 0
--- check case | One letter, and already there
(ladder_length("a", "c", ["a", "b", "c"]), ladder_length("same", "same", ["same"]))
=> (2, 1)
--- check case | About 8,000 five-letter words
ladder_length("aaaaa", "fffff", ["".join(p) for p in __import__("itertools").product("abcdef", repeat=5)])
=> 6

+++ problem | The day a target is reached
--- task
A satellite downlinks `daily[i]` megabytes on day `i + 1`, and the amounts are never negative. Write `first_days(daily, targets)`. For each target in `targets` (each more than 0), find the first day on which the running total reaches **at least** that target, as a day number counting from 1, or `-1` if it is never reached. Return the answers as a list, in the order of `targets`.

It must handle 100,000 days and 100,000 targets, so it must be about O((n + q) log n), where q is the number of targets. Build the running totals once; because no amount is negative, they never go down, so each target can be found with a binary search.

For example, `first_days([5, 0, 3, 10], [5, 6, 19])` is `[1, 3, -1]`.
--- starter
def first_days(daily, targets):
    answers = []
    for t in targets:
        answers.append(-1)
    return answers
--- solution
def first_days(daily, targets):
    totals = []
    running = 0
    for amount in daily:
        running += amount
        totals.append(running)
    answers = []
    for t in targets:
        lo, hi = 0, len(totals)
        while lo < hi:
            mid = (lo + hi) // 2
            if totals[mid] < t:
                lo = mid + 1
            else:
                hi = mid
        if lo < len(totals):
            answers.append(lo + 1)
        else:
            answers.append(-1)
    return answers
--- check case | A few targets
first_days([5, 0, 3, 10], [5, 6, 8, 9, 18, 19, 1])
=> [1, 3, 3, 4, 4, -1, 1]
--- check case | No days at all
first_days([], [3])
=> [-1]
--- check case | Days with nothing sent
first_days([0, 0, 7], [7, 1])
=> [3, 3]
--- check test | 100,000 days and 100,000 targets
(lambda r: r[:5] == [2, 3, 3, 4, 5] and r[-3:] == [99998, 99999, 100000] and sum(r) == 5000064287)(first_days([(i * 31) % 7 for i in range(100000)], list(range(1, 300001, 3))))

+++ question | A generator used twice
--- ask
What does this print?

```python
nums = (n * n for n in [1, 2, 3])
print(sum(nums), list(nums))
```
--- answer
14 []
--- why
The round brackets make a generator, a one-shot iterator. `sum` walks it to the end and adds 1 + 4 + 9 = 14. Nothing is left for `list`, which gets an empty list. With square brackets it would be a list, and both would see all three squares.

+++ question | partial inside reduce
--- ask
What does this print?

```python
from functools import partial, reduce

def power(base, exp):
    return base ** exp

f = partial(power, 2)
print(reduce(lambda acc, x: acc + f(x), [1, 2, 3], 0))
```
--- answer
14
--- why
A positional value fills parameters from the left, so `partial(power, 2)` fixes `base` at 2 and `f(x)` is 2 to the power of `x`. `reduce` starts at 0 and adds `f(1)`, `f(2)` and `f(3)`: 0 + 2 + 4 + 8 = 14.

+++ question | The name of a decorated function
--- ask
What does this print?

```python
def loud(func):
    def wrapper(*args, **kwargs):
        return func(*args, **kwargs).upper()
    return wrapper

@loud
def greet(name):
    return "hi " + name

print(greet.__name__)
```
--- answer
wrapper
--- why
`@loud` means `greet = loud(greet)`, so the name `greet` now points at the inner function, whose name is `wrapper`. Putting `@functools.wraps(func)` on the wrapper copies the original's name and docstring onto it, and then it would print `greet`.

+++ question | An exit that returns True
--- ask
What does this program print?

```python
class Quiet:
    def __enter__(self):
        return self
    def __exit__(self, exc_type, exc, tb):
        return True

with Quiet():
    print("a")
    1 / 0
    print("b")
print("c")
```
--- choice
`a`, `b` and `c`, because the error is ignored and the block carries on.
--- choice correct
`a`, then `c`: the block stops at the error, and `__exit__` swallows it.
--- choice
`a`, then a `ZeroDivisionError` traceback, because `__exit__` cannot stop an error.
--- choice
`a` and `c`, then a `ZeroDivisionError` traceback at the end.
--- why
An exception always stops the block at the line that raised it, so `b` is never printed. Then `__exit__` runs, and returning `True` swallows the exception, so the program carries on after the `with` and prints `c`. Returning `False` or nothing would have let the `ZeroDivisionError` carry on to the caller.

+++ question | Why default_factory
--- ask
A dataclass refuses `tags: list[str] = []` and asks for `field(default_factory=list)` instead. Why?
--- choice
Type annotations for lists only work with a factory.
--- choice correct
A default is made once, when the class is defined, so every object would share the same list; the factory calls `list()` again for each new object.
--- choice
An empty list counts as false, so the dataclass would think no default was given.
--- choice
`default_factory` makes the list faster to append to.
--- why
It is the mutable default argument bug again: `[]` would be evaluated once and that one list handed to every object, so appending a tag to one task would show up on all of them. `default_factory=list` passes the function `list`, which the dataclass calls once per new object.

+++ question | Three times a vector
--- ask
`Vector` defines `__mul__` and `__rmul__`. What happens, in order, when Python works out `3 * v`?
--- choice
Python calls `v.__mul__(3)`, because multiplication gives the same answer either way round.
--- choice correct
Python first tries `int.__mul__(3, v)`, which returns `NotImplemented`, and then calls `v.__rmul__(3)`.
--- choice
Python calls `v.__rmul__(3)` straight away, because the object on the right always goes first.
--- choice
It raises `TypeError`: a number on the left can never be multiplied by an object.
--- why
The left operand always gets the first try. An `int` does not know about `Vector`, so it returns `NotImplemented`, which means "not me", not an error. Only then does Python ask the right operand's reflected method, `__rmul__`. If that also returned `NotImplemented`, the result would be a `TypeError`.

+++ question | Equal but unhashable
--- ask
A class defines `__eq__`, comparing two fields, but no `__hash__`. What happens with `{Pair(1, 2), Pair(1, 2)}`?
--- choice
The set keeps both, because they are different objects.
--- choice
The set keeps one, because they are equal.
--- choice correct
It raises `TypeError: unhashable type`, because defining `__eq__` sets `__hash__` to `None`.
--- choice
It works, using each object's `id` as its hash.
--- why
Sets and dicts need equal objects to have equal hashes. The default hash is based on identity, which would give two equal pairs different hashes, so Python switches hashing off when you define `__eq__`. Define `__hash__` from the same data `__eq__` compares, such as `hash((self.a, self.b))`, and only for data that never changes.

+++ question | What a shallow copy shares
--- ask
What does this print?

```python
a = [[1], [2]]
b = a.copy()
b[0].append(9)
b.append([3])
print(a)
```
--- answer
[[1, 9], [2]]
--- why
`a.copy()` is a shallow copy: a new outer list holding the same inner lists. So `b[0].append(9)` changes the inner list that `a[0]` also points at. But `b.append([3])` changes only `b`'s own outer list, so `a` never gets the `[3]`. `copy.deepcopy(a)` would have copied the inner lists too.

+++ question | The cost of a hidden loop
--- ask
What is the running time of this function, for a list of `n` items?

```python
def dedupe(items):
    result = []
    for x in items:
        if x not in result:
            result.append(x)
    return result
```
--- choice
O(n): there is only one loop.
--- choice
O(n log n), like sorting.
--- choice correct
O(n²): `x not in result` searches the list, so there is a loop inside the loop.
--- choice
O(1) per item, because `append` is fast.
--- why
`in` on a list checks the items one by one, and `result` can grow to `n` items, so each of the `n` passes can take up to `n` steps. Keeping a `set` of the items seen beside the list makes each check O(1) on average, and the whole function O(n).

+++ question | How often the cached body runs
--- ask
`fib` is decorated with `@lru_cache(maxsize=None)`, starts with an empty cache, and is defined as `return n if n < 2 else fib(n - 1) + fib(n - 2)`. How many times does its body run for the call `fib(30)`?
--- answer
31
thirty-one
thirty one
--- why
With the cache, each `fib(k)` for `k` from 0 to 30 runs its body exactly once: the first time it is asked for. Every later request for the same `k` is a cache hit. That is 31 runs, against more than 2.6 million calls without the cache.

+++ question | When to mark a square as seen
--- ask
In breadth-first search on a grid, why is a square added to `seen` at the moment it is added to the queue, rather than when it is taken out?
--- choice
Because otherwise BFS would return a longer path than the shortest one.
--- choice correct
Because otherwise the same square can be queued many times, once by each neighbor that finds it before it is taken out, which wastes time and memory.
--- choice
Because a `set` cannot be changed while the queue is being read.
--- choice
Because squares taken out of a `deque` lose their position.
--- why
Marking when taking out still finds shortest distances, but until a square is taken out, every neighbor that looks at it adds another copy to the queue. On a big open grid that multiplies the work. Marking when queuing guarantees each square enters the queue once, which is what makes BFS O(rows × cols).

+++ question | Rounding the middle
--- ask
To find the largest `r` with `r * r <= n`, a binary search sets `lo = mid` when `mid` passes and `hi = mid - 1` when it fails. What goes wrong if it uses `mid = (lo + hi) // 2` instead of `(lo + hi + 1) // 2`?
--- choice
Nothing: rounding only changes which middle is looked at, never the answer.
--- choice
It returns one too many.
--- choice correct
When `hi` is `lo + 1` and `mid` passes, `mid` equals `lo`, so `lo = mid` changes nothing and the loop never ends.
--- choice
It raises an `IndexError` on the last step.
--- why
With `lo = 4` and `hi = 5`, rounding down gives `mid = 4`. If 4 passes, `lo` is set to 4 again and the range never shrinks. Rounding up gives `mid = 5`, and then either branch makes the range smaller. The rule: when a passing `mid` is kept with `lo = mid`, round the middle up.
